#2916. Josh Hart knows how sluggish Knicks can speed up their slow start

INDIANAPOLIS — Amid a struggling start for the Knicks wings, Josh Hart revealed he's dealt with injuries since pre-training camp and agreed Friday that the team's slow pace has negatively impacted himself, Mikal Bridges and OG Anunoby.

All three entered Friday's game against the Pacers shooting under 40 percent, while the Knicks were dead last in the league in pace at 95 possessions per game.

"There's less chances at the rim," Hart said. "I think it also affects Mikal and OG. … [Coach Mike Brown] says Mikal is the best runner in the league. So if you have the best runner in the league and you're not running, you're wasting one of his talents. If we play faster, we'll be able to do that. Sometimes OG has smaller guards defending him, so if we run he can be able to seal at the rim, be able to get easy points at the rim, which also can help our rim finishing."

Hart said the solution is to simply push the ball up quicker in possessions, even if that means Jalen Brunson isn't bringing up the ball. The Knicks were third in the NBA in frequency of field goals in the final four seconds of the shot clock, a stat indicative of a slowly evolving offense. They were also shooting an NBA-worst 57.2 percent in the paint, which is a symptom of few breakaways.

The Knicks were 23rd in the league in fastbreak points.

"We gotta get the ball out and sometimes that might have to be [Brunson] just running and not going to the ball after a make or a defensive rebound," Hart said. "Just trying to get a better and quicker flow so it's not so stagnant in the halfcourt."

Knicks guard Jalen Brunson #11 runs down court after scoring against the Bulls. Charles Wenzelberg / New York Post

The Knicks made up for some of their poor offense with offensive rebounding and second-chance points. Karl-Anthony Towns overall had a monster opening 10 games for New York.

Cavaliers guard Donovan Mitchell #45 drives to the basket as New York Knicks guard Josh Hart #3 gives chase. Charles Wenzelberg / New York Post

But Hart, Anunoby and especially Bridges weren't producing to their standard. Hart has been hindered by physical ailments. He planted his foot wrong in training camp and suffered an ankle injury, which caused him to miss consecutive games last week.

On Friday, Hart said he was sidelined for three weeks in September because "I tweaked my calf." It set back his conditioning and led to limitations in training camp, which Hart explained as a contributor to the clunky start.

"This year was a little different. Obviously, a shorter break in terms of the summer [because of the long playoff run] but also I wasn't physically able to really push my body towards the end of the summer to be prepared for the season," Hart said. "I had [the calf] injury in September, which kept me out for about three weeks, so I wasn't able to get in some of that conditioning and some of those legs under me, which is normally the time to do it.

"And then, the ankle injury in training camp. So, the ramp up was a little bit slower than I kinda wanted it to be or kinda liked it to be, so I think that's the biggest thing is just getting my legs under me."

Hart entered Friday shooting just 31.7 percent on 3-pointers — which is well below his average of 41.3 percent from last season.

"The shots I'm shooting, I like them. I think that was kinda evident with Cleveland. One of the things: All three of my 3s was just hella short. It was just, those legs, get those legs under me," Hart said. "Also, on top of that, missing a week in the season, it's cumulative of six, seven weeks of just not much activity basketball-wise. So, I think that's just what it is. So, as the season progresses and I'm out there running more, doing all those kinds of things, the performance will follow."

#2915. Chiefs-Falcons Week 10 game and score predictions from Arrowhead Pride

It's time for the Arrowhead Pride staff to put their opinions to the test. Before the Kansas City Chiefs travel to face the Atlanta Falcons, our writers predicted the final score and result.

Atlanta has been an unpredictable team both looking dominant in their trio of wins while struggling in most of their six losses. FanDuel Sportsbook currently lists the Chiefs as 3.5-point home favorites.

The Falcons also have the misfortune of not coming off of a European game into their bye week. The Falcons lost 34-17 to the Cincinnati Bengals in Madrid, Spain on Sunday. They also sent starting safety Jessie Bates to the Jacksonville Jaguars ahead of Tuesday's trade deadline.

We also want to know your score prediction! Vote in the poll on The Feed and share your score prediction!

I do worry about the Falcons' pass rush, even if their defense's other two levels were underwhelming even before trading Bates. With the league's two best running backs facing off, I expect a run heavy day that should lead to a fast moving game.

Look for the Chiefs' pass rush to build on their second half against Atlanta quarterback Michael Penix. This will be a game where Atlanta looks game for a couple of drives before the Chiefs overwhelm in route to a victory. The run game will lead to limited possessions for both sides to keep the scoring from getting out of hand.

The Chiefs and Falcons are on opposite ends of the competitive spectrum in the NFL, and this week's game should show that. The Falcons traded away veteran safety Jessie Bates to the contending Jacksonville Jaguars, while the Chiefs traded for wide receiver Josh Downs from the Indianapolis Colts.

Despite a talented core of offensive playmakers, including Bijan Robinson and Drake London, the Falcons are only 3-6, largely because their defense allows over 340 yards per game.

The Falcons are -8 in the turnover-takeaway category, 30th in the NFL, while the Chiefs are tied for 9th at +4. If they can force turnovers, they will be in good shape to win the game.

I don't think Downs will play more than 20 snaps this week, but I would expect the Chiefs to design a few routes for him to get his feet wet in the offense.

I've got the Chiefs winning this one. Josh Downs makes his debut, and I like Mahomes having another guy who can get open underneath, especially now that Atlanta traded away Jessie Bates. The Falcons also traded their starting center, Ryan Neuzil, so I expect Chris Jones to make life real uncomfortable up the middle.

What impressed me most about the Chiefs' 35–12 win over the Jets was how they finished. Kansas City has a habit of letting teams it should beat hang around, and the Jets were within two points at halftime. The Chiefs outscored them 21–0 from there. Kenneth Walker III looked like the player we saw during the 5–0 start, finishing with 143 yards from scrimmage and three touchdowns. To keep Walker rolling, the Chiefs need their receivers to win against man coverage. Seattle and Denver showed what happens when defenses can load the box and trust their corners to hold up one-on-one: the run slows down, and play-action loses its bite. If the receivers make Atlanta pay for that approach, Walker will have more room to run and Patrick Mahomes can get back to attacking off play-action.

Against Atlanta, the key is slowing down Bijan Robinson. That's the operative phrase: slow him down. He's coming off 100 rushing yards and another 47 through the air, so Kansas City has to account for him even when he doesn't get the handoff. Drake London demands attention, and Kyle Pitts can still cause problems for the Chiefs' linebackers and safeties even in a quiet season. But if Kansas City's run defense keeps Robinson from taking over, Sack Nation can build on its five-sack performance by moving Michael Penix Jr. off his spot and forcing him past his first read. I like the Chiefs' chances when Atlanta has to win that way.

Your Kansas City Chiefs head to Atlanta as big favorites against the 2-6 Falcons. Yes, they've lost 4 of their last 5, but this is a dangerous opponent. They've got the league's best RB and a streaky young QB– it's an offense that can get out to an early lead and make things uncomfortable. But, as always, this game will come down to how the Chiefs execute. From the pass protection to the receivers and the short-yardage offense, this team has questions, but they're more than capable. Ideally, they start strong with Walker and Johnson– if the run game gets going, the Chiefs are hard to beat.

The Chiefs enter this game after righting some of their wrongs last week against the Jets, although still with major question marks for a team intent on making a Super Bowl run. The Falcons, meanwhile, are on a two game losing skid after their upset win over the previously unbeaten San Francisco 49ers. I never quite know what to make of the Falcons. They're a team with some of the best offensive weapons in the entire NFL, yet they can't ever seem to put it all together.

Since Jonathan Taylor in week 2, Bijan Robinson will be the best running back the Chiefs have faced all season. The Chiefs have been great defending the run in 2026, and I think they will be able to contain Robinson, but he will still get his yards. Defensively, the Chiefs should also be able to continue the positive momentum they established last week in pressuring the quarterback and leading to mistakes by the Falcons' offense.

I'm excited to see what the Chiefs' offense has in store for new wide receiver Josh Downs. I'm keeping my expectations in check, however, as it always seems like the Chiefs onboard their wide receivers slowly. I expect Downs to play 35-50% of the team's snaps, but they will still be able to generate enough offense to take home the win.

The Atlanta Falcons only show up for primetime games; they are garbage during noon kickoffs. Despite having multiple weapons on offense, namely running back Bijan Robinson and wide receiver Drake London, they can never seem to put it all together in the daytime.

On the other hand, the Chiefs are coming off one of their best performances of the season, when the pass rush finally woke up and bullied a terrible team in the New York Jets. At the end of the day, I would expect the Chiefs offense to continue rolling while they ramp up new acquisition wide receiver Josh Downs.

Atlanta has been a surprisingly competitive team since quarterback Michael Penix Jr. came back. The Falcons have one of the dynamic duos of weapons in running back Bijan Robinson and Drake London. That alongside a decent offensive line has given the Falcons a shot to win any game. Their defense has been surprisingly competent, but after trading Jessie Bates, that will probably fall off.

I expect Atlanta to have some nice drives against the Chiefs, but the game will play out similarly to the Jets game. At some point, the Chiefs pressure will get to a pocket-bound Penix and Atlanta won't be able to catch up offensively. Chiefs pull away in the second half.

If the Falcons hadn't traded away two starters this week, this would feel much more like a spot where the Chiefs could slip up. Still, noon kickoffs have seemed to come with slow starts for Kansas City this year, and this Falcons team still has a reason to fight to stay tied for first place in the NFC South.

However, Atlanta has had more letdowns than triumphs this year, including a couple of weeks ago when the Tampa Bay Buccaneers ran for 342 yards as a team and didn't need a pass to win.

This game should be a comfortable win for Kansas City because of its offense, leaning on the ground game once again to stay in rhtyhm and open up the middle of the field for passes.

All nine panelists predicted the Chiefs to win by more than ten points. Ron (38-20) and Matt (28-13) had the most accurate Week 9 predictions with only 16 total points of error.

Our readers were less optimistic about how Kansas City would perform. Only 50.6% of our readers anticipated a comfortable Chiefs victory, while 37.2% expected Mahomes and Company to narrowly come out on top. 12.2% of respondents expected the Jets to win.

*To calculate a prediction's points of error, the differences between the prediction and the actual score in point spread, home team score, and away team score are added together. For example, a prediction calls for a 17-10 Chiefs win. They end up winning 16-10, so there were two points of error: the point spread was off by one point, the Kansas City score missed by one point, and the opponent's score was predicted correctly. But if the Chiefs lose the game 17-10, there were 28 points of error in the prediction: the point spread was off by 14 (the difference between +7 and -7), and both scores were off by 7.

#2914. Federal Reserve Board announces pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, and changes to streamline and adjust their check services

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 1:30 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, such as the clearing of checks, automated clearing house (ACH) transactions, instant payments, and wholesale payment and settlement services.

By law, the Federal Reserve must establish fees to recover the costs, including imputed costs, of providing payment services over the long run. The Federal Reserve expects to recover 101.5 percent of actual and imputed expenses in 2027, including the return on equity that would have been earned if a private-sector firm provided the services. Overall, price changes for 2027 will result in an estimated 1.4 percent average price increase for established, mature services.

In addition, the Federal Reserve Banks announced changes to streamline and adjust their check services. As described in the request for information on check services issued last year, the volume of checks processed by the Federal Reserve Banks is declining, but checks remain an important payment instrument for many consumers and businesses. The changes were informed by public feedback and ensure that the Federal Reserve Banks continue to provide check services efficiently. The adjustments include optimizing check processing windows during low-volume periods, increasing fees that banks and credit unions pay to process checks, and other operational updates. These changes do not impact consumers' and businesses' ability to pay with or receive checks and only impact how banks and credit unions process checks with Federal Reserve Banks.

The entire 2027 fee schedule for all of the Federal Reserve's payment services is published on FRBservices.org®. Additional information on the Federal Reserve's payments services, including service usage statistics, is available on the Board's website.

#2910. Agencies extend comment period for proposed third-party risk management guidance

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guide for Traditional Community Banking Organizations (PDF)

#2908. LaMelo Ball sure looks like the difference: Timberwolves and Hornets trending in opposite directions after trade

We will stipulate that, when it comes to success and struggle in the NBA, the answer is never just one thing. There's plenty that goes into teams' fortunes rising and falling, and all sorts of stuff can factor into the difference between living in the penthouse and taking up residence in the outhouse.

With that caveat in place: While the Minnesota Timberwolves were doing this in Minneapolis on Thursday, en route to dismantling the Dallas Mavericks, 133-94 …

… and the Charlotte Hornets were doing this in Phoenix on the same night, en route to a desultory 101-96 defeat …

… it was hard not to note the Just One Thing that seems to be contributing to the difference between Minnesota and Charlotte. Or, rather, the Guy Wearing No. 1.

After walloping a pretty good Mavs team, the Wolves are 9-3, in third place in the Western Conference, with the NBA's best net rating. They've won five in a row, and six out of eight since losing superstar No. 1 option Anthony Edwards to knee surgery. They've outscored their opponents by a total of 161 points this season; according to Justin Kubatko of Statitudes, that's the largest point differential through 12 games in Wolves franchise history — by 50 points.

After falling in Phoenix, mustering just 96 points against the league's fourth-worst defense, the Hornets are now 4-8, tied for 10th in the East, with the NBA's No. 26 net rating. They've lost seven of eight, have logged more turnovers than assists four times in 12 games, and have already failed to crack 100 points six times — as many times as Charlotte finished south of the century mark all of last season.

Again: It's not just that LaMelo Ball used to play for the Hornets, and now he plays for the Wolves. The operating theory for how Charlotte would generate offense in the absence of its one-time linchpin rested largely on continued growth and elevated production from Brandon Miller, Kon Knueppel and the re-signed Coby White, with a dash of second-unit contribution from veteran Dennis Schröder. The Hornets have gotten a grand total of 97 minutes thus far from White, who's on a minutes restriction as he works his way back from a left calf strain, and Knueppel, who'll miss the next several weeks with a balky left hamstring. Schröder's also on the shelf with a hamstring strain; Miller, the team's leading scorer, has missed the last two games with a groin injury.

Any team would struggle to score without the bulk of its perimeter firepower, ball-handling and shot creation. It has to be painful, though, to be mired in such a horrific offensive morass mere months after consciously choosing to bid farewell to Ball, the player whom both the eye test and the advanced numbers suggested served as the rising tide that lifted Charlotte's offense last season.

And it must be particularly galling to be suffering through the doldrums while watching LaMelo have exactly the same kind of effusive, elevating effect in the Twin Cities.

Ball had nine of the Wolves' 33 assists in the blowout win over the Mavericks on Thursday — Minnesota's fourth 30-assist game of the season, more than anybody but the Hawks, Warriors, Nuggets and Spurs. Those helpers led to 19 Minnesota points, in addition to the 22 that Ball scored himself; for the season, he's directly creating 56.9 points of offense per 100 possessions for the Wolves, according to Databallr, which is 20th in the NBA — and nearly five points-per-100 more than White, who leads this season's Hornets.

The Wolves generate a higher share of their shots at the rim when LaMelo's on the floor than when he's off it. Their 3-point shooting goes through the roof in his minutes, and nearly every member of the team sees a bump in shot quality when playing alongside him.

The Wolves are more efficient in the half-court when Ball's at the controls, and they get out in transition way more often with him out there. Minnesota's team-wide effective field-goal percentage spikes by 6.5% with LaMelo on the floor — the largest on/off split on the team, and one of the largest among all NBA players to log at least 200 minutes this season, according to Cleaning the Glass.

The numbers tell a compelling story. So, too, does the joy that seems to leap off the screen from so many of the Wolves as they run with LaMelo — orbiting, emulating and ultimately benefiting from the irrepressible style and flow with which he plays.

"I think when you have a guy like Melo, he makes it fun for everybody," Timberwolves head coach Chris Finch said after Thursday's win, according to Sarah Todd of the Minnesota Star Tribune. "And the guys can trust in the next pass, next play."

That trust is tougher to come by these days in Charlotte, where the Hornets enter Friday's action ranked dead last in the NBA in points scored per possession, field-goal percentage, free-throw attempts, assists and turnovers, struggling to consistently string together quality possessions, even against lesser defensive outfits.

"How do we stop the bleeding?" White told reporters after a recent loss to the Detroit Pistons. "There's gonna be turnovers. There's gonna be miscommunications on both sides of the ball. How do we not let it continue to derail us from what we're trying to do?"

The loss of a superstar of Edwards' magnitude would've derailed plenty of teams. The Wolves, though, have continued to thrive, buoyed in part by the NBA's No. 1 defense — a unit on which Ball has somewhat surprisingly emerged as a physical and positive contributor at the point of attack. All told, Minnesota has outscored opponents by a whopping 15.5 points per 100 possessions when Ball has played without Edwards — a loftier net rating than even their regular-season leading mark, and more than good enough to stay afloat in the brutally competitive West while Ant continues to rehab his ailing knee.

There are plenty of other factors at play. The Hornets have underperformed their expected shot quality by more than any other team in the NBA thus far, according to Synergy Sports Technology's tracking; the combination of some regression to the mean and the hopefully healthy return of some key pieces should help bolster their offense. And there's plenty of credit to go around in Minnesota, from Rudy Gobert's perpetually stalwart work on the back line to Finch getting career-best play from the likes of Jonathan Kuminga, Ayo Dosunmu and Bones Hyland to a collective closing kick that has the Wolves ranked second in the NBA in fourth-quarter net rating.

"We've definitely [elevated] our play in the fourth quarters on both sides of the ball," Finch said. "Getting key stops, rebounding really well, executing, finding really good shots. And LaMelo's been at the heart of a lot of that on offense."

LaMelo's presence has been at the heart of a lot of what's gone well for the Wolves, and his absence, it seems, has been at the heart of a lot of what's haunting the Hornets. It's never just one thing. Sometimes, though, one thing can make one hell of a big difference.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2905. Panthers Injury Report & Transactions – Week 10

The Carolina Panthers front office is busy throughout the season with transactions, roster moves, and injury updates. Here's what's happening at Mint Street this week.

For once, the Panthers got some good news on the injury front heading into their Week 10 showdown against the New Orleans Saints.

Offensive lineman Brady Christensen nearly returned from injury last week, but was held out to ensure he was fully ready to go. Hopefully this is the week the versatile player can help plug one of the many, many holes on the Panthers offensive line. He's currently listed as questionable for Sunday.

Speaking of the Panthers offensive line, guard Damien Lewis was a full participant in practice on both Wednesday and Friday, though he was limited on Thursday. The incumbent starter has missed the Panthers last two games. He looks good to go Sunday.

The Panthers have three additional questionable players this week in outside linebacker Jaelan Phillips, cornerback Will Lee, and wide receiver Xavier Legette. Carolina could definitely use all three of them, at least situationally.

Both cornerback Mike Jackson and running back Jonathon Brooks are doubtful, while linebacker Bobby Okereke is out.

Signed LB Claudin Cherelus to the practice squad

The Panthers have brought back linebacker Claudin Cherelus to the practice squad after waiving him back in September with an injury designation. This is Cherelus's fourth season in Carolina after beginning his NFL career with the Panthers as an undrafted free agent back in 2023. He has appeared in 35 games for the Panthers with six starts, registering 61 tackles.

Released WR David Moore then signed to the practice squad

Since joining the Carolina Panthers in 2024, wide receiver David Moore has been a fixture on the roster more for his veteran stability than his actual on-field productivity. This past week the Panthers waived him then re-signed him to the practice squad, just in case the wide receiver room suffers any more injuries. This year Moore has appeared in six games with one reception 27 yards. He has also returned four punts and eight kickoffs this year.

Signed DT Cam Horsley to the practice squad

Cam Horsley joined the Tennessee Titans last year as an undrafted rookie free agent out of Boston College. To his credit, he made the 53-man roster and appeared in one game, playing 14 defensive snaps without recording any stats. Horsley is a diamond in the rough the Panthers will look to polish to provide some additional depth along the defensive interior.

Placed WR Ihmir Smith-Marsette on practice squad-injured

Ihmir Smith-Marsette is a bit of a blast from the past for Panthers fans. He joined the Panthers in 2023, which was his third NFL season at the time. He saw the field, too, appearing in all 17 games that year as Carolina's primary punt returner who also had 11 kick returns. Back in 2023 he also contributed eight receptions for 51 yards and eight carries for 74 more yards. He left Carolina for the New York Jets in 2024, then didn't appear in any games in 2025. The Panthers added him to the practice squad back in September 2026, and now he has an injury designation.

#2902. Bayern Munich News: The aftermath of Germany’s 3-1 win over Serbia; Real Madrid not giving up on Michael Olise; and MORE!

Germany traveled to Serbia for a UEFA Nations League match played under harsh conditions with very few spectators, but with a clear purpose.

The Germans wanted a win and to begin the process of establishing an identity for the Jürgen Klopp era. Let's get into it; here are some quick hitters on the match:

As always, let's begin with a look at the starting XI:

There were not many surprises, but there were a few. First, Nico Schlotterbeck took an unexpected seat in favor of Jonathan Tah and Malick Thiaw at center back. That was a pretty big surprise considering how many viewed the center-back hierarchy. In addition, Maximilian Mittelstädt got the nod over Nathaniel Brown, which was not altogether shocking considering the recent form of both players. Brown has not been bad by any means, but the VfB Stuttgart star has been extremely good.

The conditions were nasty. There was hard rain and a muddy pitch, which had to have club sporting directors and coaches holding their collective breath.

Just three minutes into the game, Germany took the lead on an out-swinging corner that connected with Kai Havertz, who did not exactly get a great piece of the ball. However, he did get enough of it for the ball to bounce into the net off of Srđan Babić for an own goal, giving Germany a 1–0 advantage.

In the 14th minute, Aleksandar Pavlović slid a perfect pass to Havertz, who was making a straight run. The striker's shot was saved by Serbia's Vanja Milinković-Savić, but Florian Wirtz was waiting to pounce on the rebound to make it 2–0… and the rout was on. If Milinković-Savić does not spend his free time rating craft beers and eating artisanal sandwiches, I would be shocked.

This is sort of the problem with Havertz. He made a great run and used his speed to separate himself and put himself in a good position to score, but he could not finish. It is a little confusing as to why so many Germany coaches continue to tout him as a striker. He is a good footballer, no doubt, but his prowess as a striker leaves a lot to be desired.

Serbia was working its collective tail off, but was simply outmatched.

Similarly, Germany played with great energy and urgency. Klopp expects a certain work rate… and he got it.

By halftime, Germany was fully dominating the match.

In the second half, RB Leipzig star Brajan Gruda showed everyone why there has been so much buzz surrounding him as he drove roughly 65 yards with the ball before firing a shot from just outside the box to make it 3–0. What a play for the youngster, who is likely making himself a lot of money this season.

Serbia would answer back with a goal from Srđan Babić. Tah, who had a rough few minutes, created the chance with a foul just outside the box to give Serbia a set piece in a dangerous area. An in-swinging delivery found the head of Stefan Gudelj, whose attempt was good, but was ultimately turned away by Marc-André ter Stegen. Unfortunately for Germany, Srđan Babić, who appeared to be Tah's mark, put the rebound home to make it 3–1.

It looked like Kimmich had scored in the 65th minute, but Wirtz was ruled offside, even though he was not involved in the play.

Derry Scherhant hit the post in the second half as well. Germany was just too strong for Serbia.

A red card to Vanja Dragojević in the 76th minute iced the game for Germany as Serbia was already struggling to keep up. For good measure, Andrija Živković was also handed a red card in the 89th minute.

Overall, the Germans were very good. The team played in a style that was frantic yet structured. This particular group had the energy to play Klopp's style, and the head coach seemed pleased.

If you missed our Initial Analysis, Recap, Match Awards, Observations, or Postgame Podcast, give them a look or a listen:

Real Madrid remain interested in Bayern Munich winger Michael Olise, after previously being linked with him during the summer, according to L'Equipe. The 24-year-old is reported to have multiple top clubs in Europe 'dreaming' of signing him next summer, when he will enter the final two years of his contract at the Allianz Arena. Olise scored in France's 3-1 Nations League defeat against Italy on Thursday.

Crystal Palace values former Bayern Munich defender Chris Richards too much to let him leave in January:

Crystal Palace have no intention of selling centre-back Chris Richards in January despite interest from Inter Milan, sources have told Football Insider.

#2900. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 stocks for their solid growth potential

Investors are closely monitoring earnings releases of artificial intelligence companies amid concerns about the durability of high spending and demand. While quarterly results provide key insights into a company's performance and execution, it is prudent to focus on long-term prospects and look beyond short-term fluctuations.

Top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick stocks with attractive long-term prospects as their assessments are backed by in-depth research of a company's fundamentals and growth opportunities.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

AI-powered data analytics provider Palantir Technologies is this week's first pick. The company reported market-beating second-quarter results. Moreover, Palantir raised its full-year guidance and now expects U.S. commercial business revenue to grow by at least 134%.

Following the Q2 print, Bank of America analyst Mariana Perez Mora reiterated a buy rating on PLTR stock with a price target of $255. Mora said that Palantir's performance reflects the success of its AI strategy, which focuses on delivering the right infrastructure to customers and ensuring better outcomes.

"The main driver continues to be the U.S. commercial business, which now represents almost 40% of total (vs. 30% a year ago and just 20% a couple of years back)," noted Mora.

The five-star analyst highlighted the 149% growth in Palantir's U.S. commercial business in Q2 2026 and the expansion in its customer base and engagement levels, with clients pursuing more integrated relationships. Mora noted that the U.S. commercial customer count increased 35% year-over-year to 653, with trailing twelve months revenue per customer surging 76% to $3.5 million in the second quarter.

Citing strength across Palantir's commercial and government segments, Mora increased her sales and earnings per share estimates for 2026-2028. The analyst attributed the increase in her estimates to the growth in the commercial business, backed by stronger total contract value in the second quarter.

Mora ranks No. 444 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 32.6%. See Palantir Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

E-commerce and cloud computing giant Amazon impressed investors with its strong second-quarter results. Revenue from the company's Amazon Web Services (AWS) cloud business surged 37%, marking the unit's fastest growth since 2021.

In reaction to the Q2 2026 performance, JPMorgan analyst Doug Anmuth reiterated a buy rating on Amazon stock and raised his price target to $365 from $330, saying that it remains the firm's Best Idea. Anmuth highlighted that the company's forex-neutral top-line growth accelerated to 20% in Q2 2026, the fastest in 20 quarters. He added that the top line gained from acceleration across both AWS and stores.

The five-star analyst noted that AWS growth of 37% was the fastest in 18 quarters, with backlog jumping nearly 2.5x year-over-year and 36% sequentially to $496 billion. Amazon's backlog gained from robust demand across both core workloads and AI adoption. Anmuth also observed the strength in AWS' AI and chips businesses, Graviton revenue commitments, and AWS' core business.

Notably, Anmuth believes that the core AWS business is highly correlated to AI revenue, and he expects further strengthening of this relationship as more AI workloads move into full-scale production and fuel higher demand. Overall, the analyst is more optimistic on Amazon following Q2 results and raised his 2026 and 2027 sales estimates by about 1% to 2% and operating income by about 5% to 7%.

"Importantly, management emphasized that the [return on invested capital] on its AI investments is compelling and it has a clear line-of-sight to strong financial returns," said Anmuth.

Anmuth ranks No. 717 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.5%. See Amazon Statistics on TipRanks.

Moving on to Lam Research, a supplier of semiconductor manufacturing equipment and services. Thanks to AI-driven demand, the company delivered better-than-anticipated fiscal fourth quarter results.

In reaction to the solid results, Oppenheimer analyst Edward Yang reaffirmed a buy rating on LRCX stock with a price target of $400. Yang stated that Lam Research exceeded June-quarter expectations despite the high bar, driven by strong performance of the Customer Support Business Group and doubling of NAND revenue from the prior quarter. He also cited the improvement in Q4 FY26 gross margin.

The five-star analyst highlighted that Lam Research's September-quarter revenue and EPS outlook exceeded the Street's expectations by mid-teens percentages. Yang added that the company increased its wafer fabrication equipment spending outlook to the low-$150 billion range, up from its prior $140 billion estimate.

Importantly, Yang noted that Lam Research called calendar year 2027 "extraordinary," citing persistent supply shortages and the addition of 8 to 10 new fabrication plants. The analyst raised his 2027 revenue and EPS estimates by 7% and 9% to $33 billion and $8.88, respectively. Yang also increased his 2028 revenue and EPS estimates by 7% and 8% to $36.6 billion and $9.89, respectively.

"It's the cleanest way to play AI-driven 3D scaling across memory, foundry/logic, and packaging," said Yang about LRCX.

Yang ranks No. 256 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 71% of the time, delivering an average return of 56.4%. See Lam Research Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2888. Packers Injury Report: Green Bay rules out 4 vs. Vikings

The Green Bay Packers have officially ruled out four players on Friday: running back Josh Jacobs (finger), edge defender Micah Parsons (knee/illness-PUP), center Sean Rhyan (ankle) and tight end Josh Whyle. Of those three players, only Jacobs played against the New England Patriots last week, and he did not finish the game. Yesterday, head coach Matt LaFleur stated that Jacobs had yet to have surgery on his fractured ring finger, but that it would be coming in the near future.

The Packers used the doubtful status, which they rarely use, on linebacker Zaire Franklin, who has been out for multiple weeks with a shin fracture. He was a non-participant throughout the week's practices.

The questionable players going into Sunday's action are: running back Chris Brooks (ankle-IR), safety Javon Bullard (ankle/knee), offensive lineman Travis Glover (knee), safety Xavier McKinney (foot), receiver Skyy Moore (ankle-IR), edge defender Lukas Van Ness (back) and guard Kevin Zeitler (ankle/triceps).

In total, 9 of the active roster's 53 players were given game statuses for this week's action against the Minnesota Vikings, leaving just 44 fully healthy players on the roster. Considering that teams need 48 players to suit up for game days, that's a bit problematic.

My guess? The questionable players are more likely than not to play on Sunday, just because the Packers are going to need bodies — and their injured reserve designations are quickly evaporating. Already, Green Bay has used four of their eight allotted return designations this season — with the practice windows now open for Brooks and Moore. And that's with Jacob Monk, Jonathan Ford and Warren Brinson having the potential to return this season — along with some potential moves, like Jacobs going on IR, coming down the line.

The Packers already were able to withstand not having Rhyan, Whyle and Franklin on the roster in recent weeks, and Jacobs will likely be replaced by MarShawn Lloyd — who missed last week's action — on the game-day roster.

As long as the questionable players are ready to go, Green Bay shouldn't need to shake things up too much. If they aren't, though, their reinforcements are thin right now, and we'll probably get a heads-up on who won't be able to play by way of the team's Saturday practice squad elevations.

Draymond Green shoves Victor Wembanyama, earns Flagrant 1 in Warriors-Spurs game originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

It was only a matter of time before Draymond Green earned his first flagrant foul of the 2026-27 NBA season, and the Warriors veteran crossed it off during Golden State's NBA Cup game against the San Antonio Spurs on Friday night at Frost Bank Center.

#2880. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

The stock market continues to grapple with volatility amid geopolitical tensions in the Middle East and concerns about the sustainability of the AI boom. Given this uncertainty, investors looking for steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick the right dividend stocks, as these experts assign their ratings after an in-depth analysis of a company's financials and ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Downstream energy company Phillips 66 (PSX) is this week's first dividend pick. With a quarterly dividend $1.27 per share (annualized dividend of $5.08), PSX offers a yield of 2.25%. The company recently reported solid Q2 earnings, as the Middle East conflict affected global supplies and drove up refining margins.

Following the Q2 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Phillips 66 and raised his price target to $255 from $240, reflecting higher 2026 earnings expectations and lower interest expense next year.

Gabelman highlighted the quarter-over-quarter reduction in Phillips 66's net debt, noting management's optimism at achieving its estimated $15.5 billion net debt target one year ahead of schedule. The 5-star analyst expects the company to end 2026 with a net debt of $14.6 billion.

"The [balance sheet] improvement could re-establish PSX as a go-to defensive refiner," said Gabelman.

Management agreed that PSX's payout ratio lagged year-to-date, but expects buybacks to increase in the second half. Gabelman said Phillps indicated the possibility of a larger dividend hike following an annual increase of 5% over the past two years.

Gabelman ranks No. 554 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Phillips 66 Statistics on TipRanks.

Crescent Energy (CRGY) is an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian and Uinta Basins. Earlier this month, the company reported better-than-expected Q2 earnings and announced a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share, payable on August 31. At an annualized dividend of $0.48 per share, CRGY boasts a dividend yield of about 4%.

In reaction to Q2 results, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reaffirmed a buy rating on Crescent Energy stock with a price target of $18. He noted continued strength in the company's performance, with second-quarter oil production and cash flow surpassing the Street's expectations.

"CRGY's cash flow exceeded expectations by 10%, reinforcing its trajectory of capital efficiency," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that Crescent raised its full-year oil production guidance, citing the effective integration of the Vital Energy acquisition. Specifically, Crescent tripled its synergy target from the Vital Energy deal to as much as $300 million, significantly reducing the effective purchase price. This reflects Crescent's solid execution following the acquisition, Richardson said.

Additionally, the analyst noted that Crescent's capital spending is and heading toward the lower end of management's prior guidance, indicating disciplined financial controls.

Richardson ranks No. 579 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 65% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.5%. See Crescent Energy Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Finally, Viper Energy (VNOM) is effectively controlled by Diamondback Energy. Viper owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests in oil-weighted basins, mainly the Permian in West Texas. Viper recently announced its Q2 2026 results and declared a 32% hike in its base dividend, effective in the third quarter of 2026. The new base dividend indicates a 4.5% annualized yield.

Viper also removed its quarterly commitment to return at least 75% of cash available for distribution. The company believes that this move will ensure increased flexibility to make opportunistic share repurchases and pursue accretive mergers and acquisitions.

Following the Q2 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper, slightly raising his price target to $59 from $58. Bilkoski said Viper's solid Q2 performance supports his view that the strength in oil prices and rising operator activity are driving higher production.

"Viper has delivered, and we forecast will continue to deliver, one of the highest production per share growth profiles in our royalty universe" through the end of 2027, said Bilkoski. He believes Viper deserves a premium valuation, given its above-average growth.

The 5-star analyst said that while the biggest surprise in the quarter was Viper's revision to its shareholder return framework, he doesn't believe that the move indicates a significant change to its underlying strategy. Under the new framework, a larger portion of excess free cash flow is expected to be allocated to share repurchases than to variable dividends, Bilkoski said.

Bilkoski ranks No. 719 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Viper Energy Financials on TipRanks.

#2869. Packers must corral Vikings return man Myles Price

The Vikings' defense is incredible. Their offense should be more capable with a new quarterback under center. But their special teams also present a very interesting challenge for the Packers.

Under the guidance of new coordinator Cam Achord, the Packers' special teams have been — with some exceptions — generally pretty solid. Pre-injury, Skyy Moore bolstered the Packers' return game, but the Packers' special teams have really shined in coverage. As of this week, the Packers' have allowed the fourth-lowest average per return on punt returns and the 10th lowest figure on special teams. (This, incidentally, aligns with the special teams stuff the Packers have historically cared about the most and done the best, but it's still good to see them doing a good job there.)

But as good as the Packers are in punt and kick coverage, the Vikings are even better at punt and kick returns. Minnesota currently ranks first in punt return average (17.5 yards per return) and second in kickoff return average (29.9 per return). It's true the Vikings have logged a very long punt return (86 yards) and a pretty long kickoff return (68 yards), but even if you removed those long returns, the Vikings' averages would only drop them to the fifth- and third-best spots in the league on punt and kickoff return, respectively.

And it's all thanks to return man Myles Price.

Undrafted out of Indiana in 2025, the 5-foot-9, 183-pound Price was a special teams only player for the Vikings last season. He averaged a respectable 9.9 yards per return on 30 punt returns and 25.9 yards per return on 60 kickoff returns, but capped out at a long of 43 yards on his punt duties and 61 yards on kickoff returns. Certainly not bad numbers, but not eye popping.

That's changed in 2026. The pocket-sized Price leads the NFL in punt return yardage, boasts the longest punt return in the league, and is the only player in the NFL to score on a punt return this season. He's also the only player who's returned either a punt or a kick for the Vikings this year, meaning their impressive league-wide rankings are entirely the work of their micro machine return man.

Everybody knows the Packers will have their hands full with Brian Flores, and Kirk Cousins may be enough to bolster their offense to at least some level of respectability. But if the Packers don't take care of business on special teams, the Vikings could end up tilting the balance in that phase of the game. And in what's expected to be a close game, any little bit could make a difference.

Fortunately, special teams have never been a game-altering problem for the Packers in the past, right? Right?

#2865. Ray Dalio says Bessent move is sign that a debt crisis is getting closer; recommends gold and bitcoin

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent's debt buyback announcement this week fits into a broader pattern that can signal a forthcoming crisis, according to billionaire investor Ray Dalio.

The Bridgewater Associates founder said Bessent's plan to increase government debt purchases may portend trouble for the U.S. economy. Coupled with the Japanese government reducing its U.S. bond market exposure and surging long-dated American bond yields, Dalio said investors may want to prepare their portfolios for increased risk by owning cryptocurrencies and gold.

"I am confident that the government's financial condition is at an inflection point," Dalio wrote in a LinkedIn post published Friday. "If this is not dealt with now, the debts will build up to levels where they can't be managed without great trauma."

Dalio noted that the Treasury Department has "only limited capacity" to buy back bonds. Bessent told CNBC on Thursday that his team was going to "make a market" and that the purchases would likely top $4 billion.

The U.S. is spending about 40% more than it's bringing in, resulting in the burgeoning budget deficit, Dalio said. The U.S. budget deficit topped $432 billion in July, but Bessent told CNBC that it's likely peaked under President Donald Trump's administration.

Bessent said in the interview that a team was looking at ways to shrink spending by hundreds of billions of dollars. But Dalio said there is "very little ability" to do so given that it is either committed or considered essential.

After years of overspending, Dalio said total debt now dwarfs what the U.S. brings in each year. If the U.S. government was a business, Dalio said debt service payments would come in at roughly $11 trillion — about 200% of annual revenue.

Dalio, 77, warned the cost of repaying principal and servicing the debt will only grow over time.

To solve this, Dalio said the U.S. needs to carefully employ a three-part strategy to get the budget deficit down to 3% of gross domestic product.

First, Dalio said the U.S. government needs to reduce its spending. Second, he said tax revenue needs to be raised. Finally, the New York native and Harvard Business School alumni said lower interest rates were needed.

"All three need to happen concurrently so as to prevent any one from being too large," Dalio said. "If any one is too large, the adjustment will be traumatic."

Dalio cautioned against ramming through these adjustments by "force." For instance, "it would be very bad if the Federal Reserve unnaturally forced interest rates down," he said.

Dalio said it's important to to take steps now, while the economy is healthy. An economy in recession requires increased government spending, he said.

Dalio said the exact timing of a debt crisis can be swayed by variables ranging from military conflict to political change. On its current trajectory, the U.S. could enter such a crisis in as early as one year or as late as five. "My guess, which I suppose will be a bad one, is that it will come in three years, give or take two, if the course we're on is not changed."

To prepare, Dalio recommended investors are underweight debt assets, such as bonds.

As much as 10% to 15% of a portfolio could land in gold, as well as "a bit" of bitcoin, the hedge fund founder said.

Dalio's post came at the tail end of a volatile week for U.S. financial markets. Rising long-term Treasury yields have pressured stocks, leading the S&P 500 to snap a a three-week-long advance.

#2863. 10-year Treasury yield moves to 5.17% as traders weigh producer price report

The 10-year Treasury yield stayed over 5% on Friday after October's producer price index, a measure of wholesale prices, came in slightly less than expected.

The yield — the key benchmark for mortgages, auto loans and credit card debt — rose 4 basis points to 5.143%. It reached a high of 5.174% earlier in the day.

The 2-year Treasury note yield, typically more sensitive to short-term Federal Reserve interest rate decisions, was lower by less than 1 basis point at 4.859%. The longer-dated 30-year Treasury bond yield, sensitive to geopolitical risks, was more than 4 basis points higher at 5.472%.

One basis point equals 0.01%, and yields and prices move in opposite directions.

October's producer price index rose 0.3% versus September, less than the 0.4% expected by economists polled by Dow Jones. But on an annual basis, PPI rose 6.5% over the past 12 months, the biggest annual increase since November 2022.

There was "no silver lining" in the inflation report, said Chris Rupkey, chief economist at FWDBONDS, adding that core PPI — which excludes food, energy and trade services — increased 0.5% in October.

"It is economics 101 that producer prices lead consumer prices and right now it looks like consumers will be on the hook for a fresh burst of factory inflation," he said.

The moves come after 10-year Treasury yields jumped more than 13 basis points to more than 5% in Thursday's session, with the 2-year yield soaring more than 15 basis points.

Investors now see a Federal Reserve rate hike next month as a certainty. Traders are pricing in 100% odds of a rise at the Dec. 9 meeting, according to the CME Group's FedWatch tool. Odds of a quarter percentage point hike now stand at 89%, with the chance of a half point increase at around 14%.

Energy inflation remains a key part of the puzzle, with oil posting its second weekly decline as prospects of renewed strikes by the U.S. against Iran fade. U.S. West Texas Intermediate futures for December delivery were down 0.2% at $88.56 per barrel. Brent crude, the international oil benchmark, was higher by 0.02% at $95.84.

#2862. Landry Shamet Makes It Clear Why Knicks Trust Him to Start

New York Knicks guard Landry Shamet has seen his role within the Knicks shift drastically over the last few games and it's due to the major leap that Shamet has taken in one skill that the Knicks are in dire need of.

Shamet's offensive skills are his most obvious trait, a trait he drew even more attention to with his crucial three point shooting during the Knicks' 2026 championship run, but those skills had thus far cemented his role as New York's spark off the bench.

But now, Shamet has been alternating between a sixth man role and the starting lineup, as he did against the Milwaukee Bucks in a 117-85 victory, but there's more than elite shooting at play behind Shamet's periodic elevation to the starting five.

New York Knicks guard Landry Shamet (44) moves the ball against the Chicago Bulls during the second half at United Center. Matt Marton-Imagn Images

That trait is Shamet's defense, something that's sorely needed by the Knicks at the guard position.

After the Knicks' loss to the Cleveland Cavaliers, a game in which Shamet came off the bench, yet both had more minutes and outscored both guard Mikal Bridges and forward Josh Hart, head coach Mike Brown spoke about how he was juggling minutes between Hart, Bridges, Shamet and guard Miles McBride primarily because of the latter two's defense.

Brooklyn Nets center Moritz Wagner (13) looks to pass the ball to forward Michael Porter Jr. (17) against New York Knicks center Andre Drummond (0) and guard Landry Shamet (44) during the first quarter at Madison Square Garden. Brad Penner-Imagn Images

Defense is something that Shamet has thrived at this season, but it ahsn't always been a part of his bag. In a recent interview, he discussed the steps he's taken to make that defensive leap.

"Once you've had a number of reps, you have a feel for the rhythm and you can let your instincts go. It's definitely something that's grown because it was a weak point in my draft card coming out of college," Shamet said (via SNY Knicks).

If Shamet's leap defensively is here to stay, he could become one of the most crucial two-way players the Knicks have at their disposal.

New York Knicks guard Landry Shamet (44) defends Cleveland Cavaliers guard Donovan Mitchell (45) in the fourth quarter at Rocket Arena. David Richard-Imagn Images

A true three-and-D guard is hard to find nowadays, even if it is common in concept, and Shamet's shooting abilities put him above most of his ilk. Neither Bridges nor guard Jalen Brunson are defense-first kind of players, so Shamet could be a great solution to New York's growing defensive woes.

He's done well in the limited opportunities he received as a main-minutes getter so far for New York and with more repetition he's likely to ascend even further. The Knicks need all of the defensive help they can get and an elevation like the one that Shamet has so far made is exactly what they need.

New York Knicks guard Landry Shamet (44) looks to pass the ball against Chicago Bulls forward Noa Essengue (2) and guard Tre Jones (30) during the third quarter at Madison Square Garden. Brad Penner-Imagn Images

Finding minutes for Shamet and the players he's been so far supplanting is a delicate balance for Brown to navigate, but if it means upgrading the Knicks defensively, it's certainly worth it.

#2861. Women are taking pay cuts and 'white-knuckling it' to manage menopause and work: 'I feel like I'm sitting on the sidelines'

Around 2020, Amy Terry noticed she didn't feel like her usual self.

The 50-year-old Michigander was working as a director of programming at a health association when she says she began experiencing symptoms like memory loss, low energy, mental processing difficulties — or, as she describes it, "waking up every day feeling like you were slogging through mud."

"I went from being this highly competent professional person to barely being able to get through phone calls," Terry says.

As the healthcare system strained under the weight of the Covid-19 pandemic, Terry, like many other people, struggled to get care. When she finally did, she found out it wasn't Covid-19. It was perimenopause.

Terry got through a few more years juggling her symptoms with her job, but by 2023, she couldn't hold out any longer. She quit to start independent consulting.

Women, and some trans and non-binary people, often experience career setbacks stemming from menopause that can hurt them financially and professionally. Four in 10 women who have experienced menopause or perimenopause say it has negatively affected their life or work, according to a recent survey of over 3,800 U.S. women from Gallup and Melinda French Gates' Pivotal, a group of organizations focused on social progress for women and young people.

Of them, roughly 28% felt limited in the effort they could give at work, 15% decided against pursuing a promotion or leadership role, 12% reduced their hours, and 10% took an extended leave from work or didn't return.

"This isn't just a health issue," says Jasmine Escalera, career expert at resume builder site LiveCareer. "It's a workplace issue."

A 2023 Mayo Clinic study estimated menopause symptoms drove $1.8 billion in lost work time per year in the U.S. alone.

Perimenopause, the transitional period before menopause, typically begins in one's 40s and can last several years, often bringing symptoms like hot flashes, sleep issues, irritability and lower energy, according to the Office on Women's Health. Menopause typically occurs in one's 50s and marks 12 months since a person's last period, but is often used colloquially to include the preceding perimenopause as well.

The timing of menopause can create a perfect storm. It frequently comes at a time in many women's careers when they're breaking into leadership or approaching their peak earning years, which is often between the ages of 35 and 44 for women, according to data from the Bureau of Labor Statistics. It also typically happens when many are caregiving for aging parents or raising children. This can make for an impossible choice for many women.

"Women have fought so hard for their place in the workforce," says Mimi Wied, who began perimenopause around a decade ago. "They don't want to lose it by looking like they can't handle it."

The career consequences for women "may increase the gaps we're trying to decrease," Escalera says, referring to disparities such as those in women's pay and senior-level representation relative to men. "That is a definite talent breakdown."

For Terry, becoming an independent consultant cost her employer-sponsored healthcare at the time she most needed it. It also drastically changed her income: She says she took around an 85% pay cut in the transition.

"These are supposed to be my highest-earning years," Terry says, "and I feel like I'm sitting on the sidelines."

Many women are unprepared for menopause because of social stigma around discussing it, poor health education and dismissal of women's health issues.

"I've been in the healthcare environment for 30 years; if I can't figure this out, I don't know how anyone can," says Terry.

Wied also says she didn't know what to expect. The answer quickly became apparent: The 48-year-old from Brenham, Texas, says she experienced everything from night sweats and sleeplessness to anxiety and rage.

"Sometimes I didn't feel in control of my own mouth and my body and my actions," she says.

Wied left her job as a museum docent for a secretarial role paying roughly $20,000 less when she worried her irritability would affect her capacity to help visitors, she says: "If I could have quit working, I would have."

When women do scale back at work because of menopause, they may struggle to regain that professional ground later. Terry's job switch wasn't just downshifting to a part-time role: "It's an entire change in the trajectory of your career," she says.

Factors like ageism in the job search for those of menopausal age, as well as the high cost of healthcare, particularly for common menopause treatments like hormone replacement therapy, may keep some women silently struggling through their symptoms at work.

Erin Levin is currently working through perimenopause. As a high school English teacher, the 41-year-old from San Jose, California, needs to stay on her A-game because "there is no down moment in teaching," she says.

But since starting perimenopause around two years ago, she's experienced brain fog, difficulty sleeping, and heightened anxiety and stress that make her work difficult sometimes, she says. She sometimes struggles with tasks that "were not an issue" for her a few years ago.

"Now I'm just like, God, why does it just feel so exhausting all the time?" Levin says.

Her parents help with childcare some evenings, and she tries not to schedule big tasks first thing in the morning, but it's been an adjustment. Scaling back at work isn't an option financially, she says, as a single mother sharing custody of her child.

"I still have to maintain the same appearance of having all the same skills and energy level as other people who aren't in my medical position," Levin says. "I just have to white-knuckle it."

Some women see hope in coming legislation and employer benefits offerings.

Last year, Rhode Island became the first state to enact workplace protections for women experiencing menopause. In September, Sens. Kirsten Gillibrand, D-N.Y., and Rick Scott, R-Fla., held the first congressional hearing devoted to menopause. Illinois and the city of Philadelphia have also adopted legislation taking effect in 2027 providing accommodations and protections for those in menopause.

In September, a nongovernmental organization, the International Organization for Standardization, published the first standard for developing workplace policies and practices supporting people through menstruation and menopause.

Many organizations have a ways to go in providing menopause-related benefits for their employees.

In a 2026 study from the Society for Women's Health Research and workplace menopause research organization The Fuchsia Tent, fielded among 1,100 people, around one-third of decision-makers said menopause support is currently in place or actively planned in their workplace. Fewer than 10% of employees said their organization offers menopause-specific support.

Respondents said some broader benefits, like flexible scheduling, remote options, and confidential telehealth or virtual care, would also be helpful for menopause.

Terry, Wied and Levin said they had to meet with multiple doctors before finding one who'd take their concerns seriously and who'd prescribe them hormone replacement therapy.

Levin and Wied each have a daughter. They hope better support and women's healthcare are available for their daughters when they begin menopause.

"I'm hoping that by the time my daughter goes through this, we'll have a lot more information, a lot more resources, because as it stands, it makes you super angry," Wied says.

"It's a cost on my energy, it's a cost on my stress levels, it's a cost on my bank account," Levin adds. "I don't want my daughter to have to face any of that."

As for Terry, she says she was part of a study examining potential mental health impacts of hormonal fluctuations in perimenopausal women; she hopes the research can help future generations of women. Meanwhile, her switch to independent consulting has offered her greater flexibility and a more manageable workload. But it's not perfect.

"I'm worried about retirement, I'm worried about having enough money, I'm worried about being able to continue healthcare," she says. "But I also feel like I have to take the time to feel like I'm on solid ground again."

#2860. James Harden is foundational to the Cavs’ success

James Harden arrived to the Cleveland Cavaliers as a divisive figure.

As the centerpiece of a trade that sent fan favorite Darius Garland to the Clippers, Harden was always going to face an uphill battle.

Now, coming off an Eastern Conference Finals appearance, all eyes are on Harden as he begins his second season with the team. After twelve games, one thing is clear: his performance will determine whether the Cavaliers can improve on last year.

The vibes around Harden hit a low point after Cleveland was walloped by the Charlotte Hornets, 124-112, on November 9.

He was at the center of the team's problems. There were long stretches of minimal effort on defense, and he often looked like he was coasting, barely attempting to bring down rebounds.

Offensively, it was just as bleak: 11 points on 3-9 shooting with four turnovers. Safe to say, the fanbase was ready to drive Harden to the airport and send him to whatever team, or country, would take him.

Then came the Knicks game on November 11.

Suddenly, Harden was energized and putting his mark on the game.

He orchestrated a 115-107 road win at Madison Square Garden and put the offense on his back with 32 points, going 9-for-14 from the field with five threes and 9-11 at the free throw line.

Putting those two games back to back is as stark as it is telling of where the Cavaliers are. The team knew the risks and still entered the James Harden business.

This isn't MVP Harden, and this isn't the Houston Rockets of the 2010s, but the sentiment still stands: Cleveland is reliant on Harden to make the pieces on this roster gel. When he is at his worst, the ripples spread throughout the entire lineup.

The numbers show just how much his play matters, if not in the way Cleveland hoped. Through his first 11 games, the Cavaliers had a -5.6 net rating with Harden on the floor and a +15.7 net rating with him off it. Whatever his Knicks performance does to those numbers, it was a long way from enough to erase them.

Is this too much to ask of a 37-year-old? Yes. But the Cavaliers knew this was the route they were taking when they traded for Harden and then extended him on a three-year, $97 million deal. At times this season, it has looked like a grave mistake.

Harden is posting some of the lowest numbers of his career: 16.7 points, 5.6 assists and 4.6 rebounds per game. That scoring is about seven points below his 23.6 average last season, and about four below the 20.5 he averaged in 26 games after joining Cleveland. His assists are down more than two per game from last season's 8.0.

Still, the Knicks game suggests the player fans expect isn't gone. The problem for the Cavaliers is how to keep Harden engaged and empowered enough that performances like that don't become few and far between.

It will be a balancing act. Cleveland has to meet Harden at a level of comfort that lets him use his best traits, while melding his heliocentric style with an offense that fits the rest of the roster and lets the ball move more freely. That will take time. The Cavaliers knew this came with Harden, and fans need to understand it won't be pretty until a resolution is found on the court.

#2859. Indianapolis Colts Injury Report: Who's In and Who's Out

The Indianapolis Colts today released their Friday injury report for week 10 of the NFL season against the Miami Dolphins.

With Travis out this week the Colts will have to find a suitable replacement at right tackle, it could mean Luke Tenuta starting in his place or a reshuffle moving Matt Goncalves to right tackle a starting Dalton Tucker at right guard.

Defensive tackle Jerry Tillery (calf) has been ruled OUT of Sundays game against the Dolphins. Expect a bigger role in the defensive tackle rotation for Tim Smith and Kevin Givens with Tillery out. There could also be a practice squad elevation for Cam Ball.

Defensive end Arden Key (hamstring) and Tight End Will Mallory (hamstring) are both QUESTIONABLE for Sundays game against the Dolphins. Key managed to practice all week but hadn't played since the London game. Mallory was a full participants all week but injured his hamstring during Fridays practice.

#2858. ‘We will sue them’: Canada threatens Stellantis as Trump trade war reshapes Jeep production plans

Canada has threatened to sue auto giant Stellantis over the planned sale of an idled assembly plant in Brampton, Ontario, alleging breaches of a federal funding agreement after the carmaker announced plans to shift production to the U.S. amid President Donald Trump's trade war.

Speaking at a news conference on Thursday, Industry Minister Mélanie Joly said the government had served Stellantis with a formal notice of default, giving the company 30 days to address its concerns or face legal action.

"We sent a notice of default and, basically, they have 30 days — or else, of course, we will sue them," Joly told reporters in Toronto.

Joly said Stellantis had violated its contractual commitments to the Canadian government and the workers at Brampton by announcing an agreement to sell the plant to armored vehicle manufacturer Roshel earlier in the week. She added that the company was now "on the hook" for hundreds of millions of dollars over the move.

Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney has described the situation at the Brampton plant as "unacceptable" and a "betrayal" of its workers.

The standoff comes as the U.S. and Canada remain locked in a bitter trade dispute, with Trump repeatedly putting pressure on automakers to move vehicle production south of the border.

Stellantis, which owns auto brands including Dodge, Fiat, Chrysler and Peugeot, had scrapped plans to build its Jeep Compass at the Brampton plant and shifted future production to a factory in Illinois amid U.S. auto tariffs and other market pressures.

Stellantis Canada CEO Trevor Longley said in a statement shared with CNBC that the company would return $24 million it received in government support for the Brampton Assembly's original retooling plan. This amount, however, falls significantly short of the Canadian government's own estimate of what the company owes.

"We recognize the disappointment and uncertainty surrounding the future of the facility. While our decision to sell the plant represents a change from the original plans for Brampton, the agreement we have advanced with a Canadian manufacturer provides a path to return the facility to productive industrial use," Longley said.

"As Canada's largest automotive employer, this decision also allows Stellantis to focus its resources on sustaining competitive manufacturing operations in Canada in the face of challenging market realities," he added.

Stellantis said Monday that it had agreed to sell the Brampton assembly plant to Roshel for an undisclosed amount.

It also acknowledged its obligations to employees at the facility and said it remains prepared to work with Unifor, Canada's largest private sector union, to negotiate a settlement to assist union-represented employees.

Stellantis stock has fallen sharply since the start of the year, with its Milan-listed shares down more than 60% for 2026 so far.

— CNBC's Michael Wayland contributed to this report.

#2857. Predictions: Lions vs. New England Patriots

Nolan Bianchi, Richard Silva, John Niyo and Bob Wojnowski of The Detroit News offer predictions for Sunday's game between the Lions and New England Patriots at Allianz Arena in Munich, Germany (9:30 a.m., Fox/97.1 FM).

▶ Nolan Bianchi: The Patriots are coming off a whupping at the hands of the Packers, which is never what an upcoming opponent wants to see. New England still has a ton of leftover talent from last year's Super Bowl run but hasn't been able to consistently put the pieces together this season. Still, their defensive front could give Detroit problems, considering how much the Lions have struggled to protect Jared Goff in recent weeks. But I suspect the Lions' defense could continue its run against a shorthanded Patriots receiving corps. And for as much as the Patriots' defense wants to rebound from last week's 45-point outpouring, the Lions' offense will be looking to get back on track, too, after scoring 20 points or less in three straight games. Pick: Lions, 28-23

▶ John Niyo: The Patriots got a taste of the NFC North last week and didn't like it at all. But the Lions need to realize this might be the second-best offense they've faced to date. Keeping Drake Maye in the pocket will be the key for the Lions' defense getting off the field on third downs. But injuries at running back may help them apply more pressure and force Maye into the kind of poor decisions that really cost him last week against Green Bay. On the other side of the ball, the Lions' offensive line needs a bounce-back performance in a big way. The Patriots' secondary looks vulnerable right now, but there's real talent back there, as well as a front that's capable of shutting down Jahmyr Gibbs and the run game. Pick: Lions, 27-24

▶ Richard Silva: This'll be a nice test for Detroit's defense, which allowed 31.8 points per game from Weeks 1-4 but has since rebounded well; the Lions have allowed an average of 15.5 points over their last four contests. The Patriots haven't been very consistent, but they rank 10th in Expected Points Added (EPA) per play this season, the second-highest ranking among all of the teams the Lions have played thus far (the Buffalo Bills, who played the Lions in Week 2, are the exception). Put simply: The Patriots are, statistically, among the better offenses the Lions will face, and a positive performance against them would further legitimize this defensive turnaround. I don't think traveling to Germany will have any more of an impact on the Lions than it will the Patriots. Any effects, at least in my opinion, would show themselves in Week 11, when the Lions come back to Ford Field to host the Tampa Bay Buccaneers, who will be coming off a bye week. But that's a problem for next week. Pick: Lions, 28-24

▶ Bob Wojnowski: The Lions have played considerably better than the Patriots of late, which usually means one thing in the NFL: Watch out for the bounce-back. New England (5-4) has lost two of three and was embarrassed at home by the Packers 45-27, with Drake Maye now tied for the league lead with 11 interceptions. They'll be an ornery bunch. The Lions (6-2) have won four straight with an improving defense, but other than their 20-17 victory over Green Bay and Jordan Love, haven't faced a top-notch quarterback. The Patriots haven't resembled their Super Bowl team but they're expected to get star receiver A.J. Brown back from injury. The Lions' offense has hit a rare three-game sputter, and Jahmyr Gibbs could face tough sledding against a tough run defense. Brad Holmes' questionable decision to basically stand pat at the trade deadline will be judged quickly. Especially if Maye finds his groove in Germany and turns the Lions' secondary into sauerkraut. Pick: Patriots, 31-27
