#2836. Hackers stole $6.3 billion from Binance—2 habits that take 'a few seconds' can help you avoid similar traps

The biggest heist in cryptocurrency history began with small mistakes many internet users make. A hacking group tied to North Korea recently stole $6.3 billion from the crypto exchange Binance. The operation was sophisticated, involving fake job interviews and malicious code.

But it was also mundane. Although artificial intelligence played a role in the hack, this was not a cyberattack of the future, propelled by swarms of AI agents. Rather, it relied on humans who made some basic errors, like clicking a link because it appeared first on a search results page or trusting strangers at a fake company without taking a few seconds to look them up.

We work at the Digital Inquiry Group, a nonprofit that researches how people evaluate online information and creates free, evidence-based curriculum that helps people decide what to trust.

In over a decade of research, we've found that people frequently engage in online behavior that makes them more likely to get duped. Two simple strategies can help you better navigate today's information landscape, from traditional phishing to misleading social posts to AI fakes. Here's how.

The Binance heist began when one of the company's engineers downloaded compromised software. That engineer, according to the Reuters investigation of the hack, clicked on a sponsored search result that appeared higher on the results page and directed them to the malicious code.

Our research at the Digital Inquiry Group suggests internet users frequently behave as if first is best. They click on the first thing they see. They incorrectly associate a higher placement with vetted content, ceding judgment to tech company algorithms. Students have gone so far as to tell us "Google is almost never wrong" or "AI is always right."

Professional fact-checkers commonly practice click restraint. They understand that whether you're searching on the internet or querying a chatbot, first isn't always best. If you're researching an organization, the first link will often be to its own site, which is hardly likely to disclose anything that's wrong with it.

So the next time you search for something online, be wary of links to sponsored content, the organization you're investigating, or to open platforms where anyone can post and information isn't verified, such as Quora or Reddit. Read the brief snippets beneath each link, and make a more informed choice about what to open.

Always ask yourself: Who is behind this information? What makes them credible? Look for people and organizations that have direct expertise on the topic at hand, or have processes to ensure the accuracy of information, such as news outlets that employ editors and fact-checkers.

Binance says it caught that first malicious download, replaced the device and changed passwords. But later, another engineer downloaded compromised code as part of what they thought was a job interview, which included a virtual meeting with the supposed CEO of a fake company.

It's tempting to think that the engineer should have seen something was off. But the internet is a precarious place because bad actors know how to make content feel legitimate. Binance says its employee was snared by a hacker posing as an HR rep who "shared a social media account with him, including personal photos and videos posted on the platform, in order to build trust."

When they encounter a piece of unfamiliar content, professional fact-checkers don't just read it closely — they read laterally. They open new browser tabs to see what other, credible sources have to say. Fact-checkers know that bad actors make themselves look good on their home turf, but that it's a lot harder to fake a good reputation across the internet.

The next time you encounter something unfamiliar online, look for authoritative sources that can shed light on the person or organization. You might find a news article that confirms the claim you're looking into, or a report that identifies a supposedly neutral source as biased.

In some cases, you may find nothing at all, which is a signal in itself: if you look up a company that reached out to you and find no external confirmation of its existence, that should be an immediate red flag.

Had that first Binance engineer engaged in click restraint and verified the source of the software, they might have avoided the malicious download. Had that second employee taken time to seek external verification about the supposed HR rep, rather than trusting a polished social media profile, they might have realized that something didn't add up.

The internet and AI have made it harder to know whether what we see is authentic. They've also made it easier than ever before to investigate who or what we encounter online. It's always worth pausing to check. A few seconds up front can save us all a lot of time, grief and money later on.

Nadav Ziv is a researcher at the Digital Inquiry Group. Joel Breakstone is co-founder and executive director of the Digital Inquiry Group.

#2835. NFL news, live updates: Kirk Cousins officially named Vikings' Week 10 starter after trade deadline swap with Raiders

The 2026 NFL trade deadline is officially in the rearview mirror, and we're on to Week 10, where we'll see a couple of players in new uniforms this week — and some in their old uniforms.

Kirk Cousins rejoined the Minnesota Vikings this week via a trade with the Las Vegas Raiders. He will officially don purple this weekend against the Green Bay Packers after head coach Kevin O'Connell officially named him the team's Week 10 starter on Friday. He'll make his return to Minnesota against the very team he last faced as a Viking, when he suffered a season-ending Achilles tear. He'll look to give the offense a much-needed boost to complement Brian Flores' defense after the team's 6-2 start.

With the trade of Cousins, the Raiders officially named 2026 No. 1 pick Fernando Mendoza as their starter moving forward. He'll face a tough schedule to kick off his career as a starting quarterback, with the Seattle Seahawks and Denver Broncos on deck in Weeks 10 and 11.

Follow along with Yahoo Sports for the latest NFL news, injury updates and more ahead of Week 10.

After a great deal of speculation was made that Jonathan Taylor could be moved ahead of the NFL trade deadline, the Indianapolis Colts are reportedly standing pat.

The team will not trade him ahead of the deadline, NFL insider Tom Pelissero reported Tuesday afternoon.

Pelissero reported that the Colts had received multiple offers for Taylor of a second-round pick-plus, but instead, the team will move forward with him as a foundational piece within the locker room as they look to rebuild.

The San Francisco 49ers traded for veteran wideout Keenan Allen this week, but they're getting another reinforcement Sunday on the road against the Dallas Cowboys. Fellow receiver Deebo Samuel Sr., who has missed the past three games while recovering from a hamstring issue, has been cleared to play.

The Niners will, however, still be without Mike Evans. His Achilles injury will keep him out for a third straight game.

As expected, the Minnesota Vikings have named Kirk Cousins their starting quarterback for Week 10's matchup against the Green Bay Packers. That will mark his first start with the organization since 2023, when his season ended prematurely after tearing his Achilles (coincidentally, against the Packers at Lambeau).

It remains unclear who will back up Cousins in Week 10. Kyler Murray has been dealing with a hamstring injury, and Carson Wentz … well, he solidified the team's need to trade for Cousins in the first place with a disastrous start against the Buffalo Bills on "Monday Night Football."

Asked if he'll play on Sunday versus the Carolina Panthers, Etienne said, "I hope so. I definitely want to."

#2832. Top Wall Street analysts suggest these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Inflation data, earnings reports and tensions in the Middle East have been influencing the market. In this volatile backdrop, investors seeking income can bolster their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

The recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help inform investors in their search for attractive stocks from the vast universe of dividend-paying companies. These experts assign ratings after analyzing a company's financials and ability to consistently pay dividends while also seeking capital appreciation.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Exxon Mobil has increased its dividends for 43 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.03 per share (annualized dividend of $4.12), XOM stock offers a yield of 2.6%.

In his latest research report on energy players, Morgan Stanley analyst Devin McDermott reiterated a buy rating on Exxon Mobil stock and increased his price target to $177 from $168.

The analyst raised his free cash flow estimates to reflect Q2 guidance and the oil price strip, with the largest increase noted for integrated players due to higher refining margins. Notably, the five-star analyst raised his cash flow estimates by an average of 20% for the second half of 2026 and 7% for 2027.

McDermott highlighted that while pure-play refiners have rallied on stronger margins, integrated energy stocks have lagged, creating relative-value opportunities in major North American integrated producers, including Exxon Mobil, Suncor and Cenovus Energy.

Overall, McDermott is bullish on Exxon Mobil, as he expects the company to deliver strong earnings and cash flow growth, driven by robust upstream volumes, high-value projects in Product Solutions, and more than $5 billion of additional structural cost savings.

"XOM's large global integrated operations and strong balance sheet (0.0x 2027 net leverage) make it a clear defensive play in an uncertain macro backdrop," said McDermott.

McDermott ranks No. 726 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.80%. See Exxon Mobil Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Moving on to natural gas producer Expand Energy. The company announced a quarterly base dividend of $0.575 per share to be paid on Sept. 3. At an annualized dividend of $2.30 per share, EXE offers a yield of about 2.3%.

Recently, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reaffirmed a buy rating on Expand Energy stock and increased his price target to $113 from $99. Mehta attributed his higher price target to cash flow improvement and a strong outlook for shareholder returns. He also cited the stock's attractive valuation, with an 11% free cash flow, or FCF, yield based on his 2027/2028 estimates compared with the peer average of 9%.

The five-star analyst expects Expand Energy to generate FCF per share of about $10 in FY28, up from the prior estimate of about $8 at his mid-cycle price forecast of $3.50/MMBtu Henry Hub. Mehta raised his FCF estimate to reflect an improved outlook for share repurchases, operational efficiencies, and gas pricing realizations.

Furthermore, the analyst expects the recently announced $1.25 billion Twin Eagle acquisition to enhance the competitive positioning of the combined business to expand to premium markets and capture incremental deals in the power and LNG end markets.

Mehta ranks No. 449 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.1%. See Expand Energy Insider Trading on TipRanks.

Mehta is also bullish on Diamondback Energy, an independent oil and natural gas company focused on energy reserves that are primarily in the Permian Basin in West Texas. The company recently paid a base cash dividend of $1.10 per share for the second quarter of 2026. FANG stock offers a dividend yield of 2.2%.

Mehta increased his price target on FANG stock to $220 from $212 and reiterated a buy rating, citing the company's capital-efficient volume growth as oil prices remain high amid persistent supply disruption in the Middle East.

"We remain constructive on FANG, a high-quality, pure-play Permian Basin operator that continues to drive incremental capital efficiencies and strong well productivity relative to peers," said Mehta.

Also, the analyst said that the removal of a minimum return of capital commitment gives Diamondback more flexibility in taking capital allocation decisions amid high oil price volatility and macro uncertainty.

Mehta added that Diamondback sees a favorable environment for production growth, driven by the potential need for global inventory restocking. He highlighted that during Q2 2026, FANG's production volumes of 1,018 Mboe/d exceeded the high end of the company's guidance, driven by a stronger-than-expected natural gas output from Barnett development and improved downstream gas marketing.

Overall, Mehta is optimistic about Diamondback's growth and sees more upside in the stock, given higher production estimates, solid productivity and a deepening position in the Barnett shale formation. See Diamondback Energy Financials on TipRanks.

#2831. OpenAI launches ChatGPT Wallet, allowing its 'dots' AI agents to make purchases for users

OpenAI on Friday announced ChatGPT Wallet, a new payment feature that enables its "dots" artificial intelligence agents to make purchases on behalf of users.

The company built ChatGPT Wallet in partnership with American Express, Mastercard and Visa, and it allows cardholders to maintain their existing credit card rewards and protections. Eligible users enter their card information once, prompt their dot to find items to buy and approve each purchase with a security check.

OpenAI said its dots agents will never actually access a user's card details. Instead, their payment networks will provide the dot with a "payment token," which contains a scoped card number, security code and expiration date that is specific to a given transaction.

"Our goal here is to really focus on merchants and our network partners and start building out a really great foundation that's safe and easy to use," Chaya Nayak, who leads product for monetization and payments at OpenAI, told CNBC in an interview.

ChatGPT Wallet comes as a growing number of consumers are exploring how to use personal AI agents to manage their calendars, book travel and complete other day-to-day tasks. OpenAI introduced the dots agents during its annual developer conference in late September, shortly after Meta's Muse offering topped the App Store charts.

Purchases made through ChatGPT Wallet could eventually kick-start a new revenue stream for OpenAI, but the company said it is not taking a cut of transactions to start. With the initial launch, OpenAI is working to build relationships with merchants and position itself as a serious platform for shoppers, especially as vendors are beginning to experiment with personal agents.

OpenAI has pushed deeper into AI-enabled commerce over the last year, but some of its early attempts failed to take off. The company introduced an "Instant Checkout" feature last fall, which it quickly pivoted away from. By March, it had rolled out a revamped shopping experience that aimed to make it easier for users to find and compare products.

Nayak said ChatGPT Wallet builds on OpenAI's previous efforts, particularly around product discovery.

"We think that this is the next step, where if someone found something they're really excited to buy, then we want to be able to get them through to that endpoint," she said.

OpenAI's ChatGPT chatbot supports more than 1.2 billion weekly active users and nearly 100 million paying users as of the end of the third quarter. With such a broad user base, Gebb said OpenAI can decide to "turn up the volume" on ChatGPT Wallet when it wants to.

"It just makes sense to watch it, start it in a contained environment, and then keep rolling from there," Gebb told CNBC in an interview.

Merchants and vendors are still reckoning with the rise of personal AI agents, and some companies have opted to forgo them entirely. United Airlines said Wednesday that it will block agents from accessing its systems to book tickets, and Amazon prevents the agents from purchasing products on its platform.

OpenAI said Friday that agentic commerce is still in its "early days," and it published tools and guidance alongside its ChatGPT Wallet announcement to help businesses start to navigate the transition. The company explained how merchants can make sure their websites aren't inadvertently blocking OpenAI's agents, for instance.

"It's going to take time for the ecosystem to build the right infrastructure to be able to make this experience really amazing and seamless across all of their sites," Nayak said. "Really, who we are speaking to here is the networks and the merchants."

#2828. Supreme Court agrees to hear Robinhood challenge to IPO information omission lawsuit

The Supreme Court on Friday agreed to hear a case in which Robinhood seeks to block a class-action lawsuit over the omission of interim financial data from the online broker's initial public offering in 2021.

Robinhood wants the Supreme Court to overturn a ruling by the 9th Circuit U.S. Court of Appeals that revived a lawsuit by investors against the company and firms involved with the IPO, including Goldman Sachs and J.P. Morgan Securities.

A San Francisco federal court judge had previously dismissed the lawsuit.

The Trump administration and the Securities and Exchange Commission had urged the Supreme Court to hear Robinhood's appeal.

"We are pleased the Supreme Court has agreed to hear our case and look forward to presenting our argument," a Robinhood spokesperson said in a statement.

CNBC has requested comment from the attorneys representing the plaintiffs in the case.

Robinhood's IPO came on the heels of a surge in investor interest during the Covid-19 pandemic in so-called meme stocks, and trading in the crypto asset dogecoin.

In July 2021, Robinhood conducted its IPO, "for which it prepared a registration statement" that "contained only limited information about Robinhood's performance during the second quarter of 2021," the 9th Circuit panel that heard the case noted.

"After the initial public offering, Robinhood reported financial results from that second quarter, and its stock price dropped," the panel wrote.

The investors who sued Robinhood and the other defendants alleged the registration statement omitted material information it was legally required to disclose.

#2827. Wholesale prices rose 0.3% in October, putting annual rate at 6.5%, the highest in nearly four years

Wholesale prices increased less than expected in October, but inflation pressures persisted and the Federal Reserve is likely still on target for an interest rate increase soon.

The producer price index, which measures the prices domestic producers get for their products before they reach the consumer, rose a seasonally adjusted 0.3% for the month, against the Dow Jones consensus for a 0.4% increase, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported Friday.

Stripping out food and energy, core PPI posted a 0.3% gain, meeting expectations.

On an annual basis, headline PPI accelerated 6.5%, up 0.2 percentage point for the highest annual rate since November 2022.

The report comes just a few weeks ahead of the Federal Reserve's next policy meeting, Dec. 8-9. Markets are fully pricing in a quarter percentage point increase in the central bank's key overnight borrowing rate, with slight odds that the Federal Open Market Committee might opt for a half-point hike.

Stock market futures added to gains following the release while Treasury yields were mixed.

Earlier this week, the BLS reported that inflation at the cash register was hotter than expected.

The headline consumer price index saw a 0.4% increase in October while core was up 0.3%, both 0.1 percentage point higher than expected. Because of the government shutdown in October 2025, the BLS couldn't collect some data and refrained from putting out a 12-month inflation figure for CPI.

However, the figures showed that the Fed remains a good distance from its 2% inflation goal.

"Today's PPI data – which shows some signs of improving, albeit still too-high – is unlikely to change the Fed's approach in December because the CPI data was already outside their comfort zone," said Chris Zaccarelli, chief investment officer for Northlight Asset Management.

Moreover, policymakers are beginning to worry that inflation generated by the Iran war and higher fuel prices, along with President Donald Trump's tariffs, are beginning to spread through the economy. The Fed generally would look through such price pressures as temporary, but the breadth of inflation could indicate that inflation is becoming more entrenched.

"We've had some signs of trouble now for several months in a row," Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee said in a CNBC interview Friday. "If you look at how broad-based the inflation is, it's not just that we're above the target and have been for some time. It's that in categories that are not driven by oil prices and not driven by tariffs, you're still seeing stalled progress and in some cases going the wrong way."

Stripping out food, energy and trade services, PPI saw an increase of 0.5% in October.

Goods prices increased 0.5% in October, with more than 60% of that move driven by goods excluding food and energy, which actually saw a 0.1% decrease. On an annual basis, the goods index jumped 10.2%, the biggest 12-month surge since October 2022.

Final demand services prices increased 0.2%, most of which was linked to transportation and warehousing services, and within that category a 3.9% gain in truck transportation of freight.

"We've been above 2% for almost six years, and while it sometimes we were making progress, lately we're not making any progress, and if anything, it's going the wrong way," Goolsbee said. "Inflation is rising; it's proved far more persistent than we predicted at the beginning from the supply shocks."

Other items in the PPI report also showed multiyear gains. Prices for intermediate processed goods soared 14.6% from October 2025, the biggest annual gain since July 2022. Intermediate prices for services posted a 6.9% increase, the most since November 2022.

#2825. They train people to avoid 'honeypot' scams—here are the common red flags they spot: 'It feels so good to be wanted'

Shawnee Delaney often kicks off a training session by telling a room full of corporate workers: "OK, raise your hand if you've ever gotten a flattering message on LinkedIn just complimenting your background and your profile and your experience."

"Every hand goes up," she says. Then, she tells attendees how responding to a simple adulatory message could unwittingly open the door to a potential romance scam.

Delaney is a corporate security expert, well-versed in teaching people how to avoid “honeypot” schemes, in which a bad actor uses romantic or sexual interest to build trust and gain information, access or leverage over a target. The term is currently making headlines in the wake of former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's resignation from the company, following reporting on his admitted affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

Honeypots, also known as "honey traps," are a very human form of social engineering attack, a category that also includes various forms of phishing and use of deepfake technology. And you don't have to be a billionaire tech CEO to possess information that scammers may value.

As competition in the global artificial intelligence race heats up, OpenAI is reportedly training employees to recognize espionage tactics including bribery, romantic manipulation and blackmail, according to The Wall Street Journal. On Nov. 3, cryptocurrency exchange Binance revealed that a recent cyberattack — which saw roughly $6 billion of digital assets stolen — was partially made possible by a fake job interview scheme that infected an employee’s work computer with malware.

"People can get solicited, bribed, romanced, etc.," says Kevin Tian, co-founder and CEO of Doppel, an AI-powered platform meant to help identify and deflect social engineering attacks. He adds: "If you've got access to sensitive IP [intellectual property] — it's not just AI companies — you can be a target."

Increasingly, scammers can use AI to scour websites ranging from Reddit to Pinterest for an individual's personal information and customize a ploy designed specifically for them, Tian says. Once they have relevant data points, attackers tend to use "the oldest tricks in the book: elicitation, flattery, ego," says Delaney, a former intelligence officer with the Defense Intelligence Agency and the founder and CEO of Washington, D.C.-based corporate security consulting firm Vaillance Group.

In many cases, Delaney notes, targets have an inkling that something is suspicious — but they'd rather allow themselves to believe the interest is legitimate. So how do you keep yourself from falling victim? Trust your self-awareness, common sense and ability to identify situations that feel too good to be true, she says.

"The bottom line is, we're all human," says Delaney, adding: "It feels so good to be wanted, or to be respected, or to be appreciated, or loved."

Delaney, who has worked with Fortune 500 companies such as Uber and Merck, advises corporate clients on cybersecurity risks and develops training programs to improve employees' awareness of security threats. Her sessions aim to build awareness of manipulative connections, such as romance scams, from how they begin to the natural progression they often take. "Introduction, then repeated contact, favors, sensitive requests — the secrecy, the pressure," she says.

Bad actors may tap into any information you post online to find an interest that can be mirrored and exploited, or an acquaintance who can be infiltrated and make an introduction. If someone you know and trust makes an introduction, you might be less likely to "go do due diligence" on the new person, even if they're a stranger, Delaney says.

One of Vaillance's services includes building a "targeting package" around an executive or C-Suite member, says Delaney. "We can show how they are vulnerable and how we would target that organization just by leveraging the people around them in that circle: spouses, friends, kids, parents, administrative assistants, neighbors," she says. "That's when people's eyes open."

From there, she discusses red flags to spot. Conversations that begin with flattery and seemingly innocuous small talk can gradually shift toward digging for information, introductions to colleagues or access to documents, says Delaney. It's human nature to want to be helpful and fulfill friendly requests, she adds. Add a potential romantic connection to the mix, especially amid an ongoing "loneliness epidemic," and the urge to comply can be even harder to resist, she says.

If you feel yourself getting cornered in a situation where a recent acquaintance or romantic partner keeps asking for information you're not comfortable revealing, Delaney says it helps to have first practiced polite refusal. "One of my favorites is: They could just respond with: 'Why do you ask?'" she says. "Put the other person on the defensive. You could give a nondescript answer. You can say you don't know. You can say you can't respond."

If you're ever suspicious of a new connection — even if you don't want to be — do yourself a favor and conduct some basic vetting, she recommends. You might examine their social media profiles and other publicly available information, for example, to see if their story checks out.

If you can't find enough information to allay your suspicions, don't be shy about alerting your employer's security or human resources team, she says. For smaller companies, or scams outside the workplace, you can file a complaint for cyber threats or romance scams with the Federal Bureau of Investigation's Internet Crime Complaint Center.

And pay attention if your new paramour starts asking you specific questions about your finances or work credentials, says Delaney.

"If you have no game at home, and you get off an airplane somewhere and suddenly everyone's all over you, we call that a red flag," she says.

#2823. Retirees sometimes tap savings for one-off expenses. Here’s what advisors say to consider first

Retirees facing a large expense that's not in their regular household budget may turn to their traditional 401(k) or individual retirement account balances to cover it.

While retirement savings may be the easiest place to turn for the money, financial advisors say there are a few things worth considering before making one-off withdrawals.

"Be aware of the fact that it's not as simple as just taking out the amount of money that [covers] what you want it for," said certified financial planner Victoria Trumbower, managing member of Trumbower Financial Advisors in Bethesda, Maryland. The firm ranked No. 19 on CNBC's Financial Advisor 100 list for 2026.

Read coverage of CNBC's 2026 Financial Advisor 100 list, including stories featuring ranked firms and their advisors:

At the same time, research shows that many retirees aren't even spending their nest egg. About a third still have 100% or more of their initial assets when they reach their mid-80s, according to a May study by the Employee Benefit Research Institute, a nonpartisan research group.

"Retirement savings are meant to be spent," said CFP Alex Canellopoulos, director of wealth and a partner with Vista Capital Partners in Portland, Oregon. "A one-time withdrawal isn't necessarily a bad decision, as long as it fits within a sustainable retirement plan."

In 2025, about 15% of 401(k) owners ages 60 to 72 with balances of at least $100,000 who had left their employer made an ad hoc withdrawal from their account, according to Vanguard's How America Retires 2026 report, which analyzed more than 125,000 ex-plan participants.

This age group falls between the onset of penalty-free withdrawals — age 59½ — and the threshold for starting required minimum distributions — currently age 73. RMDs are amounts that retirees must withdraw annually from traditional 401(k)s and IRAs.

Not all 401(k) plans allow occasional withdrawals from ex-employees — about 43% did in 2025, up from 16% in 2015, the Vanguard study says. If a plan doesn't offer such ad hoc distributions, a former employee who wants to tap that money would have to withdraw or roll over the entire balance. Typically, they roll it over to an IRA and take withdrawals from there.

The most common reasons for retirees taking irregular distributions, based on a 2025 Vanguard survey of 8,000 clients noted in the recent report, are paying off debt, with 31% of respondents citing it; covering unforeseen expenses such as medical care and home repairs, noted by 23%; and discretionary spending, with 22% citing it as a reason.

Regardless of why you take the money, advisors say a key consideration is the impact on your tax situation.

Distributions from a traditional 401(k) or IRA are generally taxed as ordinary income. If you don't have other means to cover the tax bill, you may have to withdraw extra from your retirement account to compensate — which creates a tricky situation.

"It's like a double whammy because you have to pay income tax, but you don't have any money to pay income tax, so you've got to withdraw more money to pay the tax, and then you've got to pay taxes" on the extra amount, Trumbower said.

For illustration: If you need $50,000 after taxes and assume a combined federal and state income tax rate of 25%, the math says you would need to withdraw about $66,667. That's because the money withdrawn to cover the tax bill is itself taxable. A $66,667 withdrawal taxed at 25% would result in about $16,667 in taxes, leaving $50,000 after tax.

"It's going to cost you a lot more than you think," Trumbower said.

A taxable withdrawal from retirement savings can also trigger additional costs elsewhere.

For example, if you're receiving Social Security retirement benefits, the added income could cause a larger portion of that money to become taxable. Depending on how much other income you have, 0%, up to 50%, or up to 85% of your Social Security benefits may be subject to federal income tax. A large retirement-account withdrawal can increase that other income and make more of your benefits taxable.

For retirees on Medicare, a large taxable withdrawal also could increase their Part B and Part D premiums through the income-related monthly adjustment amount, or IRMAA.

For 2026, that surcharge applies when modified adjusted gross income exceeds $109,000 for an individual filer or $218,000 for a married couple filing jointly. Medicare generally bases the surcharge on tax information from two years earlier, meaning 2026 premiums are typically based on 2024 income.

"What catches many people off guard is that they often don't feel that impact until a couple of years after the withdrawal," said CFP Ciano Villaquiran, a partner with CURO Financial Planning in San Diego.

For some retirees, it may be worth delaying the additional withdrawal needed to cover the tax bill until the next calendar year, Trumbower said. That second distribution would be taxable in the following year, potentially spreading the income — and its tax impact — across two tax years.

"Pushing the [additional] distribution from the IRA into the following calendar year may possibly avoid jumping up into a higher tax bracket," Trumbower said.

However, she said, retirees should still withhold enough tax from the initial distribution to avoid an underpayment penalty. The federal income tax system is generally pay-as-you-go, meaning taxpayers must pay enough tax through withholding or estimated payments during the year rather than waiting until they file their return.

Generally, taxpayers can avoid an underpayment penalty if their withholding and estimated payments equal at least 90% of the current year's tax liability or 100% of the prior year's tax liability. For certain higher-income taxpayers, the prior-year threshold is 110%. Any remaining tax due generally can be paid when the return is filed.

If you have a brokerage account, that may be a better source to fund a one-off expense, Trumbower said.

Taxes on long-term capital gains — profits on investments such as stocks held for more than one year — are generally lower than ordinary income tax rates. Depending on your taxable income, some long-term capital gains may even be taxed at 0%.

For 2026, the 0% long-term capital gains rate applies to taxable income of up to $49,450 for single filers and $98,900 for married couples filing jointly. Above those thresholds, most long-term capital gains are taxed at 15%, with a 20% rate applying at higher income levels.

Depending on the expense, retirees may be able to borrow money instead of tapping their savings, Trumbower said.

For example, a home equity loan or line of credit could help pay for a major home improvement project, while a car purchase could be financed with an auto loan rather than a large withdrawal from retirement savings.

Of course, borrowing comes with its own costs and risks. The loan payments would need to fit within the retiree's budget, and the interest paid could outweigh the potential benefits of leaving retirement savings untouched.

Additionally, it's worth considering how the withdrawal could affect your savings over time.

"Pulling a large amount out for a one-time expense doesn't just reduce the account balance today; it can reduce what that money could have grown into down the road," Villaquiran said.

For illustration: $50,000 earning 5% annually would grow to about $81,400 after 10 years, according to the Securities and Exchange Commission's Investor.gov compound-interest calculator. If a retiree withdrew $10,000 at the start and left $40,000 invested under the same assumptions, it would grow to about $65,200 — a difference of roughly $16,300 after 10 years.

#2822. Vikings name Week 10 starter vs Packers after Kirk Cousins trade

The Minnesota Vikings finally made the announcement that many have been expecting for several days.

Quarterback Kirk Cousins will start against the Green Bay Packers in Week 10, head coach Kevin O'Connell announced on Friday, Nov. 13. The intra-divisional game will mark the start of Cousins' second stint in Minnesota, three years after his last game for the Vikings.

"Kirk Cousins will start the football game. Cannot give him enough credit for coming in here, how he's gone about the week. Both him and the coaching staff getting him ready to go, working extremely hard, using all the time we possibly can," O'Connell said.

"It's been really impressive, what he's been able to do ... just getting right back in the huddle with a lot of guys he's played with, but certainly some guys he hasn't. It's been something that everybody's been very, very impressed by. Excited to start Kirk in the game, see him play on Sunday."

KIRK COUSINS: Veteran QB kept mum about his chance to start Week 10

Coincidentally, the upcoming Nov. 15 clash will be Cousins' second straight matchup against the Packers as the Vikings' starting quarterback. The last game the 38-year-old played for Minnesota, in 2023, was also at Lambeau Field.

Cousins' first stint with the Vikings ended unceremoniously – trainers had to cart him off of the field after he tore his Achilles during a game his team went on to win. In the offseason that followed, the Vikings allowed him to walk in free agency, and he went on to sign a four-year, $180 million contract with the Atlanta Falcons.

Cousins said in his (re-)introductory press conference with the Vikings on Nov. 11 that he expects the Week 10 game to be meaningful. It's both his first game back with Minnesota but also his return to Lambeau Field for the first time since the 2023 injury.

"It means a lot to play at Lambeau Field, no matter what the circumstances," Cousins said. "Knowing that that was where I was carted off, and I had thoughts of, 'Is this my last game? Is this my last game as a Viking?' To be back out there, wearing the purple and gold. It has meaning, but you have to go make it mean something with the way you play."

In nine starts with the Las Vegas Raiders this season, Cousins has a 4-5 record. He was 202-of-315 passing for 2,340 yards, 20 touchdowns and eight interceptions. His 97.1 passer rating so far this season is his highest since his previous stint with Minnesota.

The Vikings are hoping the addition of Cousins will help kick-start an offensive resurgence. The team has lost two straight following a 6-0 start, and it sits in third place in a tightly contested NFC North.

While the defense has been a strong point – the 14.5 points per game the unit has allowed so far is the fewest in the league – the offense has struggled. Minnesota's 18.3 points scored per game are sixth-fewest in the NFL.

#2820. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 stocks for the long haul

Global stock markets have been volatile as investors assess high bond yields and continued tensions between the U.S. and Iran. There are concerns about the impact of uncertainty in the Middle East on oil prices and consequently on inflation.

Investors who prefer to look beyond short-term noise and buy stocks with attractive long-term growth potential can follow the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts. The ratings of these experts are backed by in-depth research of a company's financials and thorough analysis of the factors affecting its business performance.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Semiconductor giant Nvidia (NVDA) reported stellar fiscal second-quarter results and reassured investors about continued strength in AI-driven demand for its chips. Moreover, Nvidia's FY28 revenue growth outlook of 70% was well above the Street's expectations.

Reacting to the Q2 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reiterated a buy rating on NVDA and increased his price target to $300 from $288. "This is our Top Pick in the semis group, with a compelling product cycle, exceptional growth and valuation below peers," said the 5-star analyst.

Nvidia delivered impressive quarterly results despite supply constraints, Moore said, but he believes that the FY28 revenue and gross margin commentary mattered more, with a favorable outlook for both measures.

Moore said Nvidia's 70% revenue growth guidance for FY28 was above his own estimate of 52% and the consensus of about 40%. The top-line growth estimate was a "remarkable figure" given supply issues and Moore said he expects Nvidia to continue to address bottlenecks to deliver rapid growth.

Among other takeaways, the analyst said performance metrics for Vera Rubin chips were strong, with Nvidia emphasizing a 30x higher throughput per megawatt and 35x lower token cost compared with Grace Blackwell Ultra.

Moore ranks No. 159 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 60% of the time, delivering an average return of 24.60%. See Nvidia Statistics on TipRanks.

Ride-hailing and delivery platform Uber Technologies (UBER) was recommended by BMO Capital analyst Brian Pitz, who discussed the company's opportunity in the autonomous vehicle (AV) market in a recent research note that reiterated a buy rating and price target of $119.

The 5-star analyst highlighted Uber's transition into a diversified marketplace, with investors now focusing on its AV growth prospects. The key question is how much value can Uber generate as AVs become commercialized, Pitz asked.

"We continue to believe that Uber's AV strategy is evolving and will become a significant driver of revenue and profitability," the analyst said.

Importantly, Pitz believes that Uber can establish itself as a preferred mobility platform for AV makers, supported by its growing AV infrastructure capabilities and an expanding partner base. This would enable Uber to win a significant share of value as the AV market is commercialized.

Additionally, Pitz noted Uber's enhanced position in the AV value chain, with the company shifting from serving as a distribution channel for robotaxis to becoming a broader platform for AV mobility. The analyst discussed Uber's efforts to capture growth opportunities in the AV market, including higher capital spending, expansion into new markets and building partnerships beyond Alphabet's Waymo.

Pitz ranks No. 574 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of about 12.20%. See Uber Technologies Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Semiconductor company Marvell Technology (MRVL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the second quarter of FY27. But the company's increased FY28 guidance fell short of investors' high expectations.

In reaction to the Q2 FY27 results, KeyBanc analyst John Vinh reaffirmed a buy rating on Marvell Technology, with a price target of $400. The analyst noted that Marvell's results and outlook were driven by strength in its data center business. The Q2 FY27 data center business grew 46% year-over-year, to $2.17 billion, far above KeyBanc's estimate of $2.09 billion.

Management "highlighted accelerating AI networking demand across scale-out, scale-across and scale-up architectures, supported by strong 800G optical DSP demand, rapid 1.6T ramp, broader 51.2T switch deployments and higher TIA/driver demand," Vinh said.

Consequently, Marvell raised its data center revenue guidance, now expecting 60% growth in FY27 and more than 60% in FY28.

Regarding the recently announced $120 billion warrant arrangement with Google, Vinh said management argued that programs tied to the warrant are already priced into the custom outlook through FY28. Meanwhile, new programs and those in execution are expected to drive significant upside to the previous FY29 custom XPU revenue estimate of over $10 billion, Vinh said, citing management.

Vinh ranks No. 123 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 60% of the time, delivering an average return of 28.50%. See Marvell Technology Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2819. Hassett denies report he's leaving White House: 'Not true'

National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett on Friday poured cold water on a report that he's expected to leave the White House in the coming weeks.

That report is "not true," Hassett told CNBC's Eamon Javers in a text message.

Semafor reported earlier Friday afternoon that Hassett "has been inquiring about roles outside the administration," even before Republicans' drubbing in last week's midterm election.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, and his counselor David Zervos, are among the names being floated to replace Hassett, according to Semafor, which cited unnamed people familiar with his plans.

But a White House official told CNBC in a statement later Friday, "There is no factual basis for this reporting."

"Kevin Hassett is a key member of President Trump's economic team and will continue working to advance President Trump's economic policies," the official said on condition of anonymity to discuss the matter.

Zervos, reached by phone after Semafor's story published, declined to comment.

While Hassett denies he is departing the White House, several other Trump administration officials have stepped down since Democrats' staggering wins in the Nov. 3 midterm, flipping Congress blue and overhauling President Donald Trump's plans for the rest of his term.

White House chief of staff Susie Wiles left her role Friday and was succeeded by Stephen Miller. Multiple Department of Justice officials have also left since Election Day, including Harmeet Dhillon, a leader in the DOJ's civil rights division, and John Eisenberg, who headed the national security division.

#2818. After Jensen Huang’s exit, what top financial advisors are doing with Nvidia: Exclusive CNBC poll

Shares of Nvidia continued to slide after the market open on Friday after declining 4% on Thursday following news of co-founder Jensen Huang's resignation as chief executive, amid reports about his personal relationships and national security concerns. The news has raised questions for investors about what comes next for a company at the center of the artificial intelligence boom.

To gauge how wealth managers are responding, CNBC polled members of its Financial Advisor 100 and Financial Advisor Council. Thirty of the 122 firms and professionals contacted responded to the online, multiple-choice flash poll, conducted between 11 a.m. and 5 p.m. EST on Nov. 12. The poll is a snapshot of respondents' views, not a measure of all financial advisors.

Most respondents, 19, said they are holding their Nvidia positions steady. Another four said they are increasing their stake, while seven said they do not own the stock. None said they were selling at the time of the poll.

"Most of our clients are in a position to hold; the reason is because it makes up a smaller percentage of an overall diversified portfolio," Winnie Sun, managing director and founding partner of Irvine, California-based Sun Group Wealth Partners, said in an interview. Sun is a member of the CNBC Financial Advisor Council.

Read coverage of CNBC's 2026 Financial Advisor 100 list, including stories featuring ranked firms and their advisors:

In open responses, several advisors wrote that they are taking a "wait-and-see" approach before changing their investment strategy with Nvidia and are telling their clients to do the same.

"Don't react to short-term headlines," wrote certified financial planner Peyton Jamison, president of Jamison Private Wealth Management in Alpharetta, Georgia. "The company's valuation is extremely attractive, and the focus should remain on its underlying fundamentals and long-term investment potential." His firm is ranked No. 78 on the 2026 CNBC Financial Advisor 100 list.

Asked what factors could change their approach to Nvidia, 13 respondents cited the outlook for AI demand, 11 pointed to valuation, and 10 mentioned company leadership or governance. Some said the decision depends on the client, with eight citing risk tolerance and seven mentioning portfolio concentration. Seven said they do not expect to change their approach.

Nvidia is scheduled to report its third-quarter fiscal 2027 financial results Tuesday, after the market close. In poll remarks, advisors said the company's fiscal third-quarter earnings will help determine their course of action.

Advisors said that for investors, what to do may depend on how much Nvidia stock they already own, when they expect to need the money and how comfortable they are with volatility.

"If this is a company that's going to keep you up at night and you just feel really uncomfortable with these unknown risks, then there was always the option to sell and move that capital to another stock or another portfolio," Sun said.

Nvidia exposure can also come through mutual funds and ETFs. Nvidia's large market value makes it a significant holding in many broad-market funds, including funds that track the S&P 500, as well as target-date funds in retirement-plan options.

Investors can check their Nvidia exposure by reviewing the fact sheets for the funds in their 401(k) or brokerage accounts, which typically list a fund's largest holdings. A stock's decline does not translate one-for-one into a fund's decline.

However, investors with dedicated technology, semiconductor or growth funds may have more Nvidia exposure than they realize, advisors say.

"Nvidia is a big part of virtually everyone's portfolio whether they take an active or passive index approach," wrote Andy Pratt, a partner and director of investment strategy at Burney Wealth Management in Reston, Virginia, which is ranked No. 34 on the 2026 CNBC Financial Advisor 100 list.

He said he advises clients not to overreact to short-term news and to let the dust settle before making decisions.

— CNBC's Stephanie Dhue contributed reporting to this article.

#2817. Lutnick faces questions over Nvidia spy scandal. His sons' firm has deep ties to the chipmaker

In late September, Cantor Fitzgerald hosted Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang and other top executives from the chipmaker for investor meetings in New York, according to a Cantor research report reviewed by CNBC.

The Wall Street firm liked what it heard.

"We continue to view NVDA as the best risk/reward in our coverage," Cantor analyst C.J. Muse and his colleagues wrote in a research report dated Oct. 3 that maintained a $350 price target and overweight rating on the stock.

Yet the firm, formerly run by Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick and now led by his sons, Brandon and Kyle, reported a nearly 50% decline in the number of Nvidia shares it held between September 2025 and March 2026, according to a CNBC analysis of securities filings that excludes options. Nvidia had been one of the largest stock positions reported in Cantor's filings.

The decline coincided with White House and U.S. intelligence officials pressing Lutnick to crack down on Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company suspected of funneling Nvidia's advanced artificial intelligence chips to China. Lutnick repeatedly resisted those efforts, Axios reported Nov. 11.

By the time Cantor hosted Huang in New York, Lutnick had also received a classified intelligence warning, according to a Nov. 10 Axios report, about Huang's ties to a suspected Chinese spy and had appeared alongside the CEO at a G20 technology summit in North Carolina.

Even as Cantor pared back its Nvidia stake, it continued doing business across the chipmaker's AI empire. A CNBC review of securities filings, corporate announcements and Wall Street research found the firm has helped arrange billions of dollars in financing for Nvidia's business partners while maintaining close ties to the chipmaker's leadership.

Those ties have drawn fresh scrutiny following Huang's resignation Thursday and Axios' reporting that Lutnick repeatedly resisted a crackdown on companies suspected of smuggling Nvidia chips to China, despite President Donald Trump authorizing new export restrictions on those companies for a third time on Nov. 5.

An ethics expert said the relationship between Lutnick and his former firm's investments may raise questions, given what the secretary knew about Nvidia.

Richard Painter, the former chief White House ethics lawyer for President George W. Bush and now a corporate law professor at the University of Minnesota, said that while "there's no proof of" Lutnick sharing nonpublic information with Cantor Fitzgerald, "the appearance doesn't look great."

"His conversations with his sons better be about publicly available information," Painter told CNBC.

There is no evidence that Lutnick's sons had advance knowledge of the intelligence concerns, that changes in Cantor's reported holdings were tied to any warnings or that the firm's business interests influenced their father's decisions.

"Cantor has to date from time to time had only immaterial stand-alone proprietary positions in Nvidia, unlike the positions reflected in 13F filings which were entered into in the ordinary course of business in connection with client swap transactions, that are common in institutional markets," a Cantor spokesperson told CNBC.

Cantor declined to answer CNBC's other questions. The Commerce Department, the White House, Howard Lutnick, Brandon Lutnick and Kyle Lutnick did not respond to CNBC's requests for comment or detailed questions about the findings.

As scrutiny of Huang intensified, Cantor Chairman Brandon Lutnick was publicly praising the chipmaker's leadership. "Nvidia is one of the great companies on the planet, and they have an amazing leadership team," Brandon Lutnick told Fox Business on Wednesday.

Trump is standing by Howard Lutnick. A White House official told Reuters on Thursday that the president maintains "complete confidence" in the commerce secretary.

But cracks in the administration's unity emerged Friday, when Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent told CNBC's "Squawk Box" that the departments of Treasury, Defense and State and the Office of the U.S. Trade Representative all "were in favor of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months."

Bessent pointed to Commerce as the department responsible for imposing the entity list, which identifies restricted businesses and other groups, adding, "You will have to ask them about their internal processes."

Nvidia was Cantor's second-largest reported stock position before Howard Lutnick left Wall Street to become commerce secretary in February 2025, behind only bitcoin investor MicroStrategy, now known as Strategy. At the end of 2024, the firm disclosed more than 3.5 million Nvidia shares worth $479 million, according to its quarterly securities filing.

That share count held steady through September 2025, but its value climbed 36% to $652 million, according to Cantor's quarterly securities filing, as the Trump administration moved over the summer to ease restrictions on Nvidia's H20 chip sales to China.

Over the following six months, however, the number of Nvidia shares Cantor reported holding fell by 49%, to 3 million in December and 1.78 million by the end of March 2026, with their value dropping from $652 million to $310 million, according to a CNBC analysis of securities filings.

Pressure on Howard Lutnick was also mounting on Capitol Hill. Sens. Jim Banks, R-Ind., and Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., in March urged the secretary to suspend or reconsider Nvidia's export licenses after three people linked to Nvidia customer Super Micro Computer were indicted on charges of conspiring to smuggle AI chips to China.

"The Trump administration, I think, leaks like a sieve when it comes to nonpublic government information getting to various hedge fund guys," Painter said, though he stressed there is no evidence Howard Lutnick had shared such information with his sons.

The filings do not establish why Cantor's reported share count fell or whether its overall financial exposure to Nvidia declined. Nvidia’s stock had more than doubled from its April 2025 lows by late October, before retreating in the following months. The stock fell about 6.5% between the end of September and the end of March.

Cantor's reported Nvidia holdings remained near that lower level through June 30, the latest quarter covered by public disclosures, at 1.81 million shares. Its next quarterly filing is due Monday.

Cantor has not publicly explained the decline.

Most of those shares were attributed to CF Secured, a Cantor brokerage subsidiary that uses stocks and derivatives to hedge customer transactions, according to its audited financial statements. Cantor also disclosed substantial Nvidia options positions during the time period CNBC examined, overwhelmingly skewed toward put options, which can protect against declines in the stock price.

As concerns about Huang escalated inside the Trump administration in May, according to the Nov. 11 Axios report, Cantor was helping finance the expansion of one of Nvidia's newest business partners, securities filings show.

On May 7, Nvidia and Australia-based cloud computing provider IREN announced a five-year, $3.4 billion cloud computing contract and a strategic partnership giving the chipmaker the right to invest up to $2.1 billion in IREN.

"That's not a passive financial investment. Nvidia is a partner who wins as we deliver," IREN co-CEO Daniel Roberts told investors on the company's quarterly earnings call that day.

Five days later, as Trump headed to China aboard Air Force One, Secretary of State Marco Rubio warned him about Huang's relationship with a suspected Chinese spy, The New York Times reported Tuesday. Huang, who had not initially been invited on the trip, joined the president's delegation during a refueling stop in Alaska.

Howard Lutnick had already been sent a highly classified intelligence memo detailing concerns about Huang's relationship, according to the Nov. 10 Axios report.

On May 12, the day Trump departed for China, IREN announced the pricing of a convertible bond offering that ultimately raised $3 billion when it closed two days later. Cantor was one of seven bookrunners on the deal, alongside JPMorgan Chase and Goldman Sachs.

On the day the offering closed, May 14, Lutnick's Commerce Department quietly cleared roughly 10 Chinese companies, including Alibaba and ByteDance, to buy Nvidia's advanced H200 chips, though some of those approvals predated the intelligence memo.

It is unclear when IREN hired Cantor for the offering or how much the firm earned from the deal. Cantor declined to answer CNBC's questions about its work with IREN.

Cantor reported a substantial position in IREN's stock.

By the end of June, Cantor reported $115 million in IREN shares and $362 million in Nvidia shares. Together, the two companies accounted for more than 9% of the value of Cantor's reported $5.2 billion in securities holdings, excluding options, according to CNBC's analysis of the filing.

IREN was not the only company in Nvidia's orbit that turned to Cantor for financing.

In October 2025, Cantor advised AI infrastructure developer Lancium on a $600 million financing deal to support its data center campus in Abilene, Texas, the first site of Stargate, the massive AI infrastructure project backed by OpenAI, SoftBank and Oracle.

Newmark, a commercial real estate firm controlled by Cantor, also helped finance the Abilene campus, arranging a $7.1 billion construction loan in May 2025.

Ten months later, Nvidia agreed to invest $2 billion for a roughly 20% stake in Lancium, with up to $1 billion more tied to future milestones, The Information reported in August. Lancium, which is also backed by Blackstone, confirmed an investment and a technology partnership on Aug. 24 without disclosing financial terms.

It's unclear whether Cantor continued advising Lancium after the financing deal or played a role in Nvidia's investment. Neither Cantor nor Lancium responded to questions about the investment.

Cantor's reported ties also extend to Nvidia's supply chain.

In May 2025, days after Howard Lutnick announced the transfer of his ownership stake in Cantor to his children via trusts, Semafor reported that bankers at the firm had informally advised executives at Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co., which makes Nvidia's most advanced chips, on navigating Washington. Cantor called Semafor's report "baseless and false."

TSMC received billions of dollars in federal subsidies created during the Biden administration and administered by the Commerce Department to fund its U.S. manufacturing expansion.

Democratic senators called for an ethics investigation in December into whether the Lutnick family stood to benefit from Howard Lutnick's push to expand AI data centers.

And on Wednesday, Sens. Warren and Chris Coons, D-Del., demanded that Howard Lutnick, Rubio and White House chief of staff Stephen Miller preserve records on Nvidia's chip exports to China and intelligence warnings about Huang.

The senators questioned whether "prudent defensive counter-intelligence action was taken or national security recommendations were overruled."

"The place where I think the Trump administration has most failed here is in being persuaded by Jensen Huang and others to license the export of some advanced chips," Coons said Friday on CNBC's "Squawk Box," accusing the administration of turning a blind eye to the black market for more advanced chips.

#2816. Chiefs-Falcons Madden Simulation Week 10: On the Road Again

On Sunday, the (6-2) Kansas City Chiefs are on the road again taking on the (3-6) Atlanta Falcons. Let's see how the game plays out in Madden NFL 27.

Note: Madden simulation is 7-1 this season, predicting the outcome.

If you want to watch the simulation without spoilers, start this video — or if you can't see it, click here.

The Falcons' opening drive gained just seven yards before they were forced to punt on their first possession.

The Chiefs' ground-and-pound attack erupted on their first drive, highlighted by a 28-yard run from Kenneth Walker. Walker rushed for 36 yards on the drive, while backup running back Emmett Johnson added 13 yards on a third-and-10 run to move the chains.

The drive would end with Harrison Butker drilling a 44-yard field goal to open the scoring.

End of first quarter: Chiefs 3, Falcons 0

The Falcons opened the quarter at the Chiefs' 19-yard line. Drake London caught a 15-yard pass to reach the four-yard line. From the pistol formation, Michael Penix Jr. handed the ball to Bijan Robinson, who ran to the right, broke a tackle, and found the end zone for a touchdown.

The Chiefs failed to reach the first-down marker on their next possession and were forced to punt the ball back to Atlanta.

A 14-yard pick-and-roll pass to Jahan Dotson moved the ball to the Chiefs' 14-yard line. Bijan Robinson finished the drive by catching a pass from Penix Jr. and scoring his second touchdown of the game.

The Chiefs found themselves at midfield with a minute remaining in the quarter. On third-and-three, Rashee Rice ran a comeback route and picked up the first down to keep the drive alive. Mahomes then connected with newly acquired Josh Downs for a 12-yard gain and another first down.

The Chiefs managed to put points on the board before halftime as Butker drilled his second field goal of the game, this time from 44 yards out, cutting the Falcons' lead to eight.

End of second quarter: Falcons 14, Chiefs 6

Josh Downs hauled in an 18-yard pass on a deep out route, going up to make the catch while keeping both feet in bounds. The reception moved the Chiefs to the Falcons' 25-yard line.

Two plays later, Xavier Watts intercepted Mahomes on a high throw intended for Rashee Rice running a crossing route.

The Falcons turned to their running game to drain the clock, alternating between Bijan Robinson and Brian Robinson.

Another 11-yard run from Bijan Robinson moved Atlanta to the Chiefs' 11-yard line. Brina Robinson followed it up with a 10-yard run to carry the Falcons into the fourth quarter.

End of third quarter: Falcons 14, Chiefs 6

A toss to Bijan Robinson went untouched into the end zone for his third touchdown of the game, extending Atlanta's lead.

The Chiefs found themselves at their own 48-yard line facing a third-and-five. From the shotgun, Mahomes delivered a strike on a fly route to Jalen Royals, who hauled in the pass for a touchdown to bring the game back within one score.

The Falcons reached midfield on their next possession but were stopped at the Chiefs' 48-yard line and forced to punt.

Mahomes connected with Travis Kelce for 16 yards to move the Chiefs to their own 48-yard line.

Downs followed with a 26-yard reception on an out route.

Royals then hauled in a 17-yard pass on a deep crossing route to move the Chiefs to the nine-yard line.

Mahomes went back to the player who helped get the drive started. Kelce was targeted inside the red zone and came down with the touchdown to bring Kansas City closer.

The Chiefs attempted a two-point conversion to tie the game, but the Falcons stopped a screen pass intended for Johnson.

Kansas City's defense came up with a three-and-out on the Falcons' next possession, giving the Chiefs another opportunity. Atlanta was forced to punt, leaving Kansas City with one timeout and eight seconds remaining before the two-minute warning.

The Chiefs crossed midfield after Mahomes connected with Kelce for a 14-yard gain. However, the next play went backward as Mahomes was sacked for an eight-yard loss.

The sack put Kansas City in a fourth-and-13 situation with 15 seconds remaining.

The Chiefs' final attempt fell incomplete as Mahomes tried to make a throw while being taken down by the pass rush. Kansas City turned the ball over on downs, ending its comeback attempt.

Patrick Mahomes: 29 for 39 passing, 293 yards, 2 touchdowns, 1 interception, 1 rushing attempt, 7 yards

Kenneth Walker: 4 rushing attempts, 40 yards

Travis Kelce: 7 receptions, 74 yards, 1 touchdown.

Jalen Royals: 6 receptions, 82 yards. 1 touchdown

Harrison Butker: 2 for 2, long of 44 yards.

Michael Penix Jr.: 16 for 22 passing, 171 yards, 1 touchdown, 4 rushing attempts, 16 yards.

#2815. Lakers' Austin Reaves considered day-to-day with heel injury

On the day the Lakers hosted their annual Thanksgiving Feast event to kick off their "Season of Giving" at the team's practice facility, they were able to give some thanks for dodging serious injuries.

Austin Reaves didn't have to get further imaging on his injured left heel, coach JJ Redick said after practice Friday, and the star guard will be listed as day-to-day for Sunday's game at Phoenix.

Redick also said that All-Star guard Luka Doncic was "fine" after injuring his ankle in a win over the Milwaukee Bucks on Wednesday night, that a concussion was ruled out for guard Collin Sexton after he received stitches to his forehead following a collision with Milwaukee's Ryan Rollins, and that guard Quentin Grimes worked out in the stay-ready game against coaches after missing two games because of a finger injury.

Reaves left Wednesday's win in the third quarter and didn't return. His X-rays after the game were negative and by Thursday morning, when the team had returned to L.A. after winning the first three of the four-game trip, Reaves felt well enough that he and team personnel opted against getting more imaging.

Redick said Reaves has a bruise on his heel and described it as "minor."

Read more: 'It's forever': Lakers rookie Cameron Carr cherishing first NBA points

Doncic, who leads the NBA in scoring (38.6 points) and is ninth in assists (7.6), is doing OK after he injured his right ankle in the fourth quarter Wednesday.

"The expectation would be he's available," Redick said.

Sexton had to get stitches above his right eye after the collision with 6:51 left in the fourth quarter Wednesday.

Redick said Sexton also will be day-to-day.

Lakers guard Collin Sexton, preparing to defend Hawks guard Kingston Flemings, is listed as day-to-day after getting stitches above his right eye following a collision during a win over the Bucks. (Mike Stewart / Associated Press)

Grimes, who missed two games because of a sprained finger on his right hand, participated in the live portion of the stay-ready game Wednesday and expects to play Sunday. Grimes wore a wrap on his right pinky finger while speaking with reporters.

"I had a really good day just playing with a little tape over it," Grimes said. "Just seeing how it felt the first time, but it felt really good. We got a lot of wins out there in them games, at least. But it felt really good out there."

The Lakers will have three days off before they play again Sunday.

Even though the Lakers have the best record in the NBA (11-2), are the top-scoring team (122.2 points) in the league and rank first in offensive rating (121.6), Redick wanted to let his group know there is room for improvement.

During the film session Friday, he told his players they "were 17th in defense" rating at 113.6. And the Lakers are 27th in rebounding.

Redick said he and his staff talked about "20 things" that they can clean up.

"We filtered through our offensive stuff based on our identities of who we want to be offensively: organized, unselfish and advantage-based," Redick said. "Then we went through the stats on that.

"Then we went to our defense and our three identities: organized, physical as hell and relentless. And we went through the stats on those things. So, it's a very clean thing. We've done that for the last 2½ seasons. It's just everything that we present to the team lines up with who we're trying to be."

Redick got his message through to his players.

"Being first in the West, with the stuff that they showed us, you would think we were almost last, you know?" forward Ziaire Williams said. "But it's gotta keep us honest. But you know, it's good to know that there's still room for growth as good as we're doing. So yeah, it was great, it was great. So yeah, hopefully we'll clean that up going into the next game against Phoenix."

#2813. AI risks are the biggest threat to the financial system, Fed survey finds

Threats from artificial intelligence topped the list for greatest risks to the financial system, far exceeding any other dangers, according to respondents who participated in a Federal Reserve survey released Friday.

The central bank's twice-yearly look at a variety of dangers saw a surge in respondents saying that the biggest danger comes from AI along several fronts including debt, stock market valuations and security risks.

"Respondents noted three distinct areas of concern: (1) elevated equity valuations and the possibility that lower-than-expected earnings could result in lower valuations and a market correction; (2) cybersecurity risk, stemming from either a malicious attack by sophisticated state actors or damage caused by frontier AI models themselves; and (3) risks associated with complex forms of AI-related financing," the report stated.

The report comes with Wall Street on edge over high-flying AI hyperscalers, while communities are on alert for the rapid spread of data centers used to power the technology. Some 56% of voters in this month's midterm elections said data centers were of crucial or major importance in their choices, according to exit polling.

Respondents to the Fed survey released Friday include a variety of market participants, economists, analysts and institutional sources. The report covers the period up to Oct. 28, just a few days shy of the election.

Of the group, 87% cited AI as a "salient risk" to the system. That was up from 50% in the survey during the spring of this year and well ahead of the next most-cited danger, a risk asset correction, which was mentioned by 52% of those surveyed.

Other leading risks were higher long-term rates (48%), cyberattacks (43%) and fiscal debt sustainability (43%), the latter coming with the U.S. debt over $40 trillion and the budget deficit near $2 trillion.

The report otherwise found the system fairly stable, though it mentioned that hedge fund leverage is at its highest since 2013 and concentrated on a relatively small group.

#2812. Nvidia stock suffers longest weekly losing streak since 2012 as market value sinks by $1.7 trillion

Nvidia investors turned bearish before this week's Jensen Huang saga. But the selling on Wall Street accelerated in the past few days, sending the stock to its sixth straight weekly drop, the longest losing streak since 2012.

The chip giant, which spent most of the year as the world's most valuable company, has lost 30% of its value during the losing streak. That's a drop of roughly $1.7 trillion in market cap and it pushed the company below Apple.

Huang, who co-founded Nvidia and led it for 33 years, resigned on Thursday after two separate extramarital affairs — one with an alleged Chinese spy and the other with an employee — were made public. The board named finance chief Colette Kress as interim CEO while it conducts a search for a full-time successor.

Nvidia shares fell 13% this week, the most since April 2025. News of the first reported affair hit the front pages over the weekend, when Huang sent a letter to employees apologizing for being "unfaithful to my wife." The board convened late Wednesday after The New York Times reported on the other affair, this one with the company's vice president of health care.

By early Thursday morning, Huang was out, capping a stunning fall for the Nvidia co-founder, who had emerged as one of President Donald Trump's top allies in the corporate world.

"With the unfortunate departure of CEO Jensen Huang we expect a challenging transition for NVIDIA over the next few months," Gil Luria, an analyst at D.A. Davidson, wrote in a report on Thursday. Luria lowered his price target to $200 from $300, but maintained his buy rating. "We believe the market has appropriately adjusted for this transition," he wrote.

Last week, prior to Huang's departure, Nvidia shares dropped about 9%. Much of the decline came after the midterm elections, which resulted in sweeping victories for Democrats and the passage of dozens of ballot measures that could slow the artificial intelligence buildout.

In the weeks before the election, Nvidia's stock was sliding alongside the rest of the AI trade on concerns that rising interest rates would punish debt-heavy companies. While Nvidia is wildly profitable, big customers like neoclouds are reliant on the debt markets.

Heading into next week, the pressure is on Kress. Nvidia is scheduled to report quarterly results on Tuesday. Analysts are projecting 91% year-over-year revenue growth to $108.9 billion, according to LSEG. That's up 18-fold from the same quarter in 2022, just before OpenAI launched ChatGPT and kicked off the generative AI era.

Analysts at Oppenheimer wrote in a report on Thursday that, even though they see the company reporting solid earnings, "we expect fundamentals to take a backseat near term."

"The departure of Mr. Huang could, in our view, translate to a lingering share overhang," they wrote.

#2809. Lakers provide Austin Reaves injury update. Will star guard miss games?

Los Angeles Lakers coach JJ Redick provided an injury update on shooting guard Austin Reaves.

Redick stated during his Friday, Nov. 13 media availability that Reaves suffered a bruised heel and is considered day-to-day. The coach added that Reaves and the team's medical staff opted against having imaging done on the injury.

Reaves had landed awkwardly on his left heel after missing a transition dunk attempt in the middle of the third quarter of the Lakers' 123-112 victory over the Milwaukee Bucks on Nov. 11.

After feeling some discomfort, he limped back to the locker room. The initial X-rays taken at the arena were negative, and Redick ruled out any potential Achilles tendon damage.

Reaves was one of several injury scares suffered during the game against the Bucks.

Luka Dončić avoided a severe injury after turning his right ankle in the same game. Collin Sexton also needed stitches above his right eye after bumping heads with Ryan Rollins of the Bucks.

Dončić: Lakers star 'feeling good' after injury scare

Reaves has played in 12 of 13 game for the Lakers this season, starting in all 12 of those contests. He is averaging 24.7 points, 3.3 rebound, 4.7 assists and 1.2 steals per game, while shooting 51.3% from the field and 40.9% from beyond the arc.

Reaves and the Lakers will have more time to rest before traveling to Phoenix to take on the Suns at 4 p.m. ET (2 p.m. local) on Sunday, Nov. 15.
