#2808. SpaceX launches Starship in key test flight of massive rocket

In a key test flight on Friday, SpaceX tried but failed to recover both stages of its massive Starship rocket.

The rocket lifted off from the company's South Texas launch facility at 4:47 p.m. local time, or 5:47 p.m. ET. SpaceX had to abort its plans to catch and reuse the booster, instead diverting it to splash into the Gulf of Mexico, where it burst into flames.

Test Flight 15, the most ambitious Starship mission to date, was the first time Elon Musk's company attempted to catch both the booster and upper stage, or spacecraft portion, of the massive rocket.

Earlier on Friday, SpaceX obtained authorization from the Federal Aviation Administration. Hours later, the FAA issued a nationwide ground stop after reporting communication problems affecting "multiple" air traffic control centers. The SpaceX license wasn't effected by the flight stoppage, a spokesperson for the FAA told CNBC.

The upper stage of the Starship continued its trajectory into orbit after the company failed to catch and salvage the booster. The rocket is expected to fly around the Earth fourteen times in total. If successful, SpaceX will land the upper stage back at its Starbase facility on Saturday.

Starship's success is critical to SpaceX and its ambitions to eventually lift cargo and people to the surface of the Moon and Mars. In the near-term, SpaceX needs to send more satellites into orbit to expands its Starlink satellite internet service, which is currently the company's profit center.

The company's constellation already includes over 11,000 active satellites in low Earth orbit. One Starship rocket has the capacity to carry up to 60 Starlink v3 satellites into orbit, the company has said, representing about 20 times the capacity of earlier Falcon 9 rockets.

In the previous Starship test flight, the company released 26 v3 satellites, and released another 26 in the Friday test flight, with the goal of "Starlink becoming 51% of the internet," SpaceX said in a post on X.

When it comes to launches, "SpaceX is likely a full decade ahead of peers on this front," William Blair analysts wrote in a note out on Friday. "Even if tonight's catch is unsuccessful, a delay by a few months is not that consequential."

Meanwhile, SpaceX is facing fresh criticism over restrictions imposed on its Starshield satellite internet service in Ukraine. Starshield is built on Starlink technology and is the military-tier of SpaceX's satellite internet service.

Earlier this week, Ukraine President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said his country's military forces had experienced limits on Starshield without warning again, harming their ability to defend against Russia's ongoing aerial attacks. Similar limits on Starshield in Ukraine were temporarily imposed last month.

Some U.S. senators have condemned the restrictions, and urged Congress to investigate SpaceX.

#2807. Microsoft apologizes for massive outage that took down Outlook and other 365 services

Microsoft issued an apology Friday for a wide-ranging outage that shut down Outlook and other Microsoft 365 products, as the company worked to restore service nearly 24 hours after connectivity problems began.

"We are deeply sorry for the disruption this incident has caused our customers and partners. We know the trust they place in our services and take that responsibility extremely seriously," a Microsoft spokesperson said Friday. "We are seeing broad recovery and stability across our services."

In a statement, Microsoft said the connectivity disruption stemmed from "an issue during an earlier network capacity upgrade." The software giant said the underlying cause of the outage had been addressed and that they were still working to restore services to some customers.

Engineers scrambled to solve connectivity problems that began shortly before 1 p.m. ET on Thursday.

Email and calendar service Outlook, along with multiple Microsoft 365 services such as Sharepoint and Copilot, were largely down during the morning hours in the U.S. on Friday and throughout the Asian and European business days. Numerous external services that connect with Microsoft products were also hit. Users began to regain access to email and other services Friday morning.

In an update around 10:30 a.m. ET, Microsoft noted "encouraging recovery progress" and said that "service availability and email delivery performance [was] improving across a growing portion of affected infrastructure" as Outlook and other Microsoft 365 tools began to come back online.

In an earlier update Friday morning, Microsoft said that email queues were "approaching normal levels," but that intermittent issues were expected as recovery efforts continue. It had previously given updates by region, with Asia Pacific services among the first to start normalizing.

Engineering teams have been taking affected systems offline for fixes and bringing them back online gradually stages. They are prioritizing efforts to preserve emails delayed during the recovery process and reduce email queues and backlogs, extending the timeout window, whereby delayed emails continue to attempt delivery, to 48 hours to help protect messages.

Microsoft says it has not detected any malicious activity causing the outage, which it initially blamed on a traffic surge in part of its infrastructure and issues with a component responsible for processing server health. The company said it would issue a full report once its assessment of the outage was complete.

The company said in its initial updates on Thursday that the connectivity problems related to infrastructure "elevated load across multiple regions," caused by a "number of servers being unexpectedly unavailable; straining the remaining infrastructure." Some servers were failing automated health checks, it said.

Microsoft shares traded largely flat Friday.

Governments and businesses across the U.S., Asia-Pacific and Europe said they were monitoring the situation, which was causing widespread disruption.

The U.K.'s National Health Service said the outage was affecting some of its services but that patients should attend appointments as normal. Calls "may be subject to long waits" as a result of disruption to the organization, a hospital group serving South West London and the English county of Surrey said on X.

Earnings for Swiss luxury goods giant Richemont were slightly delayed on Friday due to the outage. Electric truck maker Workhorse Group, which was expected to report third-quarter results on Nov. 12, rescheduled the release to Nov. 16, citing business disruptions caused by the global Microsoft outage.

U.S. Rep. Josh Gottheimer, D-N.J., told CNBC's "The Exchange" that he asked for a briefing on the outage. Gottheimer worked at Microsoft prior to serving in Congress.

"Is there something that went on that's nefarious here, or is this just something else? These outages occur in a lot of the companies, but the size of it and the length of it and the timing of it," Gottheimer said.

Air India said it had "temporarily rerouted" emails so it could continue to receive customer messages, adding that its flight operations were not affected.

Australian Home Affairs and Cyber Security Minister Tony Burke said in a press conference that Microsoft "believe they are dealing in some ways similar to what we saw with respect to CrowdStrike where there is an update that has gone awry," Reuters reported.

A fault with an update issued by cybersecurity company CrowdStrike caused one of the world's biggest IT system outages in 2024, hitting airlines, banks and more.

Microsoft reported in 2025 that Copilot, its collection of AI assistants and tools, had more than 100 million monthly active users.

The incident highlights a business "resilience and dependency issue that has been decades in the making," James Owen, managing partner of Digital Risks at consultancy Control Risks, said by phone.

"We have become, organizationally in the private sector and more broadly, increasingly dependent on a smaller segment of technology and infrastructure as critical to daily life and business activities."

"Organizations are becoming more aware through a geopolitical lens about what technologies they adopt, whether their services are provided by firms in the U.S., or China, or elsewhere, but this shows the importance of proper recovery planning in the case of an IT outage. That could be manual backups, protocols that do not require internet connection, investing in diverse suppliers and crisis management planning rehearsals," he said.

Amazon Web Services and Google users also reported outages on the site on Thursday.

Amazon wrote on X that services were "operating normally" and an "unrelated disruption elsewhere on the internet is prompting some inaccurate news reports." The company referred users to its health dashboard for "accurate data."

At 4 p.m. ET Thursday, Microsoft said it had "validated an alternative service health check method" that brought some affected servers back online.

"We're now applying this change more broadly," Microsoft wrote. "We're also preparing automated recovery systems to continually detect and restart unresponsive servers, to maintain service stability while this change progresses."

#2806. Top Wall Street analysts recommend these 3 dividend stocks for higher returns

Dividend-paying stocks can enhance portfolio returns by providing a steady income stream to investors and the potential for capital appreciation. Several investors seek exposure to dividend stocks in a volatile backdrop to ensure stable income.

In this regard, recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, backed by strong financials and cash flows to support consistent dividend payments.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Energy Transfer (ET) is a diversified midstream energy company with about 140,000 miles of pipelines and associated energy infrastructure in 44 states. The company made a quarterly cash distribution of 34 cents per common unit for the second quarter of 2026. At an annualized payment of $1.36 per common unit, ET offers a yield of 6.3%.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Jeremy Tonet reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer stock and slightly raised his price target to $25 from $24. The analyst noted that the company delivered a strong Q2 beat across all key metrics.

Tonet highlighted that Energy Transfer raised its 2026 adjusted EBITDA guidance to $18.8 billion-$19.1 billion from $18.2 billion-$18.6 billion, reflecting robust base results and cost optimization. The 5-star analyst added that the company "tightened" its capex to $5.6 billion–$5.9 billion and expects capex above $5 billion annually through 2029, given solid opportunities.

About the Hugh Brinson pipeline, Tonet noted Phase 1 (1.5 bcfd) reaching full capacity by September 1 and Phase II capacity (0.7 bcfd) going online by Q1 2027. The analyst also noted completion of a 14-mile Hugh Brinson lateral in Abilene, Texas, and final stages of talks for an additional 250 mmcfd of Oklahoma power plant demand.

"All in, we see ET well positioned to continue capitalizing on organic growth opportunities across the value chain," said Tonet.

Tonet ranks No. 922 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 9%. See Energy Transfer Financials on TipRanks.

This week's second dividend pick is independent oil and natural gas company Permian Resources (PR). The company declared a base dividend of $0.16 per share for the third quarter, payable on September 30, 2026. At an annualized dividend of $0.64 per share, PR offers a yield of about 2.7%.

Recently, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Permian Resources stock and increased his price target to $27 from $22, citing an improved outlook on oil production growth and operational efficiencies.

The 5-star analyst noted that while PR stock has outperformed its peers so far in 2026, he sees further upside. Mehta is confident about the company's operational improvements and ability to deliver incremental free cash flow per share through the strategic ground game program. He added that the program has completed about $1.05 billion in bolt-on deals this year (as of August 5).

"We see the continued success of PR's ground game as constructive toward future inventory replenishment and increasing working interest over time," said Mehta.

The analyst also emphasized Permian Resources' solid track record of generating growth in free cash flow (FCF) per share. Mehta expects the company's free cash flow per share to grow at a 20% CAGR (compound annual growth rate) from 2025–2028.

Mehta ranks No. 401 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.8%. See Permian Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

Moving on to Sempra Energy (SRE), a utility holding company. Earlier this month, the company declared a quarterly dividend of $0.6575 per share, payable on October 15. At an annualized dividend of $2.63 per share, SRE stock offers a yield of about 3.1%.

Recently, Jefferies analyst Julien Dumoulin-Smith upgraded SRE stock to Buy from Hold but slightly lowered the price target to $97 from $101. The 5-star analyst noted that Sempra stock is trading at a price/earnings multiple that is 14% below its electric peers due to concerns about Texas transmission capital expenditure and the failure of California legislation.

Smith noted that the derating of weaker California peers PG&E (PCG) and Edison International (EIX) is weighing on the sum-of-the-parts (SOTP) valuation but is becoming less relevant given their materially higher fire risk compared to Sempra Energy.

Furthermore, Smith expects Sempra Energy's Texas capex plan to stay in place despite the opposition to transmission and data centers, though delays to 765-kV projects are likely. While a full recovery for SRE stock will need a clearer outcome from the Texas legislative session, Smith believes that the stock may already be an early opportunity for patient investors.

"We are early on the upgrade but see limited downside at current levels as the market is pricing in little transmission upside," said Smith.

Smith ranks No. 945 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.2%. See Sempra ETF Exposure on TipRanks.

#2805. 3-time world champion Ilia Malinin leads Skate America after a 96.74-point short program

Ilia Malinin began his four-year road to Olympic redemption with a solid short program at Skate America at Angel of the Wings Arena in Everett, Washington, on Friday night.

The three-time world champion, who was heavily favored to win gold at the Milan Games in February only to miss out on an individual medal altogether, scored 96.74 points in his Grand Prix season debut. That was enough for Malinin to easily beat out Sota Yamamoto of Japan, who was second with 91.29, and surprising Donovan Carrillo of Mexico, who was third with 86.49.

The pairs short program was later Friday night with Alisa Efimova and Misha Mitrofanov leading the American contingent.

The 21-year-old Malinin, going for his fourth Skate America title, designed his short program as a tribute to those from the close-knit figure skating community who were killed in January 2025, when their American Airlines flight collided with a military helicopter and fell into the icy Potomac River. Many were young skaters from the same club where Malinin trains in Virginia.

"Honestly I think coming into the first competition, my goal was to just feel out the program, how I felt on the ice," said Malinin, who was instrumental in the U.S. defending its team gold medal at the Olympics. "I feel as the season goes on, the short program is going to be really easy to skate, because I feel so much energy from the crowd."

Malinin wasn't perfect in his short program, set to "Cantata Memoria: For the Children" by British composer Sir Karl Jenkins. After his opening quad flip and an effortless triple axel, the self-styled "Quad God" put his hand down on a quad lutz-double toe loop.

But the performance value of the program seemed to make up for the mistake, and the entirety of choreographer Shae-Lynn Bourne's work helped to earn Malinin a big ovation from the crowd.

"The way I see it this year, there's not much on the line. I just wanted to go full-out, try my best, see how much I could push my level of skating," said Malinin, who has called this year's short program one of the hardest he has ever attempted.

The fourth of six Grand Prix events, Skate America continues Saturday with the women's short program and rhythm dance in the afternoon, while the men's and pairs free skates in the evening will determine the first medals of the weekend.

Olympic champion Alysa Liu withdrew from Skate America while taking a break from the sport, and U.S. champion Amber Glenn did likewise as she competes on "Dancing with the Stars." That leaves a young trio of American women — Sophie Joline von Felton, Logan Higase-Chen and Alexa Gasparotto — to carry the hopes of the home nation.

They'll face a field that includes Grand Prix de France winner Ami Nakai of Japan and Russian skaters Alexandra Ignatova and Adeliia Petrosian, who are allowed to compete internationally as neutral athletes for the first time since Russia's invasion of Ukraine.

Jason Brown, the 31-year-old American now a decade removed from his national championship, seemed to make time stand still with his return to Skate America on Friday night. He spun out on his opening triple axel but put together a solid short program, wiping away any lingering memories from a disastrous free skate at the U.S. championships earlier this year.

Brown scored 84.00 points, putting him in sixth place heading into Saturday night's free skate.

"It was such a long time coming and I was just so proud to be out there," Brown said. "This was a huge step for me."

#2804. Buffalo Bills safety Amani Hooker will make his debut Week 10 vs. NY Jets

It took all of three practices for Buffalo Bills head coach Joe Brady to decide that Amani Hooker, the team's new addition at safety, will play in Week 10 against the New York Jets. In meeting with members of the media, Brady revealed that Hooker "definitely is gonna play, and excited to see whatever that role kind of looks like."

The Bills and president of football operations/general manager Brandon Beane knew the time was right to take advantage of the looming trade deadline. In doing so, Buffalo has brought in someone they immediately identified as a starter for defensive coordinator Jim Leonhard's system.

Brady, ever the master press chess player, was careful not to reveal too much about their plans for Hooker. He did say that play time is yet to be determined, noting that "from a comfort level, we don't want to put him out there and do anything that is not fair to him and the team." Brady and the team's defensive coaches have been impressed with Hooker's ability to quickly assimilate within the team's defense, including the ability to "communicate in our language."

Hooker's arrival means that once again veteran safety Damar Hamlin is likely to take a step back into a reserve role. Despite that, Hamlin has embraced his new teammate, helping him get comfortable with his new role in Buffalo.

Brady was asked about Hamlin and he emphatically stated that "Damar Hamlin has an incredibly important role on this football team, and he's gonna play a big part on this football team in a lot of different areas."He did acknowledge that Hooker's arrival might prove frustrating for Hamlin, who seemed to play well the last two games, and spoke about the situation at length, saying:

"Look, regardless of (if) someone says it or not, anytime you have an opportunity to be a starter and then you trade for somebody else, it can be a natural tendency to kind of maybe get frustrated. Right? But I believe Damar, and the safeties, and the team — we have a lot of guys on this team that can put pride aside and say 'hey whatever we gotta do to win a football game'… …that's what Damar Hamlin's all about and that's what he's always been about. He just wants opportunities to compete, and he's out there competing. And if it helps the Buffalo Bills, that's what he's about. So, that's why we love him and that's why — the trade was not an indication of Damar Hamlin, and more so just an opportunity for us to improve our football team."

Though Hooker will, in fact, make his Bills debut in Week 10, it's possible we still see Hamlin in action on defense depending on the situation. He may also reclaim a significant role on special teams, where the last two games Hamlin played zero snaps with the special teams units.

#2802. Top Wall Street analysts find these 3 stocks attractive as long-term investments

Investors have been grappling with stock market volatility, weighing elevated oil prices, persistent geopolitical tensions, the Federal Reserve's rate hike and a debate about AI safety. For all that, it helps to look beyond short-term noise and focus on stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors gain clarity, as these experts conduct in-depth research to pick stocks with attractive growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Enterprise software and cloud infrastructure company Oracle (ORCL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the first quarter of fiscal 2027. The quarter benefited from solid growth in cloud infrastructure revenue.

After hosting a series of investor meetings with Ken Bond, Oracle head of investor relations following Q1 earnings, Guggenheim analyst John DiFucci reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $400. Oracle remains a Guggenheim Best Idea, he said.

"We continue to believe ORCL is a decade stock, driven by the massive (and profitable) opportunity ahead for AI training and inferencing," said DiFucci. He also cited the traditional Public Cloud business and additional AI-enabled cloud database and cloud applications for his bullish stance. DiFucci expects all the factors to drive revenue and profit acceleration over the next few years.

While Oracle delivered 850 MW of data center capacity in Q1 FY27, Bond cautioned that Infrastructure as a Service revenue in any quarter depends more on the timing of when that capacity comes online, than on the headline MW number. DiFucci expects IaaS growth to stay strong, with full-year growth expected above Q1 FY27's 120% constant currency rate. Management expects total revenue growth to accelerate sequentially throughout fiscal 2027, as the faster-growing infrastructure business makes up a larger share of the mix.

DiFucci addressed concerns regarding customer concentration, saying OpenAI's share of total remaining performance obligations has dropped from 80% last June to about 50% now, and is expected to keep falling as new contracts are signed with new and existing customers.

DiFucci ranks No. 263 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 62% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17.5%. See Oracle Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

Space systems and launch services provider Rocket Lab (RKLB) was recently initiated as a buy with an $80 price target by Raymond James analyst Brian Gesuale.

"RKLB has assembled launch, spacecraft, components, payloads and optical communications, with Neutron and Iridium completing medium-lift and applications/spectrum in 2027," said Gesuale.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that with the completion of the Iridium acquisition, Rocket Lab will become the only vertically integrated space company with positive free cash flow (FCF). The deal will add a deployed constellation valued at more than $3 billion, globally coordinated L-band spectrum and about $500 million in earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) and $300 million in free cash flow (FCF).

Gesuale highlighted RKLB's strong revenue and backlog growth and progress toward positive EBITDA and FCF in 2027/2028. Rocket Lab is moving from a heavy investment phase into monetization, with estimated revenue growth of 59% in 2026, the analyst said, noting that RKLB's backlog has more than doubled to almost $2.4 billion from $1.1 billion in Q3 2025.

Given strong growth projections, the analyst expects RKLB's gross margins to expand from about a 35% trough in 2027 to about 47% by 2030, while R&D spending is estimated to decline from ~35% of sales in 2026 to about 10% in 2028, creating substantial operating leverage as the platform matures.

Gesuale ranks No. 369 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17%. See Rocket Lab Statistics on TipRanks.

J.P. Morgan analyst Doug Anmuth recently upgraded Meta Platforms (META) to buy from hold and raised his price target to $820 from $640. Despite the year-to-date underperformance of META, the 5-star analyst still sees significant upside, as Meta is in the early stages of launching frontier models and AI-powered offerings beyond advertising, particularly the Muse AI agent and Meta Model API access.

"Frontier models are at the core of Meta's product and monetization pipeline over a multi-year period, and its path toward superintelligence," said Anmuth.

In the summer of 2025, Meta set a goal to rebuild its Meta Superintelligence Labs team and deliver frontier AI models within a year, the analyst said. The team appears to have met its goal, rapidly advancing from the rollout of Muse Spark 1.1 in July to Muse Spark 1.3, which is competitive with Claude and GPT models, Anmuth said.

Meanwhile, Meta's Watermelon product will expand opportunities in consumer products, business intelligence, and Family of Apps engagement, the analyst said. The product will enhance internal operations and overall efficiency, said Anmuth, who's confident of Meta's ability to bring consumer-driven AI products to its extensive user base of 4 billion and views its distribution network as a competitive advantage.

While Meta has focused on frontier models and AI product development, Anmuth also sees solid opportunity in its AI infrastructure capacity, which he believes will support future compute needs.

Anmuth ranks No. 832 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of about 9.40%. See Meta Platforms Financials on TipRanks.

#2801. Nvidia is done with Jensen Huang. The fallout in Washington is just beginning

Jensen Huang is out at Nvidia. Washington is just getting started with him and the company he co-founded.

Huang, the leather-jacket-clad CEO of the world's most important chipmaker, resigned from Nvidia on Thursday after revelations that he had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy. But the spy saga is gripping Washington, and lawmakers are preparing for investigations into how the now-ex-CEO's affair may have damaged U.S. artificial intelligence dominance, the national security implications and the executive's close relationship with President Donald Trump and his Cabinet.

Republicans will have their chance first during their last six weeks controlling both chambers of Congress. GOP leaders immediately summoned Huang to Capitol Hill and said they would subpoena him if he doesn't agree to show up. In January, Democrats, who are already eager to make their mark with oversight when they take over the U.S. House and Senate, will be in the driver's seat.

"In this environment where there's some really high stakes when it comes to our national security, I would expect there would be some follow-up, yeah," Senate Majority Leader John Thune, R-S.D., told reporters at the Capitol on Thursday.

Huang and the woman with whom he reportedly had a relationship, Alice Huang, an executive at the Singaporean technology company Megaspeed and an alleged Chinese spy have drawn congressional attention. And Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick has drawn fire after reports this week that he stalled President Donald Trump's crackdowns on firms routing Nvidia chips to China.

Some Congress members have also called for an investigation into the role of the Bureau of Industry and Security, a subagency of the Commerce Department that manages and enforces export controls, in the Huang scandal.

The New York Times reported on Monday that Nvidia's general counsel, Tim Teter, claimed in emails to Commerce investigators probing Alice Huang's company that he'd been in touch with Jeffrey Kessler, who leads BIS. The Commerce investigation into Megaspeed, which allegedly smuggled restricted chips to China, was then transferred to the Justice Department.

By Thursday night, Senate banking committee Chair Tim Scott, R-S.C., summoned both Huang and Lutnick to testify before his committee in December. Later that same night, Lutnick had "proactively agreed to appear voluntarily before the Banking Committee to address their questions," Kristen Eichamer, a Commerce Department spokesperson, said in a statement.

Scott threatened to subpoena Huang if he does not appear. A banking panel spokesperson did not immediately respond Friday morning when asked if they'd heard from Huang.

"Recent reports raise serious concerns about our national security and the protection of sensitive U.S. technology," Scott wrote to Huang. "The American people deserve answers."

And with Democrats poised to take control of both chambers come January, the tech mogul's close ties to the White House will add fuel to an already burning fire as the new majority spools up myriad investigations into the Trump administration.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., in a post to X on Thursday, said "Congress must demand answers from Mr. Huang and others at Nvidia about how they may have put U.S. national security at risk." Schumer is in a contested race to become Senate majority leader in the next Congress.

The senators who will lead those investigations, and hold subpoena power in the new Congress, are gearing up for Nvidia to take center stage.

Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., the likely incoming chair of the Senate banking panel, said in a statement Thursday that "Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and NVIDIA's board of directors still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls

Warren and Sen. Chris Coons, D-Del., the top Democrat on the Senate Foreign Relations subpanel with jurisdiction over East Asia and the Pacific, sent a letter Wednesday to Lutnick, Secretary of State Marco Rubio and White House Chief of Staff Stephen Miller instructing them to preserve all documents and communications related to Nvidia's exports of "advanced AI chips to China or to entities that may divert such chips to China."

Even before Democrats take control in January, Huang is expected to appear before Congress.

House Foreign Affairs Committee Chair Brian Mast, R-Fla., is actively negotiating for Huang to testify before House committees and told reporters Thursday the Nvidia CEO has agreed to appear in the coming weeks.

Details of that testimony are still being worked out, Mast said. It would likely include his committee, as well as the House intelligence panel and the House Permanent Select Committee on China, and it would be under oath and transcribed. It's not clear at this point whether it would be public.

"We want to see him basically in a week … Sooner rather than later," Mast told reporters Thursday, adding that he believed it would happen before Thanksgiving.

If Huang doesn't willingly appear, Mast said he will subpoena him.

Mast, along with House Select China Committee Chair Rep. John Moolenaar, R-Mich., and House Intelligence Committee Chair Rep. Rick Crawford, R-Ark., sent a second request Thursday to Huang and Teter requesting they appear and giving them an 8 p.m. ET Friday deadline to respond. Mast also sent a second request to Kessler.

Rep. Josh Gottheimer, D-N.J., a member of the House Intelligence Committee, said Friday during an appearance on CNBC's "The Exchange" that he wants answers from Huang on the national security and export implications of the affair.

"From an intelligence committee perspective, you can imagine from an AI perspective, how important this is for our national security, for our critical infrastructure and understand when it comes to chip policy, what exactly got to China and ... did Megaspeed divert chips?" Gottheimer said.

The calls for immediate investigation into Huang and Nvidia on the day of his resignation represent a stunning fall from grace in the halls of power for the former CEO. Huang was one of the most effective voices for the AI industry in Washington — able to toggle between leveraging his relationship with Trump and defending his industry from fears about AI's risks to lawmakers.

"He is always the TED Talk guy," one senior Democratic staff member who was granted anonymity to describe private meetings they had attended with Huang, told CNBC. "He's really good and articulate, and really good at explaining why AI is important and why it's so important for the United States to be leading here."

The staffer said Huang was a "happy warrior" for AI, and stayed longer than scheduled or anticipated for questions from lawmakers. But in the meetings with lawmakers the aide attended, they said Huang did not aggressively push against export controls or get too "deep in the weeds" to really "lobby one way or the other" with the lawmakers.

Huang has repeatedly resisted export controls publicly, a move that has rubbed some lawmakers the wrong way.

Some lawmakers even lamented the loss of Huang, despite the inevitable buzzsaw he now faces on Capitol Hill.

"It's just sad all around," Sen. John Fetterman, D-Pa., said of Huang's resignation.

#2800. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

Stock markets remain volatile as investors weigh uncertainty in the Middle East, rising interest rates and concerns about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking a stable income stream can enhance their portfolios with the addition of dividend stocks. In this regard, tracking recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks that are backed by strong financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is the first dividend pick. Earlier this month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12 per share, CVX offers a dividend yield of about 3.5%.

After a meeting with senior leadership, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on CVX and raised his price target to $240 from $225. Among the key takeaways Mehta emphasized was that Chevron is building a solid pipeline of promising international exploration projects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean. In particular, he mentioned the focus on Venezuela, where Chevron expects to more than double gross production to 600 kbd from about 280 kbd by 2031, using three joint ventures.

Mehta also noted Chevron's efforts to deploy new technologies, especially in shale and tight-oil operations. CVX is focused on boosting productivity and efficiency to maximize free cash flow, he added.

Initiatives such as artificial lift optimization, AI and machine learning, and advanced chemical treatments are expected to improve recovery by about 10% in new wells. Consequently, capital spending per barrel in U.S. shale is projected to fall 25% in 2026, with total Permian spending dropping below $3.5 billion.

Mehta also said that Chevron sees power as a growth opportunity and is using its U.S. natural gas portfolio, key equipment and partnerships to meet rising demand. Notably, Chevron recently signed a 20-year power purchase deal with Microsoft (MSFT) to supply 2.67 GW of behind-the-meter capacity. This project, called Kilby, is expected to see its first power delivery in 2028 and generate mid-teens returns and long-term cash flows.

Mehta ranks No. 536 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.3%. See Chevron Statistics on TipRanks.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD) is a master limited partnership (MLP) that provides midstream energy services to producers and consumers of natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), crude oil, refined products and petrochemicals. With a quarterly cash distribution of 56 cents per common unit ($2.24 per common unit annualized), EPD offers a yield of about 6%.

Following a recent call with the company, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on EPD with a price target of $42. The analyst slightly lowered her estimates for the second half of 2026, citing margin and volume normalization, though demand dynamics remain strong.

While the 5-star analyst expects a sequential decline due to normal seasonality and normalization following high spreads in the first half of the year, she still expects EPD to report another solid quarter (Q3 2026) and enter 2027 with a favorable setup. Ahead of Q3 earnings, Scotto expects investors to focus on export demand durability, EPD's ability to profit from spot cargo sales and growth in Permian volumes.

Scotto continues to expect $150 million of common unit buybacks per quarter in the second half of 2026. She expects buybacks to increase to $200 million per quarter in 2027, as capex spending declines and free cash flow improves.

In total, Scotto is bullish on EPD and views it as a core MLP holding that has both offensive and defensive characteristics. The analyst praised EPD's diverse asset base as ensuring stable cash flows. "Furthermore, the partnership's multi-year organic growth backlog helps provide visibility on long-term distribution growth," Scotto said.

Scotto ranks No. 199 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.9%. See Enterprise Products Partners Financials on TipRanks.

Brookfield Infrastructure Partners (BIP) operates a diversified portfolio of high-quality utilities, transport, midstream and data assets. The company declared a quarterly distribution of 45.5 cents per unit, payable on September 29. At an annualized distribution of $1.82, BIP offers a yield of 5.2%.

Recently, BMO Capital analyst Devin Dodge reaffirmed a buy rating on Brookfield Infrastructure with a price target of $47. The analyst cited improved visibility into BIP's growth and views its corporate simplification as a potential catalyst.

"Layering in the benefits from the proposed corporate simplification, a multiple well below historical levels and an attractive yield, we believe BIP offers a compelling risk/reward and it remains a preferred idea," said Dodge.

With more organic growth projects in the pipeline and new partnership investment opportunities, Dodge believes that BIP has clearer visibility into where it will deploy capital. That should support continued double-digit growth in funds from operations (FFO) per unit.

Dodge said the semiconductor foundries that BIP is developing with Intel (INTC) are expected to be fully commissioned by the end of 2026, with returns improving through Q4 2026. Dodge expects the joint venture to boost FFO by 300-400 basis points.

Dodge ranks No. 476 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 68% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.5%. See Brookfield Infrastructure Partners KPIs on TipRanks.

#2799. Trump doesn’t plan to raise Nvidia, spy issue during China trip: U.S. officials

President Donald Trump is not expected to raise allegations of a Chinese intelligence operation involving former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang during his upcoming meeting with Chinese President Xi Jinping, senior U.S. officials said Friday.

Officials also said that Elon Musk, the CEO of Tesla and SpaceX, will accompany Trump on the trip to Shenzhen, China, for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit on Nov. 18 and 19.

The White House, earlier Friday, said Trump plans to have a three-way summit there with Xi and Russian President Vladimir Putin.

Jensen Huang's relationship with the suspected spy, Alice Huang, who is not related to him, has raised concerns that a Singapore-based company where she was an executive diverted restricted Nvidia chips to China.

"The situation with Nvidia should not really have any bearing on the trip," an official said on the press call, speaking on the condition that they not be identified.

"In all of our discussions and negotiations with the Chinese, we do not negotiate American export controls ... and we don't expect it to figure at all in any of our conversations with the Chinese while we're there."

Asked if Trump would raise the allegations against Alice Huang to Chinese leader Xi Jinping, the official said, "The focus of a meeting with President Xi, at least on the economic side, is going to be about implementation of the trade agreement."

"When it comes to some of these national security matters and examinations and investigations that are going on in the U.S., [that is] probably not something that we're going to read the Chinese in on at this moment," the official said.

Another official said, "I don't expect the president to raise it — the Alice Huang issue."

China has called the allegations against Alice Huang baseless.

Huang resigned this week as Nvidia's CEO after damning reports that he had an affair with Alice Huang, who was an executive of Megaspeed, a cloud-computing company that is being eyed for possible facilitation of sending billions of dollars worth of Nvidia chips in violation of U.S. restrictions on those chips.

The scandal has rocked Nvidia and raised questions about whether the Commerce Department, which is led by Howard Lutnick, has been aggressive enough at blocking advanced chips from ending up in China.

During Friday's call, a reporter asked why Musk was going on the trip with Trump.

"I don't want to speak for the president on exactly why Elon's going," an official said.

"Obviously, he has tremendous business interests in the U.S.-China relationship. He's a big part of our national security," the official said. "There are numerous reasons, given the engagement that the president is going to have, where I think Elon would be of tremendous benefit, just even as an advisor to the president."

#2798. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2797. NFL Tells Senator Independent Experts Will Review Eagles' Jalen Hurts' Blood Clot Case

Philadelphia Eagles quarterback Jalen Hurts will have the handling of his blood clots reviewed by independent medical experts, Tim McManus of ESPN reported.

The NFL described the review in a response to Sen. Lisa Blunt Rochester of Delaware, who questioned the league in October.

"As outlined by the league and NFLPA's Collective Bargaining Agreement, mutually agreed-upon independent medical experts will review the matter and share their conclusions and recommendations with both parties," said Jeff Miller, the NFL's executive vice president of communications, public affairs and health policy, in a statement released Friday by Blunt Rochester's office. "Following the ongoing joint review, the League and NFLPA will consider appropriate next steps, including whether this case points to a need for additional league-wide guidance."

"For at least the past decade, the NFL has tracked clotting disorders among players every season through a subcommittee of its General Medical Committee, and this information is shared with the NFLPA," Miller said.

The joint review has not formally started while the league and the union work out its parameters.

The collective bargaining agreement gives the neutral physicians 60 days from their selection to issue a written report. Both sides must then act on any recommendations to address and correct problems.

The NFL Players Association has not invoked Article 39, which permits a formal union probe into medical care from club-affiliated staff.

Doctors discovered several clots in the 28-year-old's lungs in October, and he is expected to miss the remainder of the 2026 season. The three-time Pro Bowler is the fourth-leading passer in Eagles history and was MVP of Super Bowl LIX against the Kansas City Chiefs.

Hurts was cleated in the calf Oct. 4 against the Los Angeles Rams and told the medical staff Oct. 8. An ultrasound in London on Oct. 10 found a superficial blood clot.

He played in a 19-17 loss to the Jacksonville Jaguars, and a follow-up ultrasound in Philadelphia showed the clot had moved.

The Eagles placed Hurts on injured reserve Oct. 17, and his diagnosis was updated to deep vein thrombosis and bilateral pulmonary emboli. Because he is taking blood thinners, he cannot play football or take part in any other contact activity.

"Jalen's prognosis is very good, and we expect a full recovery," agent Nicole Lynn and the Eagles said in a joint statement in October. "We anticipate that he will have the opportunity to resume contact football activities when off the blood thinners."

"I'm grateful for an update on the NFL's review of Mr. Hurts' diagnosis and for information on the League's efforts to track clotting disorders in its athletes," Blunt Rochester said in a statement Friday. "While I appreciate the NFL's transparency, I encourage its leadership to use this moment to increase awareness of DVT and PE to better protect its players and support public health."

"We welcome the opportunity to provide additional information, and find ways to support your efforts around raising awareness and improving outcomes related to blood clot disorders," Miller wrote.

Hurts put up 933 passing yards, eight passing touchdowns, three interceptions, 107 rushing yards and one rushing touchdown this season. He completed 57.8% of his passes.

The Eagles signed Hurts to a five-year, $255 million extension in April 2023. However, there is little guaranteed money left on Hurts' deal after the 2026 season.

A talented runner and thrower, Hurts finished the 2025 season with 3,224 passing yards, 25 passing touchdowns, 421 rushing yards and eight rushing touchdowns. He completed 64.8% of his passes.

#2796. FAA lifts ground stop after more than four hours, but flight cancellations will stretch into the weekend

The U.S. halted flight departures across the country for hours on Friday for the second time since 9/11, blaming aging technology that was overloaded, affecting key information for multiple air traffic control centers.

More than 10,000 flights were disrupted during the nationwide ground stop, and delays and cancellations were set to stretch into the weekend, if not longer.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said at a press conference late Friday that that telecommunications routers were overloaded with data. "They went down," he said.

Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin said Friday at the White House that there was no indication that the issues were a threat to the United States. He later said at a press conference that it doesn't appear to be a cyber attack, and that it "appears this was mechanically driven."

The Federal Aviation Administration canceled the ground stop at 8:15 p.m. ET Friday after it lasted formore than four hours and disrupted the travel plans of hundreds of thousands of travelers, including some sports teams.

The agency earlier said in post on X that there was a "temporary telecommunications outage" and that it halted flights out of an "abundance of caution."

When it issued the ground stop at about 4 p.m., the FAA said the communications issues were "impacting multiple Air Route Traffic Control Centers." These facilities guide airplanes at high altitudes along their routes.

While the FAA lifted the ground stop, travel chaos continued at airports, which were forced to find places for parked planes. Delays were set to continue even as flights start taking off again.

Carriers canceled more flights as the disruption wore on. JetBlue Airways told staff it would cancel the remainder of the day's flights, aside from European routes, as well as others scheduled for Saturday so that crews and planes are in place to resume operations, according to a memo seen by CNBC.

A ground stop halts flights at their departure point. Disruptions tend to snowball if ground stops drag on as flight crews reach their maximum number of duty hours for a day.

There were 11,000 flights scheduled to depart U.S. airports after 4 p.m. ET on Friday, according to flight-data firm Cirium, with 1.5 million passengers. There were about 600 diverted flights on Friday, 104 of them sent to other airports, Cirium said.

International flights had started taking off from coastal hubs before 6 p.m. ET.

The FAA said earlier in the night that flights will be allowed to depart as en route traffic facilities recover, "through coordination with the air traffic control system."

The FAA's technicians are investigating, the agency said.

Nationwide ground stops are extremely rare. In January 2023, the FAA halted flights because of issues with a pilot alert system. Before that, traffic was halted nationwide after the Sept. 11, 2001, terror attacks.

Technical issues have repeatedly snarled air traffic in the U.S. in recent years. Aging infrastructure and staffing shortages have angered airline executives for years.

In September, hundreds of flights into Newark, New Jersey, and Philadelphia were canceled, delayed or diverted after a Verizon fiber cable was accidentally cut. Officials said it occurred during construction in New Jersey, severing a backup feed of key data for air traffic controllers.

In 2025, air traffic controllers lost contact with aircraft heading to and from Newark Liberty International Airport, an equipment failure that led to massive flight delays and raised more concerns about aging U.S. aviation infrastructure and staffing shortages.

"It's going to continue to fail," Duffy said Friday of U.S. air traffic control infrastructure. The U.S. is in the middle of an overhaul of the technology, and Duffy urged lawmakers for more funding.

Inbound international flights will be managed on a case by case basis, the FAA said.

The major U.S. airlines issued statements saying they would work with customers who were affected by the ground stop and encouraged travelers to check for updates.

— CNBC's Kevin Breuninger and Phil LeBeau contributed to this article.

#2795. Sean McVay reveals real reason why Rams suddenly changed minds on Puka Nacua’s injury status

Rams receiver Puka Nacua hasn't received a new update on his knee and toe injuries. His recovery and timeline remain the same, the team simply ran out of room on the roster.

After initially deciding against placing their All-Pro wide receiver on injured reserve, the Rams reversed course Thursday night, officially sidelining Nacua for at least four games after he suffered MCL and PCL sprains in his right knee, along with turf toe, on the final offensive play of Sunday's 20-13 victory over the Commanders.

On Friday, Rams coach Sean McVay explained the abrupt change of plans.

Los Angeles Rams wide receiver Puka Nacua warms up before an NFL football game against the Buffalo Bills in Inglewood, Calif., Monday, Oct. 12, 2026. AP Photo/Scott Strazzante

"I'm sure you guys are wondering what changed with Puka. We needed the roster spot," McVay said. "We didn't need it initially."

McVay insisted the decision wasn't prompted by a setback in Nacua's recovery. The Rams never realistically expected their superstar receiver to return before the minimum four-game absence required by injured reserve.

"Some things changed where we needed that spot because of all the moving pieces," McVay said. "That was what we did to be able to get Brennan Presley up."

Nacua, meanwhile, has begun rehabilitation and remains in good spirits, according to McVay.

With Nacua sidelined and fellow star receiver Davante Adams also unavailable, the Rams elevated Presley to the active roster ahead of Sunday's Week 10 rematch against the Cardinals.

Los Angeles Rams head coach Sean McVay talks to reporters following an NFL football game against the Las Vegas Raiders, Sunday, Oct. 25, 2026, in Las Vegas. AP Photo/John Locher

McVay praised Presley's consistency, versatility and ability to contribute beyond the receiving corps.

"He's just one of those guys that makes the plays when his number's called," McVay said. "He's a great teammate, really smart, where he can play a lot of different spots if need be, can contribute on special teams."

Presley could also factor into the kick return rotation alongside Ronnie Rivers and the recently returned Xavier Smith.

For a Rams team whose injury list keeps growing, the decision wasn't particularly complicated. Nacua wasn't coming back anytime soon, and Los Angeles needed another healthy body who could help immediately.

#2794. Troy Aikman engages in potential pre-tampering with Kyle Shanahan

If Troy Aikman exits broadcasting after this season (last month, he put the chance at “50-50”) and if Aikman lands a key position in a front office, the name at the top of his head-coaching wishlist will already be known.

Via Brandon Contes of Awful Announcing, Aikman said during a Thursday appearance on 96.7 The Ticket in Dallas that, if he were running an NFL team, he would try to hire 49ers coach Kyle Shanahan.

“I’ve been asked a lot of questions here, certainly over the last several weeks, about any aspirations that I may have of going in a front office,” Aikman said. “Or ‘Who would you hire as your head coach?’ If I was in a position to hire someone as a head coach, [the] first call I’d make is to the 49ers and say, ‘How much is it going to take to get Kyle Shanahan? Because I think he’s that good. And yes, I’ve always believed this, but coaching matters and it has an impact on an organization.”

It’s not tampering because Aikman currently isn’t running a team. (It could technically be “tampering light” if he’s still working for the Dolphins.) And the information gives Shanahan plenty of leverage in his dealings with the 49ers, if Aikman joins the likes of Matt Millen, John Lynch, and John Elway as former players with little or no front-office experience who find themselves working in high-level jobs with a team.

Aikman has previously made similar comments about Shanahan, in the context of the Bears’ latest coaching vacancy.

“There has been discussion about, ‘Do you give up some picks and see if you can maybe make a deal for a guy like Kyle Shanahan?’” Aikman said during a game in 2024. “I would be all in on that. Because he would develop a quarterback, and he’s a hell of a head coach. But can you pull that off? That’s a better question. And who might you hire that has no head-coaching experience as an offensive guy? You never know the answer to that, as to how good they’re gonna be.”

While it seems to be working out pretty well for the Bears and their head coach who had no head-coaching experience, the point remains that Aikman would try to hire Shanahan, if Aikman is ever in a position to hire a coach.

And there’s one last twist to this story that currently isn’t known. When Aikman was consulting with the Dolphins during their latest search for a G.M. and head coach, did Aikman advise Miami to call about Shanahan? If so, did they?

Shanahan is in his 10th season with the 49ers. He’s taken the team to two Super Bowls. Barring the franchise’s first championship since 1994 (which could come this year), there could be a point where Shanahan reaches an Andy Reid-style mutual expiration date.

If that ever happens, Shanahan will have options. Especially if Aikman ends up getting hired to be someone’s G.M.

#2793. Why failure without Kawhi Leonard is ok, but not without Scottie Barnes

When you start dating, there's something to be said for bad dates and breakups serving as lessons for the do's and don'ts of the world of romance. But, if you keep making the same crummy jokes, not brushing your teeth, over and over again, you're not learning much from those bad dates. At a certain point, losing becomes unproductive.

It's kind of like that with basketball, too. And in the last couple of matches, the Toronto Raptors' losing has been real, real unproductive. Because, it doesn't tell anyone anything that they don't already know.

TORONTO, ON- OCTOBER 21 – Scottie Barnes #4 of the Toronto Raptors and Kawhi Leonard #2 of the Toronto Raptors share a laugh on the bench as the Toronto Raptors play the Chicago Bulls in their season opener at Scotiabank Arena in Toronto. October 21, 2026. Steve Russell/Toronto Star (Steve Russell/Toronto Star via Getty Images) | Toronto Star via Getty Images

Both times when the Raptors have traded for Kawhi Leonard, there's been an implicit gamble attached to that. Leonard won't play a lot of regular season games, but take him to the playoffs okay, and he'll turn into the '84 Terminator, or '85 Jordan, whichever set of skills might be needed more that night.

This season, Kawhi went out early. Okay. That just means that the other guys need to start pulling extra weight a little sooner. And you remember the playoff series against the Cavaliers, and say, "okay, Scottie and RJ, maybe they can anchor this offence, even without a scorer like Brandon Ingram or Kawhi Leonard." And then… Scottie Barnes goes down.

An old teacher of mine told me that "practice doesn't make perfect — perfect practice makes perfect," and it concerns me that that Raptors are just practicing really, far, far away from perfect. Now, Scottie seems like he's going to come back soon; when exactly that will be is unclear, but there has been little indication that the groin strain is something particularly serious.

But, losing the games that the Raptors are losing, without Scottie Barnes, doesn't really help them understand how their team is going to perform in playoff settings, which, for this roster, should be the ultimate goal. Before the season started, Bobby Webster spoke to the media, and argued that the question that should remain pervasive throughout the season should be "can we win every night?"

Thus far, the answer to that question, without Kawhi Leonard, has be a resounding "no." Omer Osman illustrates that clearly, in the tweet above. It's not 2019 anymore. The Raptors are a different beast, younger, less veteran-heavy, and constructed in a less careful manner.

With Scottie missing the last couple of matchups against heavy-hitters like Boston and Philadelphia, the Raptors don't know how well the team can operate in an optimal sans-Kawhi state, because Toronto was never expecting to, and is not built to, compete without both of its All-Stars.

Barnes was showcasing that he's taken a leap offensively, and can piggy-back the Raptors — to a degree — but when facing off against diminished rosters of great teams, and the forward isn't there to help the Raptors take advantage of weakened clubs, the Raptors are effectively leaving both wins or positive reps on the table, and without Barnes, aren't necessarily getting either.

This isn't to say that there's *zero* value from playing without Barnes or Leonard. RJ Barrett just had a season high in points, and is continuing to prove his value. Jakob Poeltl has quietly been looking more like his uninjured self again. And the team's younger players are getting more time on the court to find their legs and develop themselves further. But, this season was never meant to be about development. It was meant to be about the Raptors bashing teams' heads in with lockdown defence and gliding to the playoffs.

But, ultimately, the team has to play the cards they're dealt. The season thus far hasn't been the triumphant return of Raptors basketball to the big stage anyone expected, but there's a lot of basketball left in the year.

@BAAILEYYCREATES on Twitter raised an important point; that last year, Leonard's health management kept him off the court almost entirely until the new year, and when he was back, he was whooping teams left right and centre. It's not 2019 anymore, and Kawhi is 35 years old now, but that means, simply, that ramping up looks different for Leonard now, not that he's unable to deliver on-court product. The Raptors need to be able to compete with and without Leonard for this roster to work, but without Scottie Barnes to lead the charge, Toronto will remain in dire straits.

#2791. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."
