#2758. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2757. Robert Saleh says he and Liam Coen are good after "misunderstanding" last year

When the Titans face the Jaguars on Sunday, Robert Saleh and Liam Coen will coach against each other for the first time since their run-in last year, when they exchanged words on the field over something Saleh, then the defensive coordinator of the 49ers, said something about Coen that Coen didn’t appreciate.

Last year, Saleh said that Coen’s team was good at stealing signals, intending it as a compliment for how good they are at recognizing what the opposing defense was doing. Coen interpreted it as an accusation of cheating and confronted Saleh on the field after the game.

Today Saleh was asked about that, and he said he and Coen have spoken and there are no hard feelings.

“Liam’s great, man,” Saleh said. “We’ve had plenty of interactions. The whole thing is what it was, a little misunderstanding, poor words on my part. But they’re doing a great job. Overall as an organization, they’ve done a really nice job.”

#2756. Lakeland answers questions vs. St. Thomas Aquinas, other Open Division statements

All it took was one game to throw the Open Division into chaos.

Lakeland stunned top-seeded St. Thomas Aquinas, 27-24, in overtime on 13-yard touchdown pass from Kelin Hendrix to Joseph Howard Jr.

The same program that Aquinas shut out in the past two Class 5A state championship games went to Fort Lauderdale and forced football fans to rethink what they thought about the Open Division.

Forget St. Thomas Aquinas' quest for a national title. The Raiders now face a likely must-win situation when they travel to Venice for the second week of pool play.

And Lakeland? The Dreadnaughts have a chance to take firm control of Pool A when they travel to Chaminade-Madonna.

West Boca coach Dylan Potts said after the game that the Open Division is an emotional roller coaster. This is only the first week. We have four more weekends before crowning a state champion.

Here are quick takeaways from the first week of pool play:

Look, there were legitimate reasons to question Lakeland going into its game at Aquinas.

How would the team play in a big road game? The Dreadnaughts were the only Open Division squad that didn't play a regular season road game against a team ranked in the top 50 in the MaxPreps rankings.

Would Lakeland be able to move the ball after getting shut out two years in a row?

Those questions were answered on Lakeland's first drive when the Dreadnaughts marched down the field and scored. That touchdown changed everything.

This result wasn't a fluke. It was a statement. Lakeland was the better team. And now it's Aquinas that has questions to answer.

It's hard to imagine Raines having a better start against West Boca. The Vikings marched right down the field and scored a touchdown against a team that only allowed six touchdowns during the regular season.

After that? All West Boca. Junior athlete Terriel Harmon scored two touchdowns in the Bulls 28-7 win.

The win extends West Boca's postseason win streak to 11 games, which is now the longest in the state. For Raines, it ends the team's 25-game win streak.

It was an impressive performance for West Boca, which hosts a reeling Miami Central next week. Raines hosts No. 2 Columbus, which looks like a must-win game for the Vikings.

The biggest question for Venice was how would the Indiana secondary handle Chaminade's talented receivers? Venice didn't have a lot of answers.

Tromon Isaac Jr. caught a pair of touchdown passes in the Lions 35-0 win. The game had a running clock midway through the third quarter.

Venice had plenty of chances but had seven drives stall in Chaminade territory. That includes three interceptions thrown in the red zone.

Chaminade now hosts Lakeland with the winner being in pole position in Pool A. Venice heads back home for a must-win game against Aquinas.

For the second time in less than a month, Columbus easily dispatched Miami Central. This time the score was 40-3. It was 28-3 in less than three full quarters in the Greater Miami Athletic Conference final on Oct. 16.

Kai Moore threw a pair of touchdown passes for the Explorers. Columbus now makes its longest road trip of the season when it travels to Raines next week.

The biggest question is how will Miami Central respond? The Rockets have lost three games in the past four weeks, all by double digits. The team is already without starting quarterback James Perrone, who is out with an injury. Now Central has to go to West Boca and find a way to counter one of the state's premier defenses.

No. 2 Columbus (1-0) at No. 7 Raines (0-1)

#2754. NFL predictions: Can the Falcons slow down Kenneth Walker?

The Falcons had a rough outing in Week 9, falling by two scores to the Cincinnati Bengals in Madrid. Atlanta was able to compete for the first part of the game, but quickly fell behind and ultimately looked outclassed by one of the NFL's premier offenses. That result, and the way it unfolded, seemed to prompt some soul-searching from the Falcons coaching staff and front office. In the ensuing days, Atlanta traded away star safety Jessie Bates III and starting center Ryan Neuzil.

That seems to signal a change in philosophy for the Falcons to more prominently focusing on the future. Atlanta isn't going to tank or anything close to it—the NFC South is led by a 3-win team at the moment, so nobody is even close to being "out of it". But this is at least a tacit acknowledgement that the current construction of the team isn't close enough to turn down future assets. It's the right move, and an important realization for the new staff.

That said, there's still a big game coming up this weekend against the Kansas City Chiefs in Week 10. The Falcons are currently 3.5-point home underdogs, per FanDuel, and there are some significant injury question marks to consider heading into this one.

The biggest matchup to watch here will be the beleaguered Falcons run defense against the electric Chiefs rushing attack headed by former Seahawks star Kenneth Walker. Walker has been a godsend for Kansas City, finding a new level of success beyond even his final Super Bowl-winning season in Seattle. With the Chiefs passing game still a bit lackluster, Walker's dominance on the ground has been a key cog in their offensive machine.

Even though Atlanta started out the season with fantastic run defense, teams have largely figured out how to beat the Falcons attacking-style front with inside runs and a lot of man/gap concepts. The Falcons haven't yet developed a counterpunch, and until they do…it's going to be a long day for the defense against any team with even a semi-competent rushing attack. Slowing down Kenneth Walker—or having him not play, as he's currently questionable for the game—is a pivotal matchup to watch.

On the other side of the ball, the Falcons will have to build some offensive consistency. Michael Penix Jr. is in the midst of a two-game slump and was unable to find much success against a fairly beatable Bengals defense. The bigger issue might be the ground game, which should be the backbone of the offense but hasn't had any consistent success in over a month. With Ryan Neuzil heading out and Corey Levin taking over at center, I'm not expecting significant strides there. But perhaps Levin is a better fit for what Bill Callahan wants to do, given their experience together.

This is a tough matchup for Atlanta against a Super Bowl-contending Chiefs team. I'm not expecting much from the offense or defense, to be completely honest. With a bye coming up in Week 11, I worry we might see a "let's just get to the bye" performance. Hopefully I'm wrong, and the Falcons can at least give us an entertaining game on Sunday. An upset victory would keep Atlanta right in the thick of things in the NFC South, and the latter part of the schedule is considerably easier than the last month and a half. We'll see if the Falcons can do it.

#2753. Kenneth Walker did not practice Friday with groin tightness

There is a potentially significant injury situation brewing with the Chiefs.

Head coach Andy Reid told reporters in his Friday press conference that running back Kenneth Walker sat out Friday’s practice after reporting tightness in his groin.

“We’ll just see how he feels through this afternoon with rehab and through tomorrow,” Reid said. “Somewhere he tweaked it. So, we’ll see. ... He was tight this morning.”

While Reid did not explicitly say a game status, it sounds as if Walker will be questionable.

“We’re kind of feeling it out just to see where we’re at with that thing,” Reid said. “We’re not going to put him at risk or anything like that — that’s not how we roll. So, if he can go, he can go. If he can’t, he can’t.”

Walker leads the league averaging 109.0 rushing yards per game. He’s tallied a total of 872 yards with seven rushing touchdowns in eight games. He’s also caught 26 passes for 172 yards with four TDs.

If Walker is not able to play, rookie Emmett Johnson would be in line to start and carry the load.

“Yeah, if Emmett has to do it, then we all have [confidence],” Reid said. “He’s going to play anyway, whether Ken’s there or not there, he’s gonna play. But everybody has confidence in him, yeah.”

Johnson has 111 rushing yards on 26 carries with a touchdown and 14 receptions for 237 yards with a TD so far this year.

Kansas City’s full injury report with game statuses will be released later in the day.

#2750. Cass Tech RB Julian Taylor collects 4 TDs in playoff win vs Northville

With Cass Tech nursing a nine-point lead with over six minutes left to play, the Technicians fed the ball to junior running back Julian Taylor, hoping he'd run out the clock.

He did one better, scoring on a 20-yard run that put the game out of reach.

Behind four rushing touchdowns from their star rusher, the top-ranked Technicians (12-0) beat Northville, 40-30, in the  MHSAA football Division 1 regional final on Friday, Nov. 13, in Detroit. With the win, the Technicians will face undefeated Romeo (12-0) in the Division 1 semifinals on Saturday, Nov. 21, one win away from the state finals at Ford Field.

FRIDAY'S SCORES: Michigan high school football final scores in regional finals

Taylor will tell you that he wasn't a one-man show, and based on how Cass Tech's offensive line and wide receivers were blocking all game, it's hard to disagree.

"I can't do nothing without my receivers blocking down the field, my O-line putting people on their butt, opening up a hole, and I just gotta make a play for them," he said after the game.

Of course, there was still the individual brilliance. With Cass Tech up 21-17 in the third quarter and deep in Mustang territory, Taylor took a handoff to the left side, bounced off a couple of tacklers and then did his best Jahmyr Gibbs impression, cutting all the way back to the right side of the field and darting towards the end zone for a 28-yard score – his second touchdown.

"I'm not great if I can't make a guy miss. I gotta make somebody miss," he said.

DIVISION 4: Divine Child rides ballhawk defense back to MHSAA football semifinals

That didn't come easy in the first half, however, with Taylor putting up just 14 yards on five carries as the Mustangs defense stymied the big runner. Northville (9-3), down 21-0 early in the second quarter, came back with three consecutive scores, two on passing touchdowns from senior quarterback Greyson Harris to junior wideout Jackson DeFore and one on a field goal before halftime to cut the Cass Tech lead to four points.

The Technicians then leaned on Taylor, with the junior putting up 78 yards on nine rushes in the second half with some help from a ferocious offensive line.

"He's a monster. Anytime he does touch the ball, he can go for six [points]," said junior Cass Tech quarterback Donald Tabron, a 2028 Texas A&M commit. "He's able to go make plays for himself, but the O-line is a big aspect of that one. Forty points doesn't just come from the quarterback, receivers and the running back. ... Everything is built off the O-line."

Tabron flashed some big numbers, too, going 13-for-20 for 239 passing yards with two touchdowns and an interception. He completed a clutch 15-yard fourth-down pass to junior Mylan Griggs in the third quarter that set up Taylor's third touchdown run, a three-yard dive that came with little resistance.

"He made some big-time throws," said Taylor. "He's a five-star [recruit] for a reason. He's going to Texas A&M for a reason. That's that guy."

Cass Tech's defense and special teams weren't perfect, with two onside kick recoveries from the Mustangs allowing Northville to hang around until the fourth quarter. But the Technicians flashed a pair of highlight-worthy, one-handed interceptions, one from junior defensive back Marquell Evans in the second quarter that saw the junior jumping high, reaching back and snagging the ball with his right hand in a Jordan logo pose.

"[Northville] had scored right before that, so I had to come out and do something for my guys, and then we got the ball right back," said Evans, a 2028 Pitt commit.

Tabron, who was one of several teammates on the sidelines mimicking Evans' pose right after the turnover, said he had never seen a play like that in person.

After a touchdown and a failed 2-point conversion with less than two minutes left in the game, Northville recovered an onside kick and attempted a comeback down by 10 points. A deep throw from Harris landed in the hands of Griggs, who, like Evans, hauled in the takeaway with just one hand.

"Having it [happen] again two quarters later to basically seal the game, wow, [those are] the type of athletes we've got," Tabron said.

Taylor was a bit less impressed by the interceptions, however.

"I mean, yeah, we go to Cass. You see that all the time."

#2749. Las Vegas Raiders-Seattle Seahawks FEED Topic: Monitoring Fernando Mendoza’s 1st start

While Kirk Cousins exceeded expectations as the Las Vegas Raiders' starting quarterback, getting the team to a 4-5 record and in the playoff mix at the halfway point of the season, Week 10 against the Seattle Seahawks will be the official start of the Fernando Mendoza era in Las Vegas.

John Spytek, Klint Kubiak and the Raiders' coaching staff determined that the rookie is ready for action, trading Cousins to the Minnesota Vikings for a third-round pick and naming Mendoza the starter. That creates plenty of excitement throughout the organization and fanbase. However, the No. 1 pick's first start couldn't have come against a much tougher opponent.

Seahawks head coach Mike Macdonald has a reputation as a "defensive mastermind", running a scheme that has a lot of moving parts and can create confusion for opposing quarterbacks, both from a pressure and coverage standpoint.

For example, Seattle's defense ranks first in yards per game allowed (244.2), tied for fourth in points per game (18.0), tied for third in EPA per pass (-0.17) and 13th in pressure rate (39.6%), according to Sumer Sports, this season. Also, Macdonald is 6-0 during the regular season against rookie quarterbacks, partly because even veteran signal callers can have a tough time deciphering his defense.

But the 2025 Heisman Trophy winner will have some help. Kubiak should be able to prepare him for what type of looks he'll face on Sunday since Kubiak worked for Macdonald last year as the Seahawks' offensive coordinator. Additionally, having a unique offensive weapon who can bail quarterbacks out in Brock Bowers, as well as quality check-down options in Michael Mayer and Ashton Jeanty, gives Mendoza a few security blankets to get out of trouble.

There's probably going to be some ups and downs on Sunday — and likely for the rest of the season, for that matter. But if the rookie looks sharp against arguably the best defense in the league this weekend, that should give him a lot of confidence moving forward and bode well for the Raiders' future.

So, for this week's game day discussion on The FEED, we'll be keeping tabs on how Mendoza looks in his first start. The game kicks off at 1:05 p.m. PST and will air on CBS (check your local listings). I look forward to talkin' some ball with y'all!

#2748. Eagles will release WR Hollywood Brown, at his request

Receiver Hollywood Brown, who was available in trade but had no takers, will now be released at his request, via Tim McManus of ESPN.

Thanks to a “noninjury settlement” (which presumably includes Brown waiving his right to termination pay), Brown will be released. Because the trade deadline has passed, Brown’s contract will be subject to waivers.

Brown signed a one-year deal with a $3.7 million signing bonus and a $1.3 million base salary. In addition to waiving his right to termination pay, Brown possibly agreed to return a portion of the bonus money.

A first-round pick of the Ravens in 2019, Brown appeared in only four games for the Eagles, with one start. He caught five passes for 63 yards.

Brown spent three years with the Ravens. In 2022, he was traded to the Cardinals. After two seasons in Arizona, he spent 2024 and 2025 with the Chiefs.

The minimum base salary could prompt another team to make a waivers claim — especially if that team would be willing to pay more than the prorated minimum to add him to the roster for the rest of the season.

Brown’s best year came in 2021, with 91 catches for 1,008 yards and six touchdowns.

#2746. Lakers stars Luka Doncic, Austin Reaves earn different status for battle with Suns

The Lakers received positive injury news following multiple scares in Wednesday's 123-112 win over the Bucks.

Lakers coach JJ Redick said Austin Reaves suffered a left heel bruise in the third quarter of that game and is listed as day-to-day ahead of Sunday's contest against the Suns.

The Lakers' medical staff and Reaves jointly determined that he didn't need further imaging after seeing how he felt Thursday morning. Reaves had X-rays Wednesday evening and they came back clean.

When asked if Reaves' injury was minor, Redick didn't hesitate.

"I would assume a day-to-day injury is minor, yeah," Redick said.

Austin Reaves #15 of the Los Angeles Lakers shoots a free throw against the Milwaukee Bucks on Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026, at Fiserv Forum in Milwaukee. NBAE via Getty Images

As for Luka Doncic, who tweaked his right ankle in the fourth quarter of Wednesday's game?

"Luka is fine," Redick said. "I think the expectation would be he's available."

Collin Sexton is also listed as day-to-day after receiving multiple stitches to close a deep cut over his right eye after he and Bucks guard Ryan Rollins bumped heads in the fourth quarter.

Redick said a concussion was ruled out for Sexton.

Meanwhile, Quentin Grimes, who missed the Lakers' last two games because of a sprained right pinkie, participated in the live portion of the "stay ready game" at Friday's practice.

Los Angeles Lakers' Collin Sexton (10) dribbles against Milwaukee Bucks' Ryan Rollins (13) during a game in Milwaukee on Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026. Getty Images

Grimes, who had his pinkie taped, said the swelling has gone down and his finger is "basically back to normal."

"I definitely expect to play, for sure," Grimes said of Sunday.

It's all positive news for the Lakers, who have won seven straight games and have the best record in the league at 11-2.

While Doncic spoke after Wednesday's game saying his ankle was "painful a little bit but I'll be fine," there was significantly more worry around Reaves, who did not speak to the media after the Lakers' win and was expected to receive more imaging upon the team's return to Los Angeles — until it was deemed unnecessary.

When Redick was asked if Doncic advised Reaves about the heel injury that he suffered in 2024, the coach said he wasn't sure.

Los Angeles Lakers' Luka Doncic looks at his ankle during an NBA basketball game against the Milwaukee Bucks in Milwaukee, Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026. AP Photo/Aaron Gash

"I know those guys text 47 times a day, so maybe there's a chance," Redick said. "Austin said he woke up yesterday at 7 a.m. and he had four texts from Luka, so maybe one of those was the heel text. I don't know."

Following a brutal stretch of three road games in four days, including a back-to-back, the Lakers had a much-needed three days to regroup before playing the Suns.

Redick and his staff spent the last three days auditing the Lakers, who are the top-rated team in the league on offense but are No. 18 on defense.

His analytics team prepared a 21-page presentation.

"We know we want to be a top-10 defense," Redick said. "We know we are not going to be able to do that unless we become better than a bottom-10 transition team and better than a bottom 10-defensive rebounding team."

#2742. What channel is Michigan vs. Oregon on today? Time, TV, streaming

Michigan heads west Saturday, Nov. 14, looking to rebound from an embarrassing rivalry loss when the Wolverines face No. 16 Oregon in a Big Ten matchup at Autzen Stadium in Eugene.

The Wolverines (5-4, 2-4 Big Ten) are coming off a 15-6 loss to Michigan State last week at Michigan Stadium. Michigan managed just 192 yards of offense and committed four turnovers, surrendering the Paul Bunyan Trophy to a Spartans team that entered the game on a six-game losing streak.

The offensive struggles prompted first-year Michigan coach Kyle Whittingham to fire offensive coordinator Jason Beck, with Jim Harding taking over on an interim basis. The Wolverines have scored just 12 points in their past two home games and are averaging 19.6 points per game this season.

More: Lofty goals dashed, Michigan football now playing for pride

Oregon (6-3, 4-2) is moving in the opposite direction after defeating No. 12 Ohio State, 41-28, last week in Columbus. The Ducks overcame a 14-0 deficit by scoring 38 consecutive points, earning their second straight victory over a ranked opponent.

Quarterback Dylan Raiola threw for 257 yards and two touchdowns against the Buckeyes, while running back Dierre Hill Jr. scored twice. Oregon has averaged 37.5 points over its past two games and remains in contention for a College Football Playoff berth.

Michigan's defense remains a strength, ranking fourth nationally in total defense at 255.3 yards allowed per game. But the Wolverines face a difficult challenge against an Oregon team that has won 30 of its 33 home games under coach Dan Lanning.

More: Michigan could be down starting offensive lineman at Oregon

Here's how to watch Michigan vs. Oregon in Week 11 of the college football season:

Radio: 94.7 FM WCSX in Detroit; Learfield Michigan Sports Network

Tim Brando will handle play-by-play for FS1, with former Michigan quarterback Devin Gardner as analyst and Josh Sims reporting from the sidelines.

Doug Karsch will have the Michigan radio call alongside analyst Jon Jansen and sideline reporter Jason Avant.

Kickoff: 3:30 p.m. ET (12:30 p.m. PT) Saturday

Saturday marks the seventh meeting between Michigan and Oregon and the second since the Ducks joined the Big Ten in 2024.

Oregon has won three consecutive meetings, including a 38-17 victory in Ann Arbor in 2024. Michigan won the first three games in the series, all shutouts, but hasn't defeated the Ducks since a 24-0 victory in 1973.

The Wolverines are making just their second trip to Autzen Stadium. Oregon won the previous meeting in Eugene, 31-27, in 2003.

Michigan needs one victory in its final three regular-season games to become bowl-eligible. Oregon, meanwhile, is looking to strengthen its postseason résumé after consecutive victories over Northwestern and Ohio State.

Oregon was a 10½-point favorite entering the weekend, according to FanDuel. Odds are subject to change.

#2735. Palmetto football defeats Royal Palm Beach behind Nazir Pitchford pick-six

As Nazir Pitchford ran to the locker room, Palmetto head coach Eric Sanders shouted to him.

With Royal Palm Beach threatening at Palmetto's 25, Pitchford watched as a run-heavy Lions team worked to convert on third-and-9. Royal Palm Beach's quarterback threw the ball, and Pitchford "read it perfectly," undercutting the slant.

"I took a pick-six to the crib," Pitchford said. "That gave us momentum. That just kept us going, pushing the pedal."

No. 3 seed Palmetto defeated No. 6 Royal Palm Beach 23-7 on Friday, Nov. 13, in a Region 5A-3 quarterfinal at Harllee Stadium, winning its 10th consecutive game. Late in the third quarter, Palmetto led 10-7 before Pitchford (a three-star UNC commit) changed the game, giving the Tigers a 17-7 advantage before a late touchdown added six more.

"He's made a lot of plays in his three years," Sanders said, later adding. "He's a playmaker. There's a reason why he's going to North Carolina. Huge play by him."

Palmetto's 23 points are the most Royal Palm Beach (9-2) has allowed all season, a defense that allowed 7.3 points per game in the regular season. Quarterback Matthew Hennesey finished 11-of-21 with 155 passing yards and one touchdown. Running back Shane Partridge also finished with one touchdown.

On defense, junior safety Ryan Cotton and senior defensive tackle Chase Henson recovered fumbles. Senior linebacker Kymani Sellers had a sack and a quarterback pressure.

"We knew we had to play a complete game," Sanders said. "We had to win each segment, offensive, defensive, special teams, even if it's by a small margin. I felt like we did that. It wasn't by a lot, though. It was just little wins."

Palmetto (10-1) took an early 10-point lead. After a bad snap, Hennesey grabbed the ball, throwing it to tight end Ace Luther for a 4-yard score. Leading 7-0, five-star kicker Brandin Gallaway made a 47-yard field goal during Palmetto's next drive.

But after Demetrice Rolle — a senior running back who led the Lions' offense — pushed Royal Palm Beach to the 37, sophomore cornerback Kamari Thomas took it the rest of the way early in the second quarter. The Lions inched within three, a score that held until late in the third.

The Tigers went three-and-out. Then Gallaway's 44-yard field goal attempt was blocked. With 1:19 left in the first half, Palmetto couldn't get past the 48-yard line as the Tiger's defense held strong.

"The D-line is probably the strength of our team," Sanders said. "It's so deep, and so talented. So, we just hang our hats on them, and continue to execute in other areas. And hopefully, we can find a way to make one more play than the other team."

With 2:03 left in the third quarter, Pitchford broke the game open, revitalizing Palmetto. With a 17-7 lead, the Tigers marched downfield with 6:52 left in the fourth quarter before Partridge sealed Palmetto's win with a 2-yard score.

Palmetto will travel to play No. 2 Tampa Bay Tech at 7:30 p.m. on Friday, Nov. 20.

"We gotta come ready, come out there to play," Pitchford said. "If not, we're going to lose. But, you know, we ain't doing no losing."

#2731. Why the Suns should avoid trading Jalen Green for Dejounte Murray

Would I or wouldn't I? That is the question. Ever since reports surfaced earlier today linking the Phoenix Suns to disgruntled New Orleans Pelicans guard Dejounte Murray, I've had plenty of friends reaching out to ask whether I'd pull the trigger on a potential deal involving Jalen Green. And I understand why they're asking. If there's anybody who's been more vocal about the belief that Jalen Green doesn't belong in the Suns' starting five, I'd certainly be a contender for that title.

So a straight-up swap of Green for Murray makes sense, right? Not so fast.

I understand the appeal. Bringing in Murray would theoretically provide Phoenix with a more traditional point guard, allowing the team to reestablish some semblance of positional clarity and better define the responsibilities of the players surrounding Devin Booker. And who knows? Maybe it would work. A straight-up deal would also save the Suns some money, which is certainly beneficial from a salary cap perspective, although it doesn't exactly put any additional cash in my pocket.

Murray is somebody I've been thinking about since last summer. We've discussed potential scenarios involving him for quite some time, and no matter how many times I revisit the possibility, I keep arriving at the same conclusion: I'll pass on that, thank you.

My hesitation begins with Murray's health. He suffered an Achilles injury in February 2025, and there's still uncertainty surrounding whether he can return to the player he was before that injury. That's a significant concern when you're discussing acquiring a guard whose effectiveness has historically relied on his mobility, quickness, and ability to operate on both ends of the floor.

And even before the injury, was Murray really a game-altering player? He was a serviceable guard with a defensive reputation, someone capable of contributing in multiple areas without necessarily elevating the overall ceiling of the teams he played for. The last time he was impactful was when he was in a Greg Popovich system that elevates guard play, and even then the team wasn't winning much with him.

Maybe a player with his skill set would fit more naturally alongside Booker than Green currently does. Considering Green's $36.3 million salary and declining perceived value, there's certainly an argument that swapping one distressed asset for another could make sense from a basketball operations standpoint.

But then there's everything else. Murray's recent public frustration with the situation in New Orleans, including allegations that he has used burner accounts to comment on videos, gives me a familiar and uncomfortable feeling. I call it PTKD. Post-Traumatic KD Disorder.

Say what you want about Jalen Green; we don't see him coming unhinged online when people challenge his fit.

One of the things I've genuinely appreciated about this current iteration of the Phoenix Suns is how relatively quiet they've been away from the basketball court. Outside of a few entertaining trolling commercials from Dillon Brooks, this group has largely avoided the public drama that surrounded previous versions of the franchise. They're not regularly lashing out at fans. They're not creating unnecessary distractions through social media. They're not turning every criticism into a public confrontation. They appear to handle their business internally, and I have tremendous respect for that.

Perhaps that appreciation comes from personal experience. I've been on the receiving end of Kevin Durant's verbal lashings because I dared to express an opinion about the basketball team I cover.  My apologies. I have opinions, I have a platform to express them, and that's precisely what I intend to do. As Don Draper might remind these players, that's what the money is for.

Now, perhaps a change of scenery would do wonders for Murray. Maybe getting out of New Orleans, joining a different organization, and playing alongside Booker would provide the reset he needs, both professionally and personally. That's entirely possible.

When evaluating a potential acquisition, I keep returning to the same fundamental question I've been asking about Jalen Green: Does he make your basketball team better? And with Murray, I'm not convinced the answer is yes.

When you examine his time in Atlanta and New Orleans, his on/off impact has generally been underwhelming. He's spent much of that period posting negative net rating results, raising questions about how much his individual production actually translates into improved team performance.

That's what makes this entire conversation so fascinating. In theory, the Suns would be acquiring a player with a negative statistical impact to replace another player with a similarly negative impact. You're essentially exchanging one set of problems for another, hoping that the new problems are somehow easier to manage. I'll take the guy with a better attitude every day out of the week.

There are potential advantages, of course. Murray would be cheaper, providing some financial flexibility. His presence could help clarify the responsibilities within the starting lineup, allowing Booker to operate more naturally in his preferred role while Murray handles additional facilitating duties. Even that doesn't solve every issue with the roster. The overlapping skill sets of Miles Bridges and Dillon Brooks would still exist. The Suns would still have problems with size, rebounding, and interior production. There would be no guarantee that Murray's arrival would suddenly create the offensive connectivity Phoenix has been searching for.

So where do I ultimately land? I think the Suns should wait.

If Murray is disgruntled today, there's a pretty good chance he'll still be disgruntled two months from now. His desire for a change of scenery doesn't necessarily create an obligation for Phoenix to rush into a transaction, especially when the player they'd be acquiring comes with legitimate questions of his own. Meanwhile, the Suns still have an opportunity to evaluate Jalen Green and determine whether there's a way to make his skill set work within the current roster.

Quite honestly, I believe the best immediate solution is moving Green to the bench. Allow him to operate with the second unit, where he can play a style more familiar to him without constantly navigating the responsibilities and tendencies of Devin Booker. Give him an opportunity to rebuild his confidence, establish some offensive rhythm, and continue developing the distribution skills the organization clearly wants him to improve.

The Suns' bench has already demonstrated a greater understanding of its collective identity. The players know their responsibilities, understand how to complement one another, and have shown an ability to sustain productive stretches without relying on one individual to orchestrate everything. Perhaps placing Green in that environment would help him develop comfort and confidence, ultimately making him a more effective contributor when his minutes overlap with the starters'.

If it doesn't work? Well, at least Phoenix will have gathered additional information before making another significant roster decision. No need to make a trade just to make a trade. Isn't that what got us into this mess?

The reality is that there's no obvious correct answer here. Green hasn't demonstrated that he's the right fit. Murray hasn't demonstrated that he'd necessarily be a better one. Both players carry significant salaries, legitimate concerns, and enough uncertainty to make any potential transaction a gamble. And if the Pelicans and Suns ultimately decide to swap them, what exactly are we accomplishing?

"Here's your problem. We'll take it off your hands if you take ours." "Thank you very much. Have a nice day." I like our problem better. Green is younger and more athletic, seems to be a better locker room guy, is less injury-prone, and seems to be simply having a hard time navigating his new role.

The Suns have a roster that needs work, and Jalen Green hasn't made the transition Phoenix hoped he would. But that doesn't mean Dejounte Murray is the answer. There's a difference between identifying a problem and finding a solution. Swapping two players with question marks attached to their names might make the transaction look productive, but it doesn't necessarily make the basketball team any better. How about we focus on getting some rebounding, eh? Suns are 9th in assists. They're dead last in rebounding. Let's focus on fixing what's broken.

Give Green a chance to find his footing in a different role. Give Murray and the Pelicans some time to sort through their situation. The Suns don't have to be the organization that solves everybody else's problems, especially when they have enough of their own.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2730. On Kirk Cousins (and Packers Week, Too!)

Kirk Cousins is once again the quarterback for the Minnesota Vikings. Welcome to the most exciting, if anxiety-riddled, stretch of Vikings football in memory. The next few months will be unlike any other.

General manager Nolan Teasley did what he had to do. A 3rd-round pick was a small price to pay to avoid the iceberg. After an embarrassing, mind-boggling, inept offensive showing in front of the entire nation on Monday Night Football that threatened a full-on locker room mutiny and the season's implosion, the ship has now been steered into calmer waters. Whether it will be there when the calendar turns to January is still very much up in the air.

The only situations even remotely comparable were Sam Bradford's arrival days before the 2016 season and Randy Moss' return in 2010. While the former started well, defeating the Green Bay Packers in Week 1, it ultimately petered out. The latter didn't even get that far. Let's hope for better luck this time around.

Super Bowl Dreams Are Back On. Through nine games as the Raiders' starter, with offensive weapons no one would confuse with the 1999 Rams, Kirk Cousins was on pace for more TD passes (38) than he had with the Vikings (2021 and 2022) after the NFL moved to a 17-game regular season. He would just fall short of the passing yardage he had in 2022 (4,547).

In an earlier article, I noted that a torn Achilles in a quarterback's plant leg is the worst non-throwing-arm injury you can sustain. Recovery is challenging and often takes more than one season before you start to look like yourself again. I think that may explain much of what's going on with Daniel Jones in Indy right now.

Given his performance this year, there is a persuasive argument that the nature of the injury, not necessarily age, may explain the inconsistency Cousins experienced during his stint with the Atlanta Falcons. Would he have played better if he were 25? Probably. But his play for the Raiders has been a pleasant surprise that no one envisioned before the season: 2nd in passing TDs, 5th in yards, 9th in EPA per dropback, etc.

All Cousins needs to do is be good. That's it. The Raiders are 8th in points per game (26.4). We won't even need that 95% of the time. We'd be 8-0 right now. The reason the Silver and Black are 4-5 is that their defense is last in points allowed (29.8) and yards allowed (385.4). They're basically the 2020-21 Vikings. Under Brian Flores, we're 1st and 2nd in those categories, respectively.

The NFC is wide open right now. No one is convincing me that there is a team we can't beat, with Cousins back throwing to Justin Jefferson, Jordan Addison, and T.J. Hockenson again, and Flores' defense as the ultimate insurance policy.

Cousins, Storybook Ending? Hollywood's most gifted screenwriters couldn't come up with a better scenario. Cousins returns to the Vikings to save the season, and his first game is at Lambeau Field – the very place his Vikings tenure ended the first time around. All under the larger theme of redemption, given how those highly debated years ended after the 2023 season.

Cousins realizes the feel-good, storybook ending is within reach. Reports say the Raiders left the trade decision in his hands. He knows. This is his last, best chance. A Kirk Cousins with a Super Bowl ring would not only have a legitimate Hall of Fame resume (the numbers will be there), but he'd also have a statue outside U.S. Stadium to immortalize his role in ending the long-suffering anguish of a tortured fan base.

Patience is a virtue. I'd urge people not to expect Cousins to come in seamlessly and pick up where he left off in 2023. It may take a game or two to get back into a rhythm with the offense and receivers, regardless of previous experience. That certainly doesn't mean we won't win on Sunday. We've already beaten the Packers and have won games this season with an offense that made us long for the days of the Wishbone or Single-Wing. This is about ensuring the offense is hitting its stride heading into December and (hopefully) a memorable playoff run.

KOC Gets a Mulligan (For Now). Cousins' return effectively gives KOC a new lease on life after repeatedly stepping on rakes with disastrous quarterback decisions that necessitated the trade. He may have gotten away with the "don't look at me!" routine in drafting J.J. McCarthy and letting Sam Darnold walk, but the 2026 quarterback room mess is on him. You can only play that hand so many times. Kwesi Adofo-Mensah took the blame for the earlier missteps and lost his job. Nolan Teasley can justifiably plead ignorance for being late to the game, and no one believes he traded McCarthy without KOC's approval (or encouragement).

Regardless of how this season ends, the Vikings will again begin the offseason back at the drawing board at the most important position on the field. The best-case scenario is that Cousins thrives and reworks his deal to replay the role he played with the Raiders this season if the Vikings try yet again to hit on the QBOTF in the draft. Or maybe they can swing a risky trade for Justin Herbert and effectively blow up their cap as several key in-house free agents loom. Does a Daniel Jones trade happen? Do people think Geno Smith or Mac Jones could be the answer?

The bill for the Murray-McCarthy mess will eventually come due. Let's hope KOC still has the currency to pay it without breaking a sweat. I've said before that the best-case scenario and the Vikings often go together like hot fudge and sushi. But we're due for a break after botching the quarterback situation as badly as possible, only to go back to where we started. Right? Right?

Kirk Cousins isn't coming back in another three years to save us again. Well, we are the Vikings, so you never know. That wouldn't even crack the Top 20 for the weirdest things to happen to this franchise.

With Vikings fans and the Minnesota district manager at Kohl's basking in the excitement of the Cousins trade and renewed hopes for a Super Bowl run, there is a game to play against the stupid Green Bay Packers.

As much as it pains me to say it, the Packers come into this one on a roll. They thoroughly destroyed the New England Patriots on the road last week and have won five of their last six.

All eyes will be on Kirk Cousins and the Vikings offense. The good news is that as long as Cousins doesn't miss the team bus, it'll immediately be an improvement over what we've seen recently.

Like last season without Micah Parsons, the Packers' defense is good, if not quite elite. He's close to returning but won't play in this one. They're 11th in both points and total yards allowed, but better (Top 5) in yards per play and defensive DVOA. Pass defense is their strength, as they're in the Top 10 in both pass EPA allowed and passer rating allowed. The Packers have also feasted on interceptions and are 2nd in the NFL. Their run defense looks to be middle of the pack across most traditional and advanced metrics. Hopefully this can be exploited early to get Cousins comfortable and open up the play-action pass game he excelled at under KOC the first time around.

I'm genuinely curious how much of the playbook will be open to Cousins with such a short prep, regardless of familiarity. KOC may have commented on this, but if he did, I missed it. As I noted above, however, it may be too much to expect him to just drop in and play as if he never left. Give it some time. What matters is how this offense looks heading into December.

Quarterback Jordan Love has the offense leading the NFL in passing yards per game. This has translated into strong showings for the dangerous receiving trio of Christian Watson, Matthew Golden, and Tucker Kraft. Those three had a field day against us in Week 1, but that was mostly in the first half, before we woke up and realized the season had started. This version will not be rusty and will surely have resolved the communication and coverage issues that plagued the unit two months ago.

The problem for the Cheeseheads is that their running game is abysmal, ranking 32nd, and they don't have an injured Josh Jacobs to lean on. I'd think this works in the Vikings' favor, as it will force the Packers' offense to play the game on our terms. If the pass vs. run dynamic were flipped, or even more balanced, that would concern me a bit more. That said, Love and the Packers' aerial attack are incredibly dangerous, stellar Vikings pass defense or not. We'd better be ready.

There are a lot of things that worry me about this game for the Vikings: Cousins' comfort level, not helped by an offensive line playing particularly well, likely missing Blake Cashman, who is having an All-Pro season, and the Packers' passing offense clicking on all cylinders.

1. Comfortable or not, Cousins can hit an open receiver.

I'm not letting this high wear off. No way, no how. The running game shows some life now that opposing defenses have to worry about the passing game. This, in turn, opens up some much-needed (and desperately missed) Cousins-to-Jefferson/Addison/Hockenson (if he plays) highlights. Just enough to get the job done on Sunday, with improvements to come.

#2727. Argentina vs Uruguay: The tactics board - Diego Forlan's low block blunts La Albiceleste

Uruguay beat Argentina 2-1 at La Bombonera by sitting in a compact block that frustrated the hosts in their first match since Lionel Messi's retirement. Two second-half set-pieces undid Lionel Scaloni's side.

Played in front of a sparse crowd at La Bombonera, Friday's Clasico del Rio de la Plata derby was Argentina's first outing since Messi retired after last month's friendly win against Benin, and their first since Scaloni signed his contract extension. Without their longtime talisman, the 2026 World Cup runners-up had plenty of the ball but no clear way through Forlan's well-drilled Uruguay side.

Scaloni set his team up in a 4-3-3. Exequiel Palacios sat at the base of the midfield behind Enzo Fernandez and Alexis Mac Allister, with Nico Paz on the right wing, Thiago Almada on the left, and Julian Alvarez leading the line. Because Uruguay packed the middle of the pitch, full-backs Gonzalo Montiel and Facundo Medina pushed high to offer width.

Argentina dominated the ball throughout the first half but struggled to create much of note, often settling for tame efforts from distance, including a long-range strike from Medina that Cristopher Fiermarin held comfortably. Their best opening came when Mac Allister slipped a pass into Alvarez, whose shot was saved by Fiermarin before a defender blocked Almada's follow-up for a corner. Fiermarin also made a smart stop to deny Paz before the break.

Ditching the frantic man-to-man press of the previous regime, Forlan parked Uruguay in two tight banks of four inside their own half. Rodrigo Bentancur and Lucas Torreira protected the back four, Giorgian de Arrascaeta linked the midfield to lone striker Darwin Nunez, and the visitors waited for mistakes. After defending for the opening 25 minutes, Uruguay began getting their foot on the ball through Bentancur to take the sting out of Argentina's pressure.

The game turned at half-time when Scaloni took off Palacios for winger Matias Soule to add another attacker. Instead, taking off his holding midfielder left a hole in the middle of the pitch that De Arrascaeta gladly exploited, and two quick set-pieces punished the hosts.

In the 48th minute, Nunez flicked on a De Arrascaeta free-kick from the left; Lisandro Martinez tried to head it clear, only for the ball to cannon off the back of Leonardo Balerdi's head and into his own net. Four minutes later, Nunez got the better of Balerdi and forced a fine save from Geronimo Rulli. From De Arrascaeta's resulting 53rd-minute corner, Maximiliano Araujo darted to the near post and diverted the ball off his shoulder over Rulli for 2-0.

Forlan brought on Federico Valverde for Agustin Canobbio in the 58th minute to shore up the midfield, while Scaloni introduced Gianluca Prestianni for Almada at the hour mark. Alongside Soule - who put Paz clean through for a one-on-one saved by Fiermarin - Prestianni gave Argentina genuine width.

That change paid off in the 73rd minute when Fernandez switched play out to Prestianni, who drove down his flank and crossed for Alvarez to control and slot inside the post. Scaloni threw on full-backs Agustin Giay and Roman Vega for the final ten minutes, while Forlan answered with Facundo Pellistri and Rodrigo Aguirre on the break.

Despite a blocked Fernandez shot in the 88th minute and a stoppage-time opening for Alvarez, Uruguay saw out the game to hand Forlan his third win in charge.

#2722. Cowboys aggressiveness on offense must be turned up vs. 49ers

After two straight weeks of early window kickoffs in which the Dallas Cowboys had to fight, claw, scratch, and get knocked down to go 1-1 against the Arizona Cardinals and Indianapolis Colts, they play in the Sunday late afternoon window against the San Francisco 49ers.

Both teams are off a win, which puts the Cowboys right back in a position they've been time and time again and failed in nearly every time, trying to win consecutive games under Brian Schottenheimer. The Cowboys can't afford to spin their wheels much longer if they want to make this season mean something though.

This 49ers matchup is exactly that opportunity for the Cowboys, so let's get to how they can pull off the upset.

Think for a second about how many NFL offenses are truly earning the label of "elite", "scary", or "explosive" right now, then without looking at any other metrics or analytics consider the top four tiers in the first downs per play category. They are the Bills, 49ers, Lions, and Bengals tied for first, the Cowboys in second, and the Jaguars and Rams in third just ahead of the Patriots and Chiefs.

Also among these teams, the 49ers, Lions, and Bills are in the top five for fewest third downs faced per game. The Patriots, Chiefs, Jags, and Bengals are all virtually tied with the Bills to round out the top nine. Then there are the 14th ranked Cowboys, much closer to teams like the Steelers, Texans, and Browns – none of which have the QB situation Dallas does that should be keeping them out of this not-so-great company. This is a main culprit why they feel like outliers on the elite, scary, or explosive list while the other offenses are much more deserving.

For even more evidence that the Cowboys offense is all about Dak right now, and that Prescott should be getting way more help than he is from all directions, there's the fact two of the three touchdown drives last week at the Colts came in two minute situations before halftime and at the end of the game. The Cowboys need much more than just Prescott to be at their absolute best against the 49ers, but fairly or not will also need #4 to be among the players that elevates their game this week as well.

The best path towards doing so is much more early down aggressiveness and the Cowboys embracing what they do best by throwing the ball to CeeDee Lamb, and hopefully George Pickens, not just on third down, but all the time.

Without Jake Ferguson for this game, one of the things the Cowboys are going to have an incredibly difficult time doing is persuading the 49ers defense out of loading the box. They don't have the middle of the field threat to get behind these stacked looks, especially considering the freakish coverage ability to all parts of the field from Fred Warner. Dallas will still need to find it's times to run the ball and doing so well on early downs would also be huge, but staying in spread looks and letting Prescott make the right decision should be their bread and butter.

This could also allow them to use some tempo to their advantage, getting to the line quickly to give them time to use motions, something the 49ers offense is going to do a ton of to the Cowboys defense, and Cowboys offense would be wise to try to give it back.

On defense, the Cowboys need to do everything they can to limit the vast amount of plays 49ers coach Kyle Shanahan can get to at all times. If Christian McCaffrey is breaking tackles and getting considerable gains on first downs all game, the 49ers are simply too talented and well-coached to be stopped. They will toy with Christian Parker's defense when ahead of the chains with short, intermediate, and deep layered passes.

There's also the element of Brock Purdy's effectiveness scrambling outside of the pocket, another area Quinnen Williams and Kenny Clark will have to step up in pushing the pocket and keeping contain. A complete game in both run and pass defense from the Cowboys front will give them a chance to replicate what the Atlanta Falcons did to the 49ers as the only team to beat them this season.

Even though the disparity in records is obvious in this game between the 7-1 49ers and 4-5 Cowboys, if the game was going to solely be decided by the players on the field, I think many fans on both sides would be expecting a much closer game than they currently are. The biggest gripe from Cowboys fans when it comes to things they're not looking forward to the most about this toughest game of the season to date is how likely it feels the 49ers coaching staff will run laps around the Cowboys staff, and separate even more on the scoreboard as a result of it.

This chart shows that Schottenheimer is lacking creativity in his offensive formations/plays, while Shanahan is uplifting the 49ers with his usual masterclass at taking a roster hammered by injuries and still finding a way to win.

The Cowboys have already lost to four of the coaches that presently rank above Schottenheimer on this chart, play the 49ers this Sunday, then still have to play the Jaguars and Seahawks in December. The only win for Schotty against a coach above him on this list is the one directly above him by the smallest of margins, Nick Caley with the Texans. The Cowboys are finding themselves outcoached all too often right now.

The Cowboys have the talent to compete with this 49ers team, and they have everything left to play for in the NFC East. If they come out and continue to have communication issues on both sides of the ball, call plays like they're playing to not lose instead of playing to win, and shoot themselves in the foot with egregious penalties, it will be all most fans need to see that this season is doomed to look like year one under Schottenheimer and fall short of the playoffs again.

A Cowboys head coach that's had the chance to work with a healthy Dak Prescott has never had as little success as Schottenheimer is now, and Prescott has not beaten the 49ers since being with the coach that drafted him Jason Garrett in 2017. The chance for this QB-HC duo to have a real signature moment is right there for the taking in week ten if the Cowboys play their best game of the season, a challenge the Dallas coaching staff needs to rise to instead of wilting under the pressure of.

#2721. Lakers' Austin Reaves day-to-day with bruised heel, doesn't undergo imaging

Any Los Angeles Lakers game in which Luka Dončić and Austin Reaves both exit with injuries is going to be concerning, but it appears the team has dodged any serious fallout.

Two days after the Lakers' two leading scorers left a win over the Milwaukee Bucks, head coach JJ Redick told reporters Friday that he expects Dončić to be available for Sunday's game against the Phoenix Suns. Dončić had already told reporters he was "good" the night of the injury, which was a tweaked ankle.

As for Reaves, Redick said the guard was day-to-day with a bruise on his heel. The matter is apparently minor enough that the team didn't even bother with imaging on the foot.

"AR, based on his physical evaluation and how he was feeling yesterday, we opted against imaging. He'll be day-to-day. We'll have him on the report tomorrow at 5 o'clock."

Even with their early exits, no pair of teammates is averaging more combined points per game than Dončić and Reaves. Dončić leads the league in scoring with 38.6 points per game, while Reaves is 15th with 24.7. Dončić is also ninth in the league in assists per game at 7.6.

Rotation guard Collin Sexton also exited Wednesday's game after a collision with Bucks guard Ryan Rollins. He needed stitches above his right eye, but Redick said a concussion was ruled out and he's "probably in the same boat" as Reaves.

The Lakers-Suns game is scheduled for 4 p.m. ET on Sunday in Phoenix.

#2720. Quentin Grimes drops ‘pick your poison’ approach to playing with Luka Doncic

Quentin Grimes drops 'pick your poison' approach to playing with Luka Doncic appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

LOS ANGELES – One of the Los Angeles Lakers' big offseason acquisitions was guard Quentin Grimes. The six-year veteran was brought in as an additional ball-handler who can provide Luka Doncic and Austin Reaves with some relief in that aspect. And Grimes is no stranger to playing with Doncic. The pair were teammates briefly during the 2024-25 season with the Dallas Mavericks before Doncic was traded to the Lakers and Grimes to the Philadelphia 76ers.

Now reunited with the Lakers, Quentin Grimes is still adjusting to the team's system and his new teammates. One of the ways he's found how he can help the team is by following Luka Doncic's lead and allowing that to help dictate when and where he needs to be aggressive on the court.

"It's just a rhythm thing. Luka is on a crazy heater, doing stuff we ain't seen ever in the NBA," Grimes said following Lakers' practice on Friday. "So you just got to pick your poison and find your spots out there and just try to be a relief for him for sure."

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

Grimes has been sidelined for the last two games due to a finger injury, but he acknowledged after practice that he expected to be back in the Lakers' lineup for the final game of their four-game trip on Sunday against the Phoenix Suns.

The 26-year-old has been coming off the bench this season, and is coming off a strong game in which he finished with 12 points and four assists in the team's win against the Detroit Pistons to start the road trip. But Grimes has not only been tasked with being a scoring threat and ball-handler from the Lakers, but to be an impact defender as well.

While the team has gotten off to a strong start to the season, head coach JJ Redick wants the Lakers to become a top-ten defensive team. As it stands, they are 18th in opponents' points per game (114.2) and 17th in defensive rating (114.7). That side of the floor is an area where Grimes can have a solid impact.

"[It's] just reps. There's a lot of new guys, a lot of new terminology, a lot of guys who haven't played together before," Grimes said. "It's just getting the reps out, just knowing that getting the rotation down and getting those scrambles out the way and understanding the more reps you rep it out, the easier it'll get when we play a really good team and kind of propel you to be a top-10 defensive team."

Through 11 games played, Grimes is averaging 8.0 points, 2.5 rebounds and 2.5 assists with splits of 43.1 percent shooting from the field, 24.2 percent shooting from the 3-point line and 70.6 percent shooting from the free-throw line.

To help get him back up to speed after two missed games, Grimes participated in the 'stay ready' games on Friday alongside the Lakers' lower minutes players. It was his first real on-court action since the finger injury, and he wanted to see how it respond to contact.

The injury was on his shooting hand, but he did well during the stay ready games with his team picking up plenty of dubs.

"It felt really good. I had a really good day playing with a little tape over it to see how it felt for the first time," Grimes said. "But it felt really good. We got a lot of wins in those games with me out there, so it felt really good."

#2719. Nationwide ground stop potentially complicates NFL, college games

The sudden nationwide ground stop ordered by the FAA will potentially impact this weekend’s football games.

It’s only the second nationwide ground stop since 9/11. It’s apparently due to a widespread outage impacting air-traffic control functions.

Visiting NFL teams typically travel to the sites of their games on Saturday; if the situation lingers, some teams may not be able to get to where they need to be. More immediately, college teams that travel on Friday have a mess.

As noted by Matt Maiocco of NBC Sports Bay Area, the 49ers usually travel on Friday for games that are at least two time zones away. For Sunday’s game against the Cowboys, they plan to travel on Saturday.

Some teams can get to the locations of their games by car or train. The distance from Minneapolis to Green Bay is 279 miles. For Buffalo to New Jersey (for Bills-Jets), it’s also less than 300 miles.

It’s roughly 290 miles fom Pittsburgh to Cincinnati, and 373 miles from L.A. to Phoenix.

For the other games (particularly Chargers-Ravens on Monday night), it’s a long way to go via ground transportation.
